- source: Fehr, E., & Rockenbach, B. (2004). Human altruism: economic, neural, and evolutionary perspectives. *Current Opinion in Neurobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews evidence that the availability of costly punishment in public goods games leads to much higher and more stable cooperation levels compared to games without punishment. Voluntary selection into punishment regimes further increases cooperation compared to exogenous assignment. The review also highlights that punishment is only effective at promoting cooperation when it is perceived as fair or socially justified. Neuroeconomic studies show that both cooperation and the act of punishing defectors activate reward-related brain circuits, suggesting a neural basis for strong reciprocity. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative results on efficiency or group payoff, instead focusing on behavioral outcomes and neural mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation and contributions, especially when punishment is voluntary and perceived as justified. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this review supports the expectation that punishment will increase efficiency when it increases cooperation, but the magnitude and conditions for this effect must be inferred from other sources or from the primary studies it cites.

- source: Singer, T., & Steinbeis, N. (2009). Differential Roles of Fairness- and Compassion-Based Motivations for Cooperation, Defection, and Punishment. *Values, Empathy, and Fairness Across Social Barriers*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that fairness-based motivation leads to punishment of norm violations, which can sustain cooperation in public goods games, as shown in referenced empirical studies. Compassion-based motivation, in contrast, can prevent the breakdown of cooperation in noisy environments by promoting forgiveness, but may not be effective in the long term against persistent defectors. The emotional and neural mechanisms underlying these motivations are distinct. The paper does not provide new empirical results or direct measures of efficiency or group payoff, but summarizes that punishment options in public goods games are associated with higher cooperation rates and norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and literature-based support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games increases cooperation rates and helps sustain cooperation, referencing empirical studies that show this effect. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that punishment increases cooperation (and thus likely efficiency), but does not allow for fine-grained prediction based on game design dimensions. It is most useful for understanding the motivational and emotional mechanisms behind punishment and cooperation, and for contextualizing why punishment may be effective in sustaining cooperation.

- source: Rosas, A. (2010). Evolutionary game theory meets social science: Is there a unifying rule for human cooperation?. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that cooperative strategies in public goods games and related social dilemmas are governed by a common conditional rule: cooperate with those of good reputation and enforce (by withholding benefits or costly punishment) against those of bad reputation. The choice between withholding benefits and costly punishment depends on the structure of the game—costly punishment is more relevant in public goods games where defectors cannot be targeted directly. The paper reviews experimental evidence showing that combining costly punishment with indirect reciprocity can increase cooperation at lower cost than punishment alone, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes. Theoretical and experimental evidence suggests that the same reputation rules (e.g., SCORING, SIMPLE STANDING, STERN JUDGING) operate across frameworks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation (and potentially efficiency) in public goods games depends on whether withholding benefits is a viable enforcement mechanism. It suggests that costly punishment is most effective and necessary in public goods games where direct targeting of defectors is not possible. However, the paper does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. It is most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to be effective as an enforcement mechanism, and for mapping the conceptual relationships between different enforcement strategies.

- source: Price, M. E. (2008). The resurrection of group selection as a theory of human cooperation. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The review highlights that experimental public good games and related economic games (including with punishment) show that people often contribute more than predicted by self-interest and sometimes punish non-cooperators at a cost to themselves, even when reciprocity and reputation are ruled out. This pattern is observed across diverse societies. However, the review argues that these behavioral outcomes do not necessarily indicate group selection or strong reciprocity as an evolved adaptation, and that the experimental settings may not reflect ancestral environments. The review does not report or analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the theoretical interpretation of observed contribution and punishment behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good games. It reviews behavioral outcomes (contribution, punishment) from experimental games, but does not report or synthesize efficiency, group payoff, or welfare data. The main value for prediction is contextual: it affirms that punishment is present in many public good game experiments and that it affects contribution behavior, but it does not quantify or analyze efficiency effects. The review also cautions that experimental game results may not generalize to real-world or evolutionary contexts.

- source: Varga, Z., Scarelli, A., Cressman, R., & Garay, J. (2010). Evolutionary game model for a marketing cooperative with penalty for unfaithfulness. *Nonlinear Analysis-real World Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews how different sources of power—such as punishment, reward, asymmetric dependence, and information—are operationalized in economic games, including public goods games. It highlights that punishment power is often used to enforce cooperation, but can also provoke retaliation and cycles of revenge, potentially undermining group outcomes. Delegation of punishment power and the use of centralized or third-party punishment mechanisms can help sustain cooperation. The review also notes that reward can be as effective as punishment in some contexts, but is less well understood. The paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings about efficiency or group payoff, but it synthesizes behavioral mechanisms that are relevant to the prediction of such outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic context for how punishment and reward power affect cooperation in public goods games, but does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is useful for understanding the potential pathways through which punishment may increase or decrease efficiency, such as by deterring free-riding or provoking retaliation. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be moderated by factors such as the structure of punishment (individual vs. centralized), the possibility of retaliation, and the availability of reward. However, it does not provide data that can be directly used for quantitative prediction.

- source: Jensen, K. (2010). Punishment and spite, the dark side of cooperation. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper argues that punishment, including altruistic and third-party forms, is a key mechanism for maintaining cooperation in humans, especially in public goods game contexts. It reviews evidence from the literature that allowing punishment in public goods games increases cooperation, but it does not provide new quantitative results on efficiency or group payoff. The paper also discusses the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of punishment and spite, suggesting that negative other-regarding preferences (spite) may be as important as positive ones (empathy) in shaping human cooperation. The review highlights that while punishment can deter free-riding and enforce norms, it can also be motivated by antisocial preferences, and the net effect on group welfare or efficiency is not always clear from the literature.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and conceptual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation in public goods games, referencing experimental findings from the literature. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the inclusion of punishment as a potentially positive moderator of cooperation, but does not allow for precise quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. It is useful for understanding mechanisms and for contextualizing empirical findings, but not for direct parameterization of efficiency effects.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2012). Evolutionary advantages of adaptive rewarding. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Adaptive rewarding in spatial public goods games can eliminate defectors and promote high levels of cooperation, outperforming steady (non-adaptive) rewarding. The effectiveness of adaptive rewarding depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost of rewarding (alpha), and the strength of the reward (Delta). When rewards are strong and not too costly, adaptive rewarding can lead to defector-free states, interpreted as maximal efficiency. However, adaptive punishment is even more effective: for the same cost, punishment achieves defector elimination at lower required intensity. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing under what parameter regimes each strategy dominates, coexists, or is eliminated. The main efficiency outcome is the prevalence of defector-free states, which is used as a proxy for group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, adaptive punishment is more effective than adaptive rewarding at achieving maximal efficiency (defector-free states), given the same cost-to-impact ratio. The results are highly informative for predicting the effect of introducing punishment (or reward) in spatial PGGs: punishment is more likely to yield higher efficiency than reward, especially when the cost of sanctioning is not prohibitive. The findings are most directly applicable to spatial, lattice-based PGGs with local interactions, and may be less directly transferable to well-mixed or non-spatial settings. The paper's phase diagrams and minimum required cost-to-impact ratios for defector elimination can inform quantitative predictions about efficiency under different game design parameters.

- source: Gerber, A., & Wichardt, P. C. (2009). Providing public goods in the absence of strong institutions. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a voluntary deposit-commitment mechanism in public goods games can fully implement efficient contribution profiles as equilibrium outcomes, even in the absence of strong external enforcement or punishment institutions. The mechanism works by making non-contribution costly (loss of deposit) only if all players commit, thus aligning individual incentives with group welfare. Theoretical results show that, under this mechanism, the group can achieve payoffs and efficiency levels that strictly Pareto-dominate the standard Nash equilibrium of zero contribution. The mechanism is robust to various extensions, including repeated games and alternative uses of forfeited deposits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that self-commitment mechanisms (such as refundable deposits) can substitute for traditional punishment in public goods games, leading to higher efficiency and group payoffs. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, in environments where such a deposit-commitment mechanism is available and properly designed, the efficiency with 'punishment' (here, self-sanctioning via deposit) can approach the social optimum, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the deposit mechanism is feasible and all players can observe and verify commitments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or parameterized predictions, but the qualitative direction is clear: enabling credible self-sanctioning or commitment mechanisms can dramatically improve efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Gintis, H., Smith, E. A., & Bowles, S. (2001). Costly signaling and cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player public goods game, honest signaling of quality by providing a public good (or by punishing defectors) can be an evolutionarily stable strategy, provided that the cost of signaling is lower for high-quality individuals and that the benefits of alliances or partnerships are sufficiently high. The signaling equilibrium yields higher average group payoffs than the non-signaling equilibrium. The model also shows that punishment can function as a costly signal, and that under certain conditions, prosocial signals (including punishment) are more likely to evolve than neutral or antisocial signals. The model provides explicit conditions for when signaling equilibria exist and are stable, and analyzes the evolutionary dynamics leading to these equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling costly signaling (including punishment) in public goods games can increase group efficiency and average payoff, even in the absence of repeated interactions or reciprocity. The model is directly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where punishment is interpreted as a costly signal of quality. The findings suggest that, when the cost structure and benefit parameters are appropriate, introducing punishment (as a form of signaling) should increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment. The paper also highlights the importance of the cost differential between high- and low-quality individuals, the size of the group, and the benefits of alliances in determining the stability and efficiency of the signaling equilibrium. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct experimental evidence.

- source: Reuben, E., & Tyran, J. R. (2010). Everyone is a winner: Promoting cooperation through all-can-win intergroup competition. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing tolerance-based expulsion in a spatial public goods game can substantially increase the level of cooperation (measured as the fraction of cooperators and expellers). The effect is strongest at intermediate tolerance thresholds and when expulsion costs are high. The mechanism allows expellers to balance the cost of expulsion with the benefit of removing defectors, leading to higher and more stable cooperation levels. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined from the reported results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that tolerance-based expulsion mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially when the cost of expulsion is high and the tolerance threshold is optimally set. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation levels rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that enabling punishment (in the form of expulsion) is likely to increase cooperation, but the impact on efficiency is not directly quantified and may depend on the costliness of expulsion and the structure of the population. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2010). Group penalty on the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a group penalty mechanism—removing groups whose collective payoff falls below a threshold—substantially increases the stationary fraction of cooperators in spatial public goods games across a variety of network topologies. The effect is robust to initial conditions and is sensitive to the deletion threshold and the number of connections of newcomers. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the findings are limited to cooperation rates rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that group-level punishment (group penalty) can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates may suggest higher efficiency, but this is not demonstrated or quantified. The model is theoretical/simulation-based and does not manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions in a way that allows for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Orr, S. W. (2001). The economics of shame in work groups: How mutual monitoring can decrease cooperation in teams. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds, through theoretical argument and reference to motivation crowding theory, that mutual monitoring and social sanctions (including social disapproval) in public-goods-like group incentive schemes can decrease cooperation among normatively motivated individuals due to crowding out of intrinsic motivation. The effect is non-linear: some monitoring may initially increase cooperation, but excessive or controlling monitoring leads to suspicion and a shift from normative to gain frames, reducing contributions. For individualistic types, monitoring may crowd in normative motivation and increase cooperation. The overall effect of mutual monitoring or social sanctions on group performance is thus frequency-dependent on the motivational composition of the group. The paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that in groups with many normatively motivated members, mutual monitoring may reduce cooperation and thus likely reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical caution that enabling mutual monitoring or social sanctions (punishment) in public-goods-like environments may not always increase efficiency or cooperation, especially in groups with many normatively motivated members. The effect of punishment or monitoring is predicted to be non-linear and frequency-dependent: it may increase cooperation among individualists but decrease it among cooperators if perceived as controlling. For the prediction task, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and may depend on group composition and the perceived nature of monitoring. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency, so its value is mainly in highlighting possible negative or mixed effects of punishment mechanisms, especially social (rather than material) sanctions.

- source: Kitts, J. A. (2006). Collective action, rival incentives, and the emergence of antisocial norms. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that collective action and group efficiency in public-goods-like environments are shaped by a three-way interaction between the value of selective incentives (rewards), the rivalness of those incentives, and the strength of peer influence (cohesiveness). When incentives are nonrival, increasing their value or increasing peer influence both increase participation and group welfare. When incentives are rival, however, strong peer influence can lead to the enforcement of antisocial norms that discourage contribution, reducing group efficiency. The model demonstrates that under high rivalness and high incentive value, increasing peer influence or incentive value can actually decrease collective action and group welfare. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are most applicable to small groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, rival selective incentives and norm enforcement) on group efficiency is highly contingent on the design dimensions of the incentive system and the strength of peer influence. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling strong peer enforcement or increasing the value of selective incentives will not always increase efficiency—if the incentives are rival, these interventions can backfire and reduce group welfare. The model is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games with reward-based incentives, especially when those rewards are rival. While the paper does not model punishment per se, the logic and results are highly informative for any system where sanctions or rewards are rival and peer enforcement is possible.

- source: Forsyth, P. A. I., & Hauert, C. (2011). Public goods games with reward in finite populations. *Journal of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in finite populations, allowing individuals to reward contributors to the public good can initiate and maintain a significant minority of cooperators, even though the majority of the population is typically composed of non-cooperating shirkers. Reward is effective at jump-starting cooperation but is unable to stabilize it in the long run due to exploitation by second-order free-riders. The average group payoff (efficiency) is increased relative to pure defection, but full efficiency is not achieved. The effectiveness of reward is largely independent of group size when key parameters are scaled appropriately. The paper does not provide new results on punishment, but notes that punishment is more effective at stabilizing cooperation once established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence on the effects of reward (not punishment) in public goods games. It shows that positive incentives can increase efficiency above the baseline of mutual defection, but do not achieve full efficiency due to the persistence of second-order free-riders. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, reward alone is insufficient to fully stabilize cooperation and maximize efficiency. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with reward enabled, but only indirectly inform predictions about punishment. The analysis of group size, MPCR, and reward parameters is informative for understanding how these dimensions affect efficiency with reward. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for cases where the treatment is reward (not punishment), and less directly informative for punishment-enabled games.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Unemi, T. (2011). Replicator dynamics in public goods games with reward funds. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a compulsory public goods game with a pool-rewarding mechanism, cooperation and high efficiency can be maintained if the net benefit from the reward fund exceeds the cost of contributing to the public good. Specifically, if c2*(r2-1) > sigma (where sigma is the net cost of contributing to the public good), the population can converge to all rewarders (full efficiency) or exhibit stable cycles with high average payoff. If this condition is not met, defectors dominate and efficiency collapses. The model also shows that introducing second-order sanctions (reducing rewards to second-order free riders) can stabilize full cooperation. The findings are directly relevant to predicting efficiency in PGG-like games with reward mechanisms, but only indirectly inform about punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public goods games with reward funds, the efficiency outcome depends critically on the relative effectiveness and cost of the reward mechanism compared to the cost of cooperation. The explicit condition c2*(r2-1) > sigma can be used to predict when enabling a reward mechanism will increase efficiency relative to the control (no reward) case. However, the paper does not analyze punishment, so its direct value for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency is limited. The model structure and results may be informative by analogy for understanding how the cost and effectiveness of sanctions (punishment or reward) interact with game parameters (group size, MPCR, etc.) to determine efficiency outcomes.

- source: Kurokawa, S., Wakano, J. Y., & Ihara, Y. (2010). Generous cooperators can outperform non-generous cooperators when replacing a population of defectors. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player public goods games, reciprocators who are 'generous' (i.e., tolerate some defection before retaliating) can outperform less generous reciprocators and defectors in terms of average payoff and fixation probability, especially when group size is at least 4. The optimal generosity level depends on the benefit-to-cost ratio and group size. Generosity facilitates the emergence of cooperation (replacement of defectors by reciprocators) but does not help maintain cooperation once established. The model provides explicit conditions for when generosity is advantageous and quantifies the optimal generosity for maximizing the likelihood of cooperation's evolutionary success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated public goods games with n ≥ 4, the design of reciprocation strategies (specifically, the degree of generosity/tolerance for defection) can significantly affect the efficiency outcome when cooperation is replacing defection. While the model does not include explicit punishment mechanisms (such as costly punishment), it analyzes a form of contingent cooperation that functions as a sanctioning mechanism (withholding cooperation from defectors). The findings suggest that, for predicting efficiency in games with repeated interaction and contingent cooperation, allowing some tolerance for defection (generosity) can increase the likelihood of efficient cooperative outcomes, especially in the emergence phase. However, the model does not address explicit costly punishment or its direct efficiency effects, so its relevance to games with explicit punishment is adjacent rather than exact.

- source: Hauert, C. (2010). Replicator dynamics of reward & reputation in public goods games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, the opportunity to reward contributors (with or without reputation) can encourage the emergence of cooperation if rewards are sufficiently appealing and visible (via reputation). However, reward alone cannot stabilize cooperation, as second-order free-riders (who cooperate but do not reward) undermine the system, leading to cycles of cooperation and defection. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and explicit payoff calculations for different strategy types. The paper contrasts these findings with the literature on punishment, noting that punishment can stabilize cooperation but is less effective at establishing it. Efficiency (average population payoff) is analyzed as a function of the strategic composition of the population, but the paper does not provide direct results for punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the mechanisms by which reward and reputation affect efficiency and cooperation in public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but its discussion of the contrasting roles of reward and punishment can inform predictions: reward can increase cooperation and thus efficiency in the short term, but cannot maintain high efficiency due to second-order free-riding. Punishment, as discussed from prior literature, can stabilize high efficiency once cooperation is established. The explicit payoff equations and analysis of group size, reward cost/benefit, and reputation can inform parameterization of models, but direct quantitative predictions for punishment effects are not provided.

- source: Zschache, J. (2012). Producing public goods in networks: Some effects of social comparison and endogenous network change. *Social Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on networks, the ability of agents to change their social ties in response to social comparison (i.e., to seek out like-minded cooperators and avoid defectors) can sustain or increase cooperation and thus the production of public goods. Low network density and low costs of network change (low cohesion) allow cooperators to persist by forming supportive subgroups, leading to higher efficiency. High initial cooperation and low segregation also promote full cooperation. When network change is costly (high cohesion), cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model shows that both forward-looking and backward-looking (adaptive) agents can achieve high efficiency under favorable network conditions, but backward-looking agents require even more favorable starting conditions. Explicit costly punishment is not modeled; instead, social comparison provides a form of endogenous, non-costly social pressure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that endogenous network change and social comparison can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game designs allowing for dynamic partner selection or network rewiring (low cohesion/costs) will see higher efficiency, especially when initial cooperation is high and the network is not highly segregated. However, the absence of explicit costly punishment means the results are most applicable to settings where social comparison or endogenous social pressure is the main incentive, not where formal punishment is enabled. The results are directly informative for predicting efficiency in networked PGGs with endogenous tie change, but less so for classic PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Bergstrom, T. C. (2002). Evolution of social behavior: Individual and group selection. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in linear public goods games (multiplayer prisoners' dilemmas), cooperation cannot be sustained in equilibrium under nonassortative (random) matching and individual selection; only defectors survive, leading to low group efficiency. However, cooperation and higher group payoffs can be sustained if (a) matching is assortative (e.g., kin selection, spatial structure, partner choice), (b) the game is not a prisoners' dilemma (e.g., stag hunt), or (c) the game is repeated and social norms (including punishment) are enforced. The paper discusses the theoretical role of punishment in repeated games as a means to sustain cooperation and efficient outcomes, but does not provide direct analysis or predictions about the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical context for the importance of game structure (e.g., matching, repetition, group size, payoff parameters) in determining whether cooperation and high efficiency can be sustained in public goods games. It highlights that punishment can, in theory, support efficient cooperation in repeated games, but does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about the magnitude or direction of punishment's effect on efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of repeated interaction and enforceable punishment norms are key moderators, but does not provide parameter-level guidance for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in specific game designs.

- source: Suzuki, S., & Akiyama, E. (2007). Evolution of indirect reciprocity in groups of various sizes and comparison with direct reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-person public goods games with indirect reciprocity, both cooperative (discriminating) and defective strategies can be evolutionarily stable, but the threshold for cooperation increases sharply with group size. As group size increases, it becomes much harder for cooperation to evolve and persist, leading to lower expected group efficiency/payoff. The reputation mechanism (standing vs. image scoring) is a strong moderator: standing allows for cooperation to be stable under a wider range of parameters. The presence of implementation errors (failures to cooperate) further undermines the stability of cooperation. When unconditional cooperators are present, cooperation is only stable for group sizes greater than two and under certain parameter ranges. The results are derived analytically from payoff and replicator dynamics equations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing group size in public goods games with indirect reciprocity (and no explicit punishment mechanism) makes it much harder for cooperation and high efficiency to evolve. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in large groups, the baseline efficiency is likely to be low, and the marginal effect of punishment may depend on whether it can overcome the group size barrier. The paper also highlights the importance of the reputation mechanism: if the punishment or sanctioning system is more like 'standing' (justified punishment does not harm reputation), cooperation is more likely to be stable. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, error rates, and reputation mechanisms, but do not directly model explicit costly punishment as a game option.

- source: Hauert, C., De Monte, S., Hofbauer, J., & Sigmund, K. (2002). Volunteering as Red Queen mechanism for cooperation in public goods games. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, allowing voluntary participation (the option to become a 'loner' and receive a fixed outside payoff) leads to persistent coexistence and oscillations among cooperators, defectors, and loners. This mechanism can sustain cooperation even in large groups and without repeated interactions, memory, or identification of defectors. The average population payoff can, depending on parameters, be higher or lower than the loner's payoff. The paper contrasts this with punishment and reward mechanisms, which require identification of defectors. The findings are robust across well-mixed and spatially structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it focuses on the alternative mechanism of voluntary participation. However, it provides important context: it notes that punishment and reward can establish cooperation if defectors can be identified, but that voluntary participation can also sustain cooperation without identification or repeated interaction. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a voluntary participation (loner) option is a strong moderator of efficiency and cooperation, and that the absence of punishment does not necessarily doom cooperation if such an option exists. The paper does not provide parameterized predictions about punishment effects, so its direct value for predicting efficiency with/without punishment is limited.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2011). An Institutional Mechanism for Assortment in an Ecology of Games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that capacity constraints (limits on the number of players per game) are a simple and effective institutional mechanism for promoting positive assortment of cooperators in an ecology of public goods games. When group size is limited, cooperators can cluster together and achieve higher payoffs relative to defectors, even without punishment or reputation. Budget constraints (limits on the number of games per player) are less effective. The model does not include punishment, but discusses it as a more complex and potentially costly alternative. The main outcome is the relative cooperator payoff, not group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model punishment or sanctions. However, it is relevant for understanding how institutional design (e.g., group size limits) can promote cooperation and higher payoffs without the need for punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that capacity constraints can increase the relative payoff of cooperators, but does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is not directly useful for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Gao, J., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2010). Diversity of contribution promotes cooperation in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially contribute more to groups with higher cooperation levels (high-quality groups) significantly promotes the evolution of cooperation in public goods games, both on regular lattices and scale-free networks. The mechanism works by enabling cooperators to cluster and avoid exploitation by defectors, and by increasing the wealth/payoff of cooperators for a given cooperation level. The effect is stronger as the preference parameter (alpha) increases. The paper interprets the reduction of contributions to low-quality groups as a form of indirect, costless group-level punishment, but does not model or analyze explicit costly punishment. Group efficiency or total group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how endogenous contribution patterns (where cooperators can direct more resources to cooperative groups) affect cooperation and the distribution of payoffs in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of explicit, costly punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that indirect, costless group-level punishment (via reduced contributions to low-quality groups) can promote cooperation and increase the payoffs of cooperators, but the implications for overall group efficiency are not quantified. For prediction tasks focused on explicit punishment interventions, this paper provides only adjacent evidence, mainly relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment.

- source: Arenas, A., Camacho, J., Cuesta, J. A., & Requejo, R. J. (2011). The joker effect: Cooperation driven by destructive agents. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing destructive agents ('jokers') into a public goods game leads to robust cycles in which cooperation periodically surges. The mechanism is that jokers harm defectors more than cooperators, creating a dynamic where cooperators can invade jokers, jokers invade defectors, and defectors invade cooperators. The cycles are robust to mutation and population size. The model provides explicit payoff expressions for each strategy, but the main focus is on the evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how destructive threats can promote cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions per se. Instead, it analyzes the effect of introducing a destructive agent type that harms all non-jokers. The model is a theoretical extension of the PGG and provides insight into how external threats or destructive forces can promote cooperation through population dynamics. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment, nor does it manipulate or analyze standard punishment mechanisms. Its main value is in highlighting that destructive forces can, under certain conditions, increase cooperation, but this is not equivalent to the effect of punishment in PGGs.

- source: Takács, K., & Janky, B. (2007). Smiling contributions:: Social control in a public goods game with network decline. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical framework for public goods games with endogenous network change and social control mechanisms. It predicts that clustered networks reduce collective action and that the ability to delete ties leads to segregation between contributors and defectors. Social control is operationalized as the ability to send happy or sad smileys, which are intended to induce conformity or selective incentives. The experimental design is described, but no data or results are presented. The main dependent variable in the proposed experiments is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how network structure and non-costly social control (smileys) might affect contribution behavior in public goods games. However, it does not provide empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it study costly punishment as typically defined in the literature. The model suggests that social control (even if non-costly) and network clustering can influence cooperation, but the lack of empirical results and the focus on non-payoff outcomes limit its direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions. The design dimensions are discussed in the context of the model and proposed experiment, but not tested empirically.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2010). Reward and cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly reward in a spatial public goods game can promote cooperation, particularly when the synergy factor (MPCR) is low. However, the effect is non-monotonic: moderate rewards can be more effective than high rewards due to the emergence of cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and rewarding cooperators. At high synergy factors, network reciprocity alone can sustain cooperation, and reward mainly determines which cooperative strategy prevails. The paper notes that, compared to punishment, reward is less efficient at promoting cooperation, especially because rewarding cooperators always pay a cost, making them vulnerable to second-order free-riders. The main reported outcomes are the stationary frequencies of each strategy and the phase diagrams of their coexistence, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reward (not punishment) on cooperation in spatial public goods games. It does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it suggests that costly reward can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but is generally less effective than punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the role of reward mechanisms and the importance of spatial structure, synergy factor (MPCR), and cost-benefit ratios in shaping cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only adjacent: it does not provide direct evidence on punishment or efficiency, but may inform expectations about the relative effectiveness of reward versus punishment and the importance of game parameters.

- source: Li, Y. X., Jin, X. G., Su, X. C., Kong, F. S., & Peng, C. B. (2010). Cooperation and charity in spatial public goods game under different strategy update rules. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that charity (modeled as payoff transfer from egalitarians to the lowest-payoff neighbor) does not enhance cooperation under random selection update rules, but does promote cooperation under threshold selection rules (where only the lowest-payoff individuals update their strategies). The effect of charity is measured by the frequency of cooperation, not by group payoff or efficiency. The model shows that the presence of charity can lower the threshold for the multiplication factor (r) needed to sustain cooperation under threshold selection. The frequency of charity is positively correlated with the variance of payoffs in the population. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect evidence about the effect of a redistribution mechanism (charity) on cooperation rates under different update rules. The findings suggest that the effect of a second-stage redistribution mechanism (distinct from punishment) on cooperation depends on the selection dynamics, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for the prediction task. The game structure and update rules are described in detail, which may be useful for mapping to similar simulation studies.

- source: Wu, T., Fu, F., & Wang, L. (2009). Partner selections in public goods games with constant group size. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that, in public goods games with constant group size on networks, increasing group size generally promotes higher cooperation rates, especially when individuals can select partners based on reputation. Reputation-based partner selection (interpreted as costless exclusion of defectors) is more effective at sustaining cooperation than degree-based selection. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms. The main reported outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. Analytical calculations show that larger group size increases the payoff advantage of cooperators over defectors, but these are not translated into reported efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how group size and partner selection (especially reputation-based exclusion) affect cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff. The reputation-based exclusion mechanism is a form of costless, indirect punishment, which differs from the costly punishment typically studied in experimental PGGs. Therefore, the findings should not be directly transferred to predictions about the efficiency effects of enabling costly punishment. The paper may be useful for understanding the role of group size and partner selection in cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Wang, W. X., Lai, Y. C., & Armbruster, D. (2011). Cascading failures and the emergence of cooperation in evolutionary-game based models of social and economical networks. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in evolutionary games on networks (including PGG), introducing a survival threshold (death/bankruptcy) leads to catastrophic cascades when defection is present. After such cascades, only cooperators survive, regardless of network topology or game type. Defectors may gain higher payoffs temporarily but are ultimately eliminated. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcomes are the fraction of dead agents, the survival of cooperators, and the conditions for extinction or survival, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff. The model's 'sanction' is the endogenous removal of agents with low payoffs, not a punishment technology as in standard PGG experiments. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency. It may provide some adjacent theoretical context about the consequences of defection and the importance of cooperation for system survival, but not about the efficiency impact of punishment interventions.

- source: Quek, H. Y., Tan, K. C., & Tay, A. (2009). Public Goods Provision: An Evolutionary Game Theoretic Study Under Asymmetric Information. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Intelligence and Ai in Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that information asymmetry generally reduces cooperation and welfare compared to homogeneous information settings. Restricting contribution choices (all-or-nothing) and using provision point mechanisms (thresholds for public good provision) can increase efficiency and align welfare across types, especially when both are combined. Reward schemes that select individuals based on accumulated group achievements (rather than group-based rewards) are more effective at increasing welfare and discouraging free riding. Larger group sizes and population sizes have complex effects, with larger populations amplifying both cooperative and defecting tendencies depending on the information structure. The paper does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how information structure, action space granularity, provision mechanisms, and reward/selection schemes affect efficiency in public goods games. However, it does not include any form of punishment or sanctioning, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the impact of other design dimensions, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Benchekroun, H., & Van Long, N. (2008). The build-up of cooperative behavior among non-cooperative selfish agents. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game with no punishment or sanctions, but where agents can condition their behavior on a public 'stock of cooperation' (social history), a continuum of Markovian Nash equilibria exist. These equilibria can support steady-state contribution and welfare levels above the static Nash equilibrium but below the social optimum. The social optimum cannot be stably achieved without punishment. The model provides explicit analytical characterizations of these equilibria and shows that the more patient the agents (lower discount rate), the closer the best equilibrium can approach the social optimum. The paper emphasizes that, unlike models with punishment, the highest sustainable efficiency is strictly less than the social optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games without punishment or sanctions, especially when social history or trust mechanisms are present. It provides strong theoretical evidence that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency can be improved above the Nash baseline if agents coordinate on social-history-based strategies, but the social optimum is not achievable. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment (which is absent here) would allow efficiency to reach or exceed the best equilibrium described in this model, and potentially reach the social optimum. The paper is not informative about the effect of punishment per se, but it provides a theoretical upper bound for efficiency in the absence of punishment, given social history mechanisms.

- source: Archetti, M., & Scheuring, I. (2011). COEXISTENCE OF COOPERATION AND DEFECTION IN PUBLIC GOODS GAMES. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the public goods function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold) fundamentally changes the equilibrium outcome. In nonlinear/threshold (Volunteer's Dilemma-like) games, cooperation and defection can coexist at a stable mixed equilibrium, and the public good is produced with positive probability, even in one-shot, well-mixed populations without relatedness or repeated play. In linear (NPD) games, only defection is stable in the absence of assortment. The paper provides analytical and numerical results for the equilibrium frequency of cooperation and the probability of public good provision as a function of group size, cost/benefit ratio, and the threshold parameter. No punishment, reward, or external enforcement mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the public good production function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold), group size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions. It provides direct theoretical predictions for efficiency and group payoff in the absence of punishment, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. For downstream prediction tasks focused on the effect of punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is valuable for understanding baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, especially in games with nonlinear or threshold public goods functions.

- source: Killingback, T., Bieri, J., & Flatt, T. (2006). Evolution in group-structured populations can resolve the tragedy of the commons. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game with continuous investment, cooperation and high group efficiency do not evolve in well-mixed populations, but can evolve and be stably maintained in group-structured populations with limited dispersal. The mechanism relies on group structure and population dynamics, not on punishment, reputation, or optional participation. The model demonstrates that for a range of dispersal rates, high levels of cooperative investment (and thus high group payoffs) can be sustained. However, the model does not include any form of punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is explicitly excluded from the model. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment and for considering the effects of group structure and dispersal, but it does not inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is relevant only as a control or baseline for environments without punishment.

- source: Takezawa, M., & Price, M. E. (2010). Revisiting The Evolution of Reciprocity in Sizable Groups: Continuous reciprocity in the repeated n-person prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated n-person public goods games, continuous reciprocity (where players match the average contribution of others) is much more robust than discrete reciprocity (all-or-nothing) in supporting the evolution of cooperation, especially as the marginal per capita return (mpcr) increases. The basin of attraction for continuous reciprocity grows almost linearly with efficiency, allowing cooperation to invade from low initial frequencies when mpcr is high. Assortative interaction (preferential grouping of reciprocators) further enhances the viability of continuous reciprocity, even at lower efficiency. However, both forms of reciprocity struggle to invade when efficiency is low or group size is large, unless assortativity is present. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency of cooperation (mpcr) is a critical moderator of the success of reciprocity-based cooperation in public goods games. In the absence of punishment, high mpcr (approaching 1) is necessary for cooperation to be stable or to invade. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the mpcr dimension should be weighted heavily when estimating efficiency outcomes in control (no-punishment) games. However, because the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, it does not directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight that in high-mpcr environments, cooperation can be stable even without punishment, while in low-mpcr environments, cooperation is unlikely to persist without additional mechanisms.

- source: Boza, G., & Számadó, S. (2010). Beneficial laggards: multilevel selection, cooperative polymorphism and division of labour in Threshold Public Good Games. *Bmc Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public good games, stable polymorphism (coexistence of cooperators and defectors) can be maintained by multilevel selection, even in the absence of punishment or spatial structure. Group efficiency (measured as group payoff or average fitness) is maximized when the number of cooperators matches the threshold required for success, not when all members cooperate. Increasing group size or cost of cooperation reduces the range of parameters where cooperation is stable. Division of labour can evolve, with individuals specializing in different collective actions. Spatial structure further promotes cooperation. The paper does not address punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effects of punishment or sanctions in public good games. It is highly relevant for understanding baseline efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in threshold PGGs without punishment, and for predicting the efficiency of control games (punishment disabled) as a function of group size, cost, and threshold. However, it does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled, nor does it provide evidence on any moderators of punishment effects. It is useful for modeling baseline efficiency in threshold PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Hauert, C., Holmes, M., & Doebeli, M. (2006). Evolutionary games and population dynamics: maintenance of cooperation in public goods games. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games, if population density is allowed to vary as a function of average payoff, cooperation can be maintained through eco-evolutionary feedback. When defection leads to low payoffs, population density drops, which in turn reduces group size and can make cooperation advantageous. This can result in stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors at intermediate frequencies, with population density and average payoff stabilizing at levels where neither strategy dominates. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is not modeled. However, it is highly informative about how ecological feedback (variable group size as a function of payoff) can stabilize cooperation and improve group payoffs in the absence of punishment. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it provides a theoretical baseline for understanding efficiency in public goods games without punishment or reward.

- source: Zhao, L., Ye, X. J., Huang, Z. G., Sun, J. T., Yang, L., Do, Y., & Wang, Y. H. (2010). Promotion of cooperation in the form C0C1D classified by 'degree grads' in a scale-free network. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in scale-free networks, cooperation can persist even when the PGG enhancement factor r < 1, due to a mechanism where high-degree ('hub') cooperators (C0) are imitated by their neighbors (C1), who may receive negative payoffs but still support the hub. The average payoff of agents is analyzed as a function of their network position (degree grads), showing that only the hubs (C0) receive positive payoffs, while most other cooperators (C1) have negative payoffs. The system-level average payoff increases with r, but the persistence of cooperation at low r is due to network structure and imitation, not payoff incentives. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how network structure and imitation can sustain cooperation and affect payoff distribution, but it does not inform predictions about the impact of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task (treatment efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper is not directly relevant, as punishment is not present or discussed as a mechanism.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
