  findings: The paper finds that in scale-free networks, assortative degree mixing (where hubs are connected to other hubs) inhibits the emergence and sustainment of cooperation in the PGG, as the core-periphery structure weakens the ability of cooperative hubs to influence peripheral nodes. In disassortative networks (where hubs connect to low-degree nodes), cooperation can persist even at low multiplication factors, as hubs maintain their initial strategies and can influence their leaf neighbors. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, with some analysis of average payoff per degree. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It is relevant for understanding how network structure (degree correlation) affects cooperation and individual payoffs in PGGs without punishment. The findings may be useful for contextualizing baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in networked PGGs, but do not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., Wang, W. X., Lai, Y. C., & Wang, B. H. (2011). Impact of link deletions on public cooperation in scale-free networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in multi-group PGGs on scale-free networks, deleting links connected to hubs (network attack) predominantly reduces the fraction of cooperators, especially at low multiplication factors. There is a narrow region where the effect is strictly negative, but outside this region, an intermediate number of link deletions can minimize cooperation. Wealth distributions become less heterogeneous as links are deleted, which is associated with lower cooperation. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but does analyze total payoffs (wealth) at the individual level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how network structure and heterogeneity affect cooperation and individual payoffs, but not for predicting the effect of punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task. However, the analysis of wealth distributions may offer indirect insights into how structural features (like network heterogeneity) can moderate payoff outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Huang, Z. G., Wu, Z. X., Wu, A. C., Yang, L., & Wang, Y. H. (2008). Role of collective influence in promoting cooperation. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games on heterogeneous networks, both the structure of the network and the way individuals distribute their contributions (collective influence parameter alpha) strongly affect cooperation and payoffs. When individuals prefer to contribute more to smaller groups (alpha < 0, social loafing), cooperators receive higher net payoffs, and cooperation is more likely to persist, especially in scale-free networks. The paper provides detailed analysis of individual net payoffs (P_ii) as a function of network degree and alpha, showing that social loafing can make cooperation individually rational for a larger fraction of players. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides useful context for how network structure and contribution allocation rules (collective influence) affect cooperation and payoffs in PGG-like environments. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency and cooperation in the absence of punishment, especially in networked or heterogeneous group settings.

- source: Santos, F. C., Santos, M. D., & Pacheco, J. M. (2008). Social diversity promotes the emergence of cooperation in public goods games. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing social diversity via heterogeneous (scale-free) network structures and varying individual participation in multiple public goods games can significantly enhance the emergence and stability of cooperation. Two main models are compared: (1) fixed cost per game (each cooperator pays a cost in every game they play), and (2) fixed cost per individual (each cooperator pays a total cost, divided among all games they play). The latter model, combined with network heterogeneity, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperation, even at lower enhancement factors. The paper also shows that in all-cooperator populations, wealth (fitness) distributions are more unequal on scale-free graphs, but the overall number of 'poor' is reduced under the fixed cost per individual model. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or reputation mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff relative to the cooperative optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it explicitly excludes punishment and focuses on the effects of social diversity and network structure. It is useful for understanding how heterogeneity in group structure and contribution rules can promote cooperation, but does not inform the impact of punishment or sanctions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, especially in heterogeneous network settings.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2010). Diversity of game strategies promotes the evolution of cooperation in public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a continuous range of cooperation degrees (strategy diversity) in public goods games leads to higher average cooperation, especially when all players have equal initial wealth. The transition to high cooperation occurs at lower values of the multiplication factor (mpcr) than in previous models with only two strategies. The presence of strategy diversity prevents long-term exploitation and leads to the extinction of defectors at relatively low mpcr values. However, the paper does not study punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings on strategy diversity and cooperation rates may be contextually informative for understanding how game structure affects cooperation, but it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to adapt both their strategies and their network connections leads to higher and more robust levels of cooperation in public goods games. The time scale of network updating relative to strategy updating (parameterized by ω) significantly affects the prevalence of cooperation. Heterogeneous network structures and clustering emerge, which further support cooperation. However, the paper does not include any form of punishment or sanctioning, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how network adaptation and coevolution can promote cooperation, but are not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wang, B. K., Chen, X. J., & Wang, L. (2012). Probabilistic interconnection between interdependent networks promotes cooperation in the public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game played on two interdependent networks, the probability of interconnection between corresponding nodes in the two layers has a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: an intermediate interconnection probability maximizes the fraction of cooperators in the system. The coupling effect between layers can promote cooperation in both, even if one starts with only defectors. The study does not include or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency/payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how network structure and interdependence affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based or punishment-related predictions. The absence of punishment, reward, and efficiency outcomes means it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Xiao, Y., & Hua, D. Y. (2012). Promotion of Cooperation in a Spatial Public Goods Game with Long Range Learning and Mobility. *Chinese Physics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the evolution of cooperation in a spatial public goods game is strongly affected by the interplay of population density, learning range, and mobility range. When the learning range is larger than the mobility range, small local groups at low population density support high cooperation. At moderate densities, mobility allows cooperators to escape defector invasions, supporting stable cooperation. When the mobility range is larger than the learning range, high population density enables the formation of compact cooperator domains, which also promotes cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; no efficiency or group payoff results are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It is relevant for understanding how spatial structure, mobility, and learning range affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based efficiency outcomes or the effect of punishment. Therefore, it should not be weighted heavily in predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency in the context of punishment.

- source: Roca, C. P., & Helbing, D. (2011). Emergence of social cohesion in a model society of greedy, mobile individuals. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial public goods game with voluntary contributions and no punishment or reward, cooperation and social cohesion can emerge if individuals have moderate levels of greediness (aspiration). Too little greediness leads to stagnation in noncooperation, while too much greediness destabilizes cooperation and social ties. The model demonstrates that these beneficial levels of greediness can be evolutionarily selected. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, agglomeration, instability), not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include or analyze punishment, and thus provides no direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It models a PGG environment without punishment or reward, and focuses on the role of individual learning and mobility. While it is useful for understanding alternative mechanisms for cooperation, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. The game design dimensions are partially covered (e.g., player count, group size, mpcr), but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot be used to predict efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Shi, D. M., Zhuang, Y., & Wang, B. H. (2012). Effect of the depreciation of public goods in spatial public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a depreciation mechanism (where the value of the public good is reduced if not all cooperate) promotes cooperation compared to the standard PGG. There is an optimal intermediate value of the depreciation parameter (beta) that maximizes cooperation, and this optimal range broadens as the multiplication factor (r) increases. The results are based on simulations of a spatial PGG on a lattice, with the main outcome being the fraction of cooperators. No punishment or sanctioning is studied, and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are limited to the effects of a depreciation mechanism on cooperation rates. It may be relevant for understanding how endogenous changes to the public good's value affect cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment interventions.

- source: Li, J., Wu, T., Zeng, G., & Wang, L. (2012). Selective investment promotes cooperation in public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that selective investment (where cooperators invest in only a fraction of their neighbors) robustly promotes cooperation in networked public goods games. Lower selectivity parameter (alpha) accelerates the emergence and maintenance of cooperation compared to the classical model where cooperators invest in all groups. Social-preference and wealth-preference patterns (where investment is biased toward high-degree or high-payoff neighbors) hamper the speed and stability of cooperation compared to random selection. Anti-preference patterns (investing in less connected or lower-payoff neighbors) can further accelerate cooperation. Theoretical analysis on double-star networks supports these simulation results. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how network structure and selective investment affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results may be useful for contextualizing the baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2009). Topology-independent impact of noise on cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in spatial public goods games, increasing the group size (number of players per game) can qualitatively change the relationship between noise (uncertainty in strategy adoption) and the survival of cooperation. On regular graphs with low clustering (no overlapping triangles), pairwise interactions (G=2) show an optimal intermediate noise level for cooperation, but larger groups (G>2) remove this effect and make the system behave more like high-clustering networks. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it provides informative context about how group size and network topology affect cooperation rates in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatial PGGs without sanctions.

- source: Sigmund, K., Hauert, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2001). Reward and punishment. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player public goods minigames with punishment or reward, evolutionary dynamics without reputation lead to asocial equilibria with low efficiency, regardless of the presence of punishment or reward options. When a small chance of reputation is introduced (players can sometimes observe others' punishment/reward behavior), the system can support a stable, high-efficiency equilibrium with cooperation and punishment. This effect is stronger and more robust for punishment than for reward. The model predicts that punishment with reputation is much more effective at promoting group efficiency than reward with reputation, and that without reputation, neither mechanism is effective in the long run.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment alone does not increase efficiency in public goods games unless players have some information about others' punishment behavior (i.e., reputation). In the absence of reputation, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency; the system converges to defection and non-punishment. With even a small probability of reputation, enabling punishment can create a bistable system where high-efficiency outcomes are possible and can be risk-dominant. The effect is parameter-dependent (e.g., punishment cost, effectiveness, benefit/cost of cooperation), but the qualitative result is robust. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the presence of reputation or information about others' actions. If the game design does not allow for reputation, enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency. If reputation is present, punishment can have a strong positive effect on efficiency, more so than reward.

- source: Tarui, N., Mason, C. F., Polasky, S., & Ellis, G. (2008). Cooperation in the commons with unobservable actions. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dynamic common property resource game with unobservable actions, a two-part symmetric group punishment strategy (harsh group punishment after a defection, then forgiveness and return to cooperation) can support full cooperation (first-best efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, provided certain conditions on the discount factor, resource growth rate, and group size are met. The analysis shows that the threat of the harshest possible group punishment (worst perfect equilibrium) is sufficient to deter defection, even when individual actions are not observable. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for when this is possible and uses numerical simulations to illustrate how the sustainability of cooperation depends on group size and other parameters. Notably, the effect of group size is non-monotonic: increasing group size can initially make cooperation easier to sustain (by making punishment more credible), but for larger groups, the incentive to defect eventually dominates, making cooperation harder to sustain.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in public-goods-like environments with unobservable actions, the availability of credible, symmetric group punishment can enable full efficiency (relative to the first-best cooperative outcome), provided the punishment is sufficiently harsh and the parameter conditions (discount factor, resource growth, group size) are favorable. The analysis is directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games where only aggregate outcomes are observable. The findings suggest that punishment can have a strongly positive effect on efficiency, but only if the punishment is credible and harsh enough to deter defection. The effect of group size is nuanced: increasing group size can initially make punishment more credible (and thus support efficiency), but for larger groups, the incentive to defect increases and efficiency becomes harder to sustain. The paper does not address other design dimensions such as chat, reward, or information feedback, so its predictive value is strongest for settings matching the model's assumptions (no chat, no reward, symmetric group punishment, unobservable individual actions, continuous contribution/harvest choices).

- source: Congleton, R. D., & Vanberg, V. J. (2001). Help, harm or avoid? On the personal advantage of dispositions to cooperate and punish in multilateral PD games with exit. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in three-person repeated joint production games with exit, conditional cooperation and the ability to exit dysfunctional teams allow for higher average payoffs (efficiency) than unconditional defection. However, in short-lived enterprises, targeted punishment of defectors is necessary to sustain cooperation and high payoffs. When targeted punishment is strong enough, even in short-lived games, cooperation and efficiency are restored. The results are robust in both static and evolutionary (replicator dynamic) simulations. The study provides direct evidence that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms are key moderators of the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that enabling targeted punishment in small-group repeated public goods games with exit increases efficiency, especially when the number of rounds is small. The effect of punishment is more positive when the punishment is strong (high impact per cost) and can be targeted at defectors. The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of targeted punishment, the number of rounds, and the ability to exit are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. Control efficiency (without punishment) is higher when exit and conditional cooperation are possible, but enabling punishment further increases efficiency, especially in short games. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: ABREU, D., MILGROM, P., & PEARCE, D. (1991). INFORMATION AND TIMING IN REPEATED PARTNERSHIPS. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated partnership games with imperfect monitoring, the ability to sustain efficient cooperation through punishment is fundamentally limited by the informativeness of the monitoring technology and the timing of information. When monitoring is imperfect, reducing the interest rate (making players more patient) increases the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes, potentially allowing near-first-best payoffs. However, shortening the period of fixed action or reducing the lag with which information is reported can actually decrease efficiency, sometimes eliminating the possibility of cooperation altogether. Delaying information release can, counterintuitively, increase efficiency by reducing the cost of deterring deviations. The paper provides generalizations to n-player symmetric games and shows that these results are robust to more complex action and signal spaces.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency of repeated public-goods-like games with punishment under imperfect monitoring. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the informativeness of monitoring, the timing of information, and the patience of players. In particular, enabling punishment does not guarantee high efficiency unless monitoring is sufficiently informative and the timing of information and action periods is chosen carefully. For prediction, the paper suggests that increasing patience (lower interest rate) is reliably associated with higher efficiency, but increasing the frequency of action or information updates can reduce efficiency when monitoring is imperfect. The explicit formulas and comparative statics in the paper can be used to estimate efficiency given the relevant game design dimensions.

- source: Noailly, J., van den Bergh, J. C. J. M., & Withagen, C. A. (2009). Local and Global Interactions in an Evolutionary Resource Game. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that local punishment mechanisms in a spatial CPR game allow for the emergence and persistence of cooperative and mixed-strategy equilibria, which are associated with higher aggregate payoffs and efficiency than the all-defector equilibrium. The frequency of efficient equilibria increases with higher sanction levels and larger population sizes, and spatial clustering of cooperators and enforcers is crucial for sustaining cooperation. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and local enforcement can overcome the limitations of non-spatial models, leading to more diverse and efficient outcomes. Sensitivity analysis shows that increasing the cost or effectiveness of sanctions, or increasing population size, generally increases the prevalence of efficient (cooperative or mixed) equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and aggregate payoff, especially when punishment is local and sufficiently strong. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, the introduction of local punishment will often lead to higher efficiency, particularly in larger groups and with higher sanction levels. The results also highlight the importance of spatial structure and local interactions, which may moderate the effect of punishment compared to non-spatial models. The paper's parameter sensitivity analysis can inform predictions about how changes in punishment cost, effectiveness, and group size affect the efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Okada, D., & Bingham, P. M. (2008). Human uniqueness-self-interest and social cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a two-stage game-theoretic model in which players can cooperate or not, and, if there are non-cooperators, cooperators can choose to fight (coerce) or not. The key result is that the cost structure of coercion (punishment) is decisive: if coercion is cheap and effective (as in synchronous/remote attack, e.g., human projectile weapons), then robust equilibria with high cooperation and high efficiency are possible. If coercion is costly and ineffective (as in asynchronous attack, e.g., most non-human animals), only non-cooperation is robust, and group efficiency is low. The model thus predicts that enabling effective, low-cost punishment can transform a low-efficiency public goods game into a high-efficiency one, provided certain thresholds are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling effective, low-cost punishment (i.e., high punishment effectiveness per unit cost) in a public-goods-like environment can shift the equilibrium from low to high efficiency, especially as group size increases. The model is directly informative for the effect of punishment cost and punishment technology (effectiveness), and indirectly for player count and the structure of the contribution decision. It suggests that, all else equal, lowering punishment cost or increasing punishment effectiveness should make punishment more likely to increase efficiency. The findings are most relevant for one-shot or short games with simultaneous moves and no communication, and may be less directly applicable to repeated or highly structured lab experiments with additional features.

- source: Noailly, J., Withagen, C. A., & van den Bergh, J. C. J. M. (2007). Spatial evolution of social norms in a common-pool resource game. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a spatial CPR game, the introduction of enforcers (who punish defectors) leads to the emergence and persistence of cooperative equilibria, which are associated with higher aggregate payoffs and more efficient resource use. The frequency of such efficient equilibria increases with the sanction rate (punishment effectiveness) and is further enhanced when resource dynamics (feedback from resource depletion) are included. Simulations show that, for a wide range of parameters, punishment increases the likelihood of equilibria with cooperation and higher payoffs, compared to scenarios without punishment. The spatial structure (local punishment) is crucial for these results, as it allows cooperators and enforcers to protect each other and sustain cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in a spatial CPR (public goods-like) game increases the frequency of efficient, high-payoff equilibria, especially when the punishment is sufficiently strong and resource feedbacks are present. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (with nontrivial cost and effectiveness), local interaction, and resource dynamics should be expected to increase treatment efficiency relative to control. The results are most directly applicable to settings with local punishment, spatial structure, and evolutionary imitation dynamics, but the qualitative direction (punishment increases efficiency) is robust across parameter sweeps. The paper does not provide precise quantitative predictions for efficiency, but shows that the effect is substantial and positive under a wide range of conditions.

- source: De Silva, H., Hauert, C., Traulsen, A., & Sigmund, K. (2010). Freedom, enforcement, and the social dilemma of strong altruism. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical analysis and simulations demonstrate that costly punishment enables the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in joint effort games, but only when participation is voluntary. In the voluntary regime, the stationary distribution is dominated by punishers, leading to higher group payoffs and efficiency. In the compulsory regime, defectors dominate and group efficiency collapses. This result is robust for both 'weak' and 'strong' altruism (i.e., whether or not contributors benefit from their own contributions). The findings imply that the combination of voluntary participation and punishment is critical for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency is highly contingent on whether participation in the public goods game is voluntary or compulsory. If participation is voluntary, introducing punishment leads to a regime dominated by punishers and high group efficiency. If participation is compulsory, punishment does not prevent the dominance of defectors and group efficiency remains low. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of voluntary participation (i.e., whether players can opt out) is a key moderator of the efficiency impact of punishment. The model also suggests that these results are robust to variations in group size, population size, and the strength of selection, as long as the basic structure (voluntary participation, costly punishment) is maintained.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). The evolution of strong reciprocity: cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The model shows that in groups where some members are willing to incur costs to punish norm violators (strong reciprocators), high levels of cooperation and group efficiency are sustained. Without punishment, groups devolve to all-Selfish agents and low efficiency. The presence of punishment leads to a stable mix of Reciprocators, Cooperators, and Selfish agents, with low average shirking rates and high group payoffs. The model is robust to changes in group size, cost of punishment, and other parameters. The findings support the idea that costly punishment is a key mechanism for sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling costly punishment in public-goods-game-like environments increases group efficiency and average payoffs, relative to control conditions without punishment. The model is not an empirical experiment but simulates a wide range of parameter values, showing that the positive effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to variations in group size, cost of punishment, and other design features. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly punishment mechanism (even with moderate cost and effectiveness) should be expected to increase efficiency, especially in settings with repeated group interaction and no central authority. The model's structure and outcomes are directly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Weibull, J. W., & Salomonsson, M. (2006). Natural selection and social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary stability and efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games depend on the structure of reproductive externalities. When group members' fitness is positively affected by others' payoffs (cooperative breeding), punishment and cooperation can become stable and lead to higher group welfare, especially if the punishment is sufficiently harsh and the cost is not too high. In contrast, under strong reproductive competition, punishment can be stable but may not increase group efficiency. The model shows that the presence and effectiveness of punishment as a mechanism for promoting cooperation and efficiency is highly sensitive to the underlying fitness function and game parameters. The analysis provides explicit conditions for when punishment leads to more efficient, cooperative outcomes and when it does not.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on when punishment is likely to increase efficiency in public-goods-like games, depending on the game design and the nature of social/reproductive externalities. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universal: it can be positive, nil, or even negative, depending on whether the environment favors cooperative or competitive externalities. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the structure of payoffs, and the degree of group interdependence (e.g., MPCR analogs) are critical moderators. The findings support the idea that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when group welfare is a significant component of individual fitness, and less likely (or even counterproductive) when individual competition dominates.

- source: POLLOCK, G. B. (1988). POPULATION-STRUCTURE, SPITE, AND THE ITERATED PRISONERS-DILEMMA. *American Journal of Physical Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that punishment-based strategies (TFT/NTFT) can maintain high group efficiency and suppress defection when the population is structured into multiple groups or dyads, allowing intergroup competition. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation and high payoffs is reduced as the scope of spite (the number of groups a defector can affect) increases or as population structure becomes less partitioned. In the limit of a single group (no structure), punishment cannot prevent defection and efficiency collapses. The analysis generalizes to n-person games, showing that the same logic applies: partitioned populations with group-level competition allow punishment to sustain cooperation and high efficiency, while unstructured populations do not.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to control, but only when the game design includes sufficient population structure (multiple groups, limited overlap in group membership, or mechanisms for intergroup competition). If the game is a single group or highly overlapping, punishment will not improve efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the marginal per-capita return, group size, and population structure can be specified, and they highlight the importance of these dimensions as moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Kendal, J., Feldman, M. W., & Aoki, K. (2006). Cultural coevolution of norm adoption and enforcement when punishers are rewarded or non-punishers are punished. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both reward-based and punishment-based metanorms can enable the stable coexistence and fixation of norm-adopting punishers, leading to high group efficiency (mean fitness) under a wide range of parameter values. The presence of a metanorm (either rewarding punishers or punishing non-punishers) is necessary for this outcome; without it, the only stable equilibrium is full defection (no norm adoption, no punishment). The Reward Model is generally more effective than the Punishment Model in enabling the population to reach the efficient equilibrium, especially when starting from low frequencies of norm-adopters and punishers. The probability of fixation of the efficient equilibrium is higher under the Reward Model, and the region of attraction is larger. The models also show that cultural drift can facilitate the transition from the inefficient to the efficient equilibrium, especially in small populations and when costs of norm observation and punishment are low. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are relevant to public goods game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (especially when reinforced by a metanorm, such as rewarding punishers or punishing non-punishers) can shift a population from an inefficient equilibrium (low efficiency, no cooperation) to an efficient one (high efficiency, full cooperation and sanctioning), provided the costs and benefits are favorable. The results suggest that, in public goods game-like environments, the introduction of punishment (with or without additional metanorms) can substantially increase group efficiency, especially if the cost of punishing is not too high and the benefit to punishers is sufficient. The Reward Model (rewarding punishers) is particularly effective, suggesting that institutional or social mechanisms that reward sanctioning may be more robust in promoting efficiency than those that only punish non-punishers. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, given knowledge of the cost/benefit structure and initial conditions. However, the results are theoretical and assume well-mixed populations and specific forms of cultural transmission.

- source: Sethi, R., & Somanathan, E. (2003). Understanding reciprocity. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment and reciprocity can support efficient outcomes in both repeated and sporadic interactions, but only under certain conditions. In repeated games, strategies that support cooperation (and thus efficiency) can be stable if complexity costs or implementation errors are considered, but many inefficient strategies can also be stable. In one-shot or sporadic interactions, punishment can induce cooperation and improve efficiency, but this stability is fragile: populations of punishers can be invaded by non-punishing cooperators or defectors, and the cost of punishment can erode its effectiveness. In public goods and common pool resource games, the presence of reciprocators willing to punish can lead to higher group payoffs, but only if monitoring and punishment costs are not too high and if group composition allows reciprocators to coordinate. The evolution of mixed populations (reciprocators and defectors) is common, and the efficiency gains from punishment depend on the game structure and population dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines, but only under specific conditions: punishment must be sufficiently effective (high impact per cost), not too costly, and the population must contain enough reciprocators or the ability to assort. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive; in some models, punishment does not prevent the evolution of inefficient outcomes, and the stability of efficient cooperation is sensitive to the cost and observability of punishment, as well as to the possibility of second-order free-riding. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and the ability to observe or assort by type. Control efficiency (without punishment) is often low, but the introduction of punishment can raise efficiency if these moderating conditions are met.

- source: De Silva, H., & Sigmund, K. (2009). Public Good Games with Incentives: The Role of Reputation. *Games, Groups, and the Global Good*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with punishment, cooperation and punishment do not persist in the long run under strict anonymity; the population converges to asocial strategies. Introducing reputation—where players can learn about others' punishment behavior—creates bistability: both full cooperation with punishment and full defection without punishment can be stable. The presence of errors (occasional failures to cooperate) is necessary for reputation to be informative and for punishment to have an effect. The model is primarily theoretical and does not provide explicit efficiency or group payoff results, but the implication is that reputation can enable punishment to support higher cooperation and, by extension, higher efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the importance of reputation and information flow in enabling punishment to sustain cooperation in public goods games. In the absence of reputation, punishment does not improve efficiency; with reputation, punishment can support high cooperation, but outcomes depend on initial conditions and parameters. For prediction tasks, the presence or absence of reputation mechanisms (e.g., information about others' punishment behavior) is a critical moderator of punishment's effect on efficiency. However, the paper does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes, so its support is indirect and mechanistic rather than predictive.

- source: Galan, J. M., Latek, M. M., & Rizi, S. M. M. (2011). Axelrod's Metanorm Games on Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of punishment and metapunishment to sustain cooperation in a public-goods-like environment is highly sensitive to the underlying network structure. Specifically, higher clustering and interconnectedness (more triplets per agent) make it more likely that the population will spend most of its time in a cooperative, norm-enforcing state, which is associated with higher expected payoffs. In contrast, higher average degree (more neighbors per agent) can make norm collapse (defection, low punishment) more likely, reducing group welfare. The results are derived from both mathematical analysis of expected payoffs and extensive agent-based simulations, but the main simulation outcomes are behavioral (frequency of norm establishment/collapse) rather than explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, especially when considering the role of network structure. It suggests that punishment (and metapunishment) can lead to higher efficiency (more time in cooperative states) when the network has high clustering and interconnectedness, but may be less effective or even counterproductive in networks with high average degree and low clustering. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff values, its support for quantitative prediction is limited to qualitative or relative effects based on network features. The findings are most relevant when the game design includes networked interactions, costly punishment, and possibly metapunishment.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The coevolution of altruism and punishment: Role of the selfish punisher. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote the evolution of cooperation (and thus higher group payoffs) in both well-mixed and lattice-structured populations, but only under certain conditions. In the score-dependent viability model, punishment is more effective at suppressing defectors and promoting cooperation than in the fertility model. The presence of selfish punishers (defectors who punish other defectors) can help the evolution of altruistic punishers by first eliminating pure defectors, but pure altruists (who cooperate but do not punish) can hinder the evolution of punishment by being exploited. The benefit of cooperation, the fine imposed by punishment, and the cost of punishing all interact to determine evolutionary outcomes. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent and can be positive or negative depending on parameter values and population structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency (group payoff) in public-goods-like games depends on the interaction of several design dimensions: population structure (well-mixed vs. lattice), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the benefit of cooperation, and the evolutionary update rule (viability vs. fertility). Punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when the punishment fine is large relative to the cost of punishing, and in spatially structured populations. However, if the cost of punishing is too high or if pure cooperators are common, punishment may not evolve or may even reduce cooperation. The findings suggest that predictions of treatment efficiency from control efficiency should account for these moderators, especially the structure of interactions and the punishment parameters. However, as the paper does not report explicit efficiency values, its support is indirect and based on evolutionary stability of cooperation.

- source: Nakamaru, M., & Dieckmann, U. (2009). Runaway selection for cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in spatially structured populations, the co-evolution of cooperation and punishment (with continuous strategies for both) can lead to runaway selection for both high cooperation and strict-and-severe punishment. This dynamic is most pronounced when punishment is not too costly and cooperation is neither too cheap nor too expensive. The model shows that strict punishment norms (high strictness and severity) evolve naturally and reinforce high cooperation, resulting in much higher group-level cooperation than in the absence of punishment. In contrast, in well-mixed populations, punishment and cooperation do not evolve. The findings suggest that punishment can greatly enhance group cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, but only under certain cost structures and spatial conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group cooperation and, by extension, efficiency—especially when punishment is strict, severe, and not too costly. The model suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly contingent on the cost structure of cooperation and punishment, and on the population structure (spatial vs. well-mixed). For prediction tasks, the findings imply that punishment will have a more positive effect on efficiency when: (1) the game is spatially structured (analogous to local interactions), (2) punishment is strict and severe, (3) costs of punishment and cooperation are not too high, and (4) baseline cooperation is low without punishment. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical efficiency ratios, so its quantitative predictions should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Whitmeyer, J. M. (2004). The group control catastrophe. *Sociological Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a simulated environment where an external agent can sanction group members (individually or collectively) to induce compliance (a public good), increasing monitoring efficacy does not always monotonically increase compliance. Instead, for intermediate monitoring efficacy, compliance can collapse to zero, creating a bifurcation with two possible equilibria (full compliance or full defection), depending on initial conditions (e.g., the number of initial cooperators). The 'catastrophe' is robust across a range of group sizes, sanction levels, internal control efficacies, and production function shapes (except linear). The equilibrium with high compliance yields higher payoffs for group members than the low-compliance equilibrium. The cost to the agent of obtaining compliance can be minimized by using only one type of sanction (individual or collective) and by leveraging initial cooperation to reach the high-compliance equilibrium, especially at moderate monitoring efficacy. However, the model does not directly report efficiency in the sense of group payoff relative to the maximum possible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (sanctions) on group compliance and payoffs in public-goods-like environments is highly non-linear and can depend critically on monitoring efficacy, initial conditions, and the structure of sanctions. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency or group payoff: under some parameter regimes (especially intermediate monitoring efficacy), punishment can lead to catastrophic drops in compliance and thus lower payoffs. The model also highlights the importance of initial cooperation and the potential for multiple equilibria. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, and because it is a simulation/theory paper rather than an empirical study, its findings should be used to inform qualitative expectations about non-monotonic and path-dependent effects of punishment, rather than to provide quantitative predictions.

- source: Gardner, A., & West, S. A. (2004). Cooperation and punishment, especially in humans. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote and stabilize cooperation in public-goods-like environments, leading to higher group fitness (efficiency), provided that individuals who punish tend to receive more cooperation. The cost of punishment decreases as cooperation increases, making punishment more sustainable in cooperative populations. Relatedness between individuals is not required for punishment to be favored; instead, the key is a positive association between an individual's punishment and the cooperation she receives. The model predicts that punishment is more easily maintained than initially evolved, and that small group size and effective punishment (where the cost of being punished exceeds the cost of cooperation) facilitate the evolutionary stability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective (i.e., the penalty for being punished is greater than the cost of cooperation) and when there is a mechanism (such as behavioral adjustment) that links punishment to increased cooperation received. The findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing punishment should increase group efficiency, particularly in smaller groups and when punishment is not too costly to the punisher. However, the results are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data, so quantitative predictions require caution. The paper also highlights that the effect of punishment is not dependent on relatedness or repeated interactions, but on the structure of behavioral associations.

- source: Shutters, S. T. (2012). Punishment Leads to Cooperative Behavior in Structured Societies. *Evolutionary Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in agent-based simulations of a continuous prisoner's dilemma, cooperation (measured as mean contribution) does not evolve in the absence of both punishment and social structure. When only one of these is present, cooperation also does not evolve. However, when both social structure (local interactions on networks) and the option for costly punishment are present, populations can evolve to high levels of cooperation, depending on the strength of punishment and the network structure. The transition from defection to cooperation requires stronger punishment as network density increases. Scale-free networks are an exception, showing little or no response to punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of both local social structure and effective punishment mechanisms is necessary for the evolution of high cooperation rates in continuous-contribution social dilemmas. However, because the outcome is mean contribution rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence for efficiency effects. The results suggest that, in structured populations (except scale-free networks), enabling punishment can dramatically increase cooperation rates, which would likely increase efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this directly. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment is only effective in structured (non-well-mixed) populations, and that the effectiveness of punishment depends on network density and punishment strength.

- source: Prietula, M. J., & Conway, D. (2009). The evolution of metanorms: quis custodiet ipsos custodes?. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of a public-goods-like dilemma, the addition of metanorms (punishing those who do not punish defectors) is necessary for the emergence of a behavioral norm against defection. When only first-order punishment is available, defection dominates. When metanorms are present, defection rates drop and punishment rates rise, indicating norm emergence. Decoupling the strategies for punishing defectors and non-punishers does not prevent norm emergence and can reduce the overall level of punishment required. In group-structured games with unequal power, metanorms alone do not produce a norm against defection; only with decoupled sanctioning structures does the strong group enforce a norm against the weak group's defection, leading to group dominance rather than universal cooperation. The outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral strategy values (boldness, vengefulness), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence of punishment and especially metanorms (second-order punishment) can dramatically reduce defection rates in public-goods-like environments, even when punishment is costly. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral measures (defection, punishment) rather than efficiency or group payoff, so direct quantitative prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (especially with metanorms) is likely to increase efficiency in analogous empirical games, but the magnitude and exact efficiency outcomes are not reported. The paper also highlights that group structure and the sophistication of the punishment mechanism (e.g., decoupling punishment types) can moderate the effect, sometimes leading to group dominance rather than overall cooperation.

- source: Hilbe, C., & Traulsen, A. (2012). Emergence of responsible sanctions without second order free riders, antisocial punishment or spite. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that responsible punishment and cooperation can co-evolve and become stable in populations when interactions are non-anonymous and reputation can be built. In anonymous settings, cooperation and responsible punishment do not emerge, and antisocial punishment can persist. The model provides a threshold for the amount of information (reputation) needed for responsible punishment to be adaptive. The results are robust to errors in reputation and extend to public goods games with more than two players, though larger group sizes raise the threshold for reputation needed. The paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but the emergence of cooperation and responsible punishment implies higher group welfare than in the absence of these behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games can increase cooperation and, by implication, efficiency, but only when reputation or non-anonymity is present. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by whether players can observe each other's actions and reputations. In anonymous settings, punishment does not promote cooperation or efficiency. The model also suggests that larger group sizes make it harder for punishment to be effective. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes, its value for direct quantitative prediction is limited; it is most useful for understanding the qualitative role of reputation, anonymity, and group size as moderators.

- source: Lehmann, L., Rousset, F., Roze, D., & Keller, L. (2007). Strong reciprocity or strong ferocity? A population genetic view of the evolution of altruistic punishment. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (costly helping plus costly punishment) can evolve in spatially structured populations if helping and punishment are tightly linked (no recombination) and relatedness is sufficiently high. In this case, strong reciprocity can invade even when rare. When helping and punishment are unlinked (recombination is strong), neither costly helping nor costly punishment can invade from rarity unless punishment yields a direct fitness benefit to the actor. The model shows that kin selection (genetic or cultural) is necessary for the evolution of strong reciprocity. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide direct predictions about group efficiency or payoff, but rather about the evolutionary stability and spread of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the evolutionary preconditions for the emergence of punishment and cooperation in public-goods-like settings, especially the importance of linkage between helping and punishment, population structure (relatedness), and direct fitness effects. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or payoff with and without punishment, nor does it report outcomes directly comparable to experimental public goods games. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting that punishment is unlikely to increase cooperation or efficiency in anonymous, unstructured, or one-shot settings unless there is kinship or direct benefit to punishers. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Henrich, J., & Henrich, N. (2006). Culture, evolution and the puzzle of human cooperation. *Cognitive Systems Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that punishment, especially when combined with cultural learning mechanisms (such as conformist and prestige-biased transmission), can stabilize costly cooperative norms in human groups. It reviews theoretical models showing that the effectiveness of punishment in maintaining cooperation depends on the cost of punishing relative to the cost of being punished, and that social norms enforced by punishment can persist even when the cooperative act is individually costly. The paper also discusses how group-level selection can favor groups with more effective cooperative norms, and how cultural evolution allows for rapid adaptation of cooperation strategies. However, the paper does not provide empirical or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the mechanisms and evolutionary logic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation in public goods-like environments, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly relative to the cost of being punished. It highlights the importance of social norms, cultural learning, and group structure in moderating the effects of punishment. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the magnitude of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. Its value for prediction lies in its mechanistic insights and identification of key moderators (e.g., punishment cost/effectiveness, group size, norm strength), but not in providing direct outcome estimates.

- source: Guzmán, R. A., Rodríguez-Sickert, C., & Rowthorn, R. (2007). When in Rome, do as the Romans do:: the coevolution of altruistic punishment, conformist learning, and cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of both conformist learning and altruistic punishment allows for the stable maintenance of high levels of cooperation in large groups, even when migration is high and intergroup conflict is rare. Conformism preserves between-group variation and stabilizes punishment, while punishment protects against the spread of defection. The model demonstrates that cooperation can actually increase with group size when both mechanisms are present. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral frequencies (cooperation, punishment) rather than direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment (especially when combined with conformist learning) can sustain high cooperation in public-goods-like environments, particularly as group size increases. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given control efficiency and game design dimensions. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of conformism and punishment as moderators of cooperation, especially in large groups and under high migration. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Fehr, E., & Fischbacher, U. (2003). The nature of human altruism. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment (costly punishment of norm violators) is a robust phenomenon in laboratory experiments, including public goods games and third-party punishment games. It reviews evidence that, in the absence of punishment, cooperation in public goods games typically decays over repeated rounds, but that the opportunity for punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation. The paper also discusses the limits of altruism, noting that higher costs of punishment or contribution reduce the frequency of these behaviors. While the paper provides strong theoretical and empirical support for the importance of punishment in sustaining cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff effects from punishment interventions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment in public goods games and similar environments can sustain higher cooperation and deter free-riding, especially in repeated or group settings. However, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, MPCR, punishment cost, etc.) moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is most useful for contextualizing the likely direction of punishment effects (i.e., more positive for cooperation and, by implication, possibly for efficiency), but it does not provide parameter estimates or direct outcome data for model training or calibration.

- source: Engelen, B. (2008). The sources of cooperation - On strong reciprocity and its theoretical implications. *Theory & Psychology*.

  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The paper argues that standard egoistic and instrumental accounts (kin selection, direct/indirect reciprocity, reciprocal altruism, costly signaling) cannot fully explain the high levels of cooperation and costly punishment observed in human experiments such as public goods games, ultimatum games, and trust games. Instead, it posits that 'strong reciprocity'—a norm-driven, non-instrumental willingness to cooperate and punish defectors—better accounts for the empirical findings. The paper highlights that such behavior is observed even in one-shot, anonymous interactions, and is motivated by internalized norms and emotions rather than by material self-interest. The author further argues that cultural group selection and the internalization of social norms are key to understanding the evolutionary origins of strong reciprocity and widespread human cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct quantitative or experimental evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, provide strong theoretical and interpretive support for the idea that the availability of punishment mechanisms can sustain higher levels of cooperation, even at personal cost, and that this is robust to one-shot and anonymous settings. For the prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment (especially when norm internalization is strong) is likely to increase cooperation, but it does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes directly. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, but it does support the general mechanism by which punishment can increase cooperation in public goods environments.

It reviews evidence from behavioral economics (including public goods games and ultimatum games) showing that the possibility of punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance, and that individuals are willing to incur costs to punish norm violators. The commentary also synthesizes neuroscientific findings on the brain regions involved in norm enforcement, proposing a model in which domain-general cognitive processes (such as value learning, mentalizing, and response selection) are repurposed for social norm enforcement. The paper notes that both punishment and reward can promote cooperation, but does not provide new empirical data on efficiency or payoff outcomes.

For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the general expectation that enabling punishment will increase cooperation, but does not allow for precise prediction of efficiency changes based on game design dimensions or control efficiency.
