- source: Boyd, R., & Richerson, P. J. (2009). Voting with your feet: Payoff biased migration and the evolution of group beneficial behavior. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that payoff-biased migration—where individuals move from lower-payoff to higher-payoff groups and assimilate—can lead to the spread and predominance of group-beneficial behaviors, resulting in higher average group payoffs (efficiency). This effect is strongest when migration rates are low enough to maintain between-group cultural variation, but not so low as to prevent migration from having an effect. The model is general and can represent public goods, mutualism, and punishment-based norm enforcement. The presence of punishment is discussed as a mechanism that can stabilize group-beneficial equilibria, which are associated with higher group payoffs. The model predicts that, under plausible conditions, group-beneficial (efficient) norms will predominate, but alternative (less efficient) norms may persist in a polymorphic equilibrium.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that mechanisms which stabilize group-beneficial behaviors (including punishment) can lead to higher group efficiency, especially in structured populations with migration. The model is not a direct PGG experiment, but its payoff structure and discussion of punishment-based norm enforcement are closely related. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of effective punishment (or other norm-stabilizing mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when migration or group structure allows for between-group variation and selective migration. However, the model is abstract and does not provide quantitative estimates for specific game design parameters such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost, so its guidance is qualitative: enabling effective punishment or norm enforcement should increase efficiency, especially when group structure and migration allow for the persistence and spread of efficient norms.

- source: Rankin, D. J., Bargum, K., & Kokko, H. (2007). The tragedy of the commons in evolutionary biology. *Trends in Ecology & Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper reviews the tragedy of the commons as a framework for understanding how individual-level selfishness can reduce group-level outcomes, including group fitness and productivity, in both human and non-human systems. It highlights that mechanisms such as punishment, policing, kin selection, and ecological feedbacks can prevent or reduce the severity of the tragedy, sometimes restoring group productivity or preventing collapse. The review draws analogies to public goods games and discusses the importance of distinguishing between complete collapse (collapsing tragedy) and partial reduction in group fitness (component tragedy).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment and related mechanisms can improve group outcomes (efficiency) in public-goods-like environments by altering incentives and deterring selfish behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence that would allow direct prediction of efficiency changes from game design dimensions. Its value lies in supporting the general expectation that punishment, when effective and not too costly, can prevent or mitigate efficiency losses due to free-riding, but it does not specify how this effect depends on specific game parameters.

It notes that spatial structure promotes cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma but can reduce cooperation in the Snowdrift game. Indirect reciprocity, punishment, and reputation can promote cooperation, especially when individuals can condition their behavior on others' reputations. The review also discusses the generalization to public goods games and the impact of group size, voluntary participation, and continuous investment. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the frequency and stability of cooperation.

It suggests that punishment and reputation mechanisms can promote cooperation, and that spatial structure can have divergent effects depending on the game. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, it is not directly usable for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. Its value is mainly in informing mechanistic expectations and highlighting possible moderators, not in providing quantitative effect estimates.

  findings: Ostrom reviews and synthesizes experimental and field evidence on collective action, focusing on public goods and common-pool resource dilemmas. She identifies that communication and the ability to punish free riders both substantially increase cooperation rates in laboratory public goods games. The paper argues that real-world resource regimes that allow users to design their own rules, monitor each other, and impose graduated sanctions tend to be more sustainable and effective. However, the paper does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report new experimental data. Instead, it provides a conceptual framework for understanding how punishment, communication, and institutional design affect cooperation and the sustainability of collective action.

It suggests that punishment mechanisms, especially when locally designed and enforced, can help sustain cooperation and reduce free riding. However, the paper does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify the effects of particular game design dimensions in a way that would allow direct prediction of treatment efficiency from control efficiency and design features. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the inclusion of punishment and communication as positive moderators of cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence for efficiency outcomes or for the effect sizes associated with specific design parameters.

It highlights that uncertainty and irrational responses (e.g., loss aversion, framing effects) can inhibit cooperation. The authors review evidence that punishment and reward can both increase cooperation in public goods games, but that punishment is costly and may only be effective once cooperation is already established, while rewards may be more effective in early stages. Communication and information about others' actions also increase cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly, but suggests that these mechanisms can indirectly improve collective outcomes by increasing cooperation.

The review highlights that punishment is more effective when cooperation is already high, and that rewards may be more effective in low-cooperation settings. The paper is useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators but does not provide direct empirical input for efficiency prediction.

- source: Ohtsuki, H. (2004). Reactive strategies in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity settings with public-goods-game-type payoffs, discriminating strategies (which conditionally cooperate based on others' past behavior, akin to punishment of defectors) are not stable evolutionary outcomes. Instead, populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation (All C) or defection (All D), depending on whether the expected benefit of cooperation (modulated by information transfer and continuation probability) exceeds the cost. Discriminators can promote cooperation transiently by increasing the average 'discriminatoriness' in the population, but are ultimately outcompeted by more generous strategies. The paper also shows that errors (in perception or implementation) further destabilize discriminating strategies, making unconditional cooperation or defection more likely. The main outcomes are expressed in terms of average payoff (efficiency), and the model provides explicit threshold conditions for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-game-like environments with indirect reciprocity, strategies that punish defectors (by refusing to cooperate with them) do not lead to stable increases in group efficiency or payoff. Instead, such strategies are only transient promoters of cooperation, and populations tend to evolve toward unconditional cooperation or defection depending on the benefit-to-cost ratio and information transfer. The model does not include explicit costly punishment as in standard PGG experiments, so its direct predictive value for the effect of enabling punishment in a PGG is limited. However, it suggests that punishment-like strategies may not robustly increase efficiency unless additional mechanisms (e.g., standing, justified punishment) are present. For prediction tasks, this paper implies that simply enabling punishment (in the form of conditional cooperation) may not reliably increase efficiency, especially in the presence of errors.

- source: Castro, L., & Toro, M. A. (2008). Iterated prisoner's dilemma in an asocial world dominated by loners, not by defectors. *Theoretical Population Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated N-person Prisoner's Dilemma games, the presence of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary opt-out for a fixed payoff) changes the evolutionary dynamics: loners can displace defectors, and conditional cooperators (TFT0) can invade a population of loners if the benefit of cooperation is high enough and the number of repeated interactions is sufficient. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be established and maintained in sizable groups without the need for explicit punishment, provided that individuals can opt out. The paper does not model or test punishment directly, but discusses its potential role as an additional mechanism for sustaining cooperation in large groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that voluntary participation (the loner strategy) can enable high-efficiency outcomes in repeated public-goods-like games, even without punishment. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, as punishment is not modeled. The findings suggest that in environments where voluntary participation is possible, the need for punishment to sustain cooperation may be reduced. For prediction, this paper is most informative about the baseline efficiency in control games with voluntary participation, but not about the incremental effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Foster, K. R. (2004). Diminishing returns in social evolution: the not-so-tragic commons. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper develops and analyzes mathematical models of social evolution in group-structured populations, focusing on how the functional form of returns to investment (linear vs. diminishing) affects group performance (efficiency) in the face of selfish competition. It finds that, under diminishing returns, the negative impact of competition on group efficiency is greatly reduced, and group performance can be close to the optimum even with low relatedness and no explicit enforcement. The paper provides examples from social vertebrates, insects, and microbes, arguing that diminishing returns are common in real biological systems. Explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are not modeled, but enforcement is discussed as one of several mechanisms that can limit the tragedy of the commons.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of selfish competition in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the shape of the returns to investment. In prediction tasks, if the underlying game or biological system features diminishing returns to cooperation or competition, the efficiency loss from selfish behavior (and by extension, the potential efficiency gain from adding punishment) may be much smaller than predicted by linear models. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the paper does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of enabling punishment in a PGG. It does, however, suggest that in environments with diminishing returns, the baseline efficiency may already be high, potentially reducing the incremental value of punishment.

- source: Heintzelman, M. D., Salant, S. W., & Schott, S. (2009). Putting free-riding to work: A Partnership Solution to the common-property problem. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals form partnerships that share revenue equally, the resulting free-riding reduces excessive effort in common-property problems, potentially achieving the social optimum in aggregate payoff. The number of partnerships is a key design parameter: too many partnerships (everyone solo) leads to excessive effort, while too few (everyone in one group) leads to under-provision. An intermediate, endogenously determined number of partnerships can induce the efficient outcome. The model shows that this solution is stable under certain conditions (e.g., when solo production is sufficiently disadvantageous). The Partnership Solution can outperform standard regulatory interventions in terms of participant welfare. The paper generalizes the result to various sharing rules and to cases with heterogeneous agents.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional design—specifically, the structure of group formation and revenue sharing—can have large, predictable effects on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not study punishment per se, it shows that endogenous free-riding within partnerships can substitute for external sanctions in curbing over-extraction and maximizing welfare. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that efficiency can be increased by mechanisms that induce free-riding when the baseline is excessive effort, and that the number and size of groups (player_count, partnership structure) are critical moderators. However, the absence of explicit punishment means the results are most directly relevant to settings where group structure, not punishment, is the main intervention.

- source: Iwasa, Y., Uchida, T., & Yokomizo, H. (2007). Nonlinear behavior of the socio-economic dynamics for lake eutrophication control. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the interplay between social pressure (which increases with both the number of cooperators and the level of pollution) and individual cost-benefit decisions can lead to complex group-level outcomes. Depending on parameter values, the system can settle into a high-cooperation/clean-water equilibrium, a low-cooperation/polluted equilibrium, or exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics. The model shows that increasing the effectiveness of pollution-reducing technology can paradoxically worsen water quality if it reduces the perceived need for cooperation. Conservatism (slower behavioral change) can stabilize cooperation and maintain higher group welfare. The model does not include explicit punishment, but social pressure is discussed as a possible proxy for reputational or sanctioning effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how social feedbacks and behavioral dynamics can affect group efficiency in public-goods-like settings. While it does not directly model punishment, it suggests that mechanisms increasing social pressure (which could include punishment, reputation, or social norms) can promote cooperation and improve group outcomes, but may also lead to multiple stable states or complex dynamics. The findings support the idea that the structure and strength of social incentives (including, potentially, punishment) are critical moderators of efficiency. However, the absence of explicit punishment or experimental manipulation limits the direct applicability of the results to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in empirical PGGs.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taylor, P. D. (2010). Joint evolution of multiple social traits: a kin selection analysis. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that when social traits such as public good production and exploitation co-evolve, their interactions can lead to complex evolutionary dynamics, including bistability between efficient (social) and inefficient (asocial) outcomes. The efficiency of the group (measured as total payoff or welfare) depends on the joint equilibrium of these traits, which is sensitive to relatedness, the presence of independent public good production, and the nature of trait interactions (synergy or interference). In some cases, higher relatedness increases both cooperation and exploitation, with ambiguous effects on efficiency. The model highlights that multidimensional social dilemmas can have multiple stable outcomes, and small changes in parameters or initial conditions can lead to large differences in group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how the design dimensions of a public goods game—especially those affecting trait interactions, relatedness, and the possibility of multiple social traits—can lead to non-linear and sometimes counterintuitive effects on group efficiency when punishment-like or enforcement traits are present. While the model does not directly analyze punishment in the standard PGG sense, it shows that enforcement or exploitation traits can interact with cooperation to produce bistability or runaway dynamics. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment or enforcement mechanisms may not always increase efficiency; the effect depends on the interaction structure, initial conditions, and whether the system is in a region of parameter space that supports social or asocial equilibria. The findings caution against simple extrapolation from one-dimensional models and highlight the importance of considering multidimensional trait interactions.

- source: Panchanathan, K., & Boyd, R. (2004). Indirect reciprocity can stabilize cooperation without the second-order free rider problem. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that linking collective action (public goods contribution) to indirect reciprocity (mutual aid based on reputation) can stabilize cooperation through exclusion of non-contributors from future aid. This mechanism avoids the second-order free rider problem because punishers (Shunners) do not incur a direct cost; instead, they withhold aid from defectors, which is socially sanctioned. The model identifies conditions under which this strategy is evolutionarily stable and shows that both full cooperation (Shunner) and full defection (Defector) can be stable equilibria, depending on initial frequencies and assortment. The model also shows that strategies linking collective action and indirect reciprocity cannot easily invade populations practicing only indirect reciprocity. While the model discusses group payoff and welfare in the context of equilibrium selection, it does not report explicit efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that indirect punishment via exclusion (rather than direct costly punishment) can stabilize cooperation in public goods games, especially when reputation is visible and linked across domains. It suggests that such mechanisms can lead to higher group payoff and welfare, and that equilibrium selection processes (e.g., group selection, random drift) may favor these strategies. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment in a standard PGG. Its relevance is strongest for settings where punishment is implemented as exclusion from future benefits rather than direct payoff reduction. The model's insights are most applicable to predicting when and why punishment-like mechanisms (especially exclusion) might be effective, but less so for precise efficiency prediction in standard experimental PGGs with direct costly punishment.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2012). Risk-driven migration and the collective-risk social dilemma. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study shows that risk-driven migration, where players move in response to the risk of collective failure, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a collective-risk social dilemma. Cooperation is promoted most effectively at intermediate population densities and when the collective target is high and the risk function is not too flat. The mechanism outperforms random migration, which can actually inhibit cooperation. The results highlight the importance of spatial structure and self-organized mobility for sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how migration and risk of collective loss affect cooperation in public-goods-like environments, but it does not directly address the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms; instead, it uses a collective-risk loss as a sanction for group underperformance. Therefore, while the findings may inform predictions about the role of endogenous migration and risk in promoting cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency as required by the downstream prediction task. Use this paper for contextual or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Hauert, C. (2006). Spatial effects in social dilemmas. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that spatial structure can both promote and inhibit cooperation in social dilemmas, depending on the type of game (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift, by-product mutualism, coordination) and the microscopic update rule. In strict prisoner's dilemma or public goods settings, spatial structure allows cooperators to survive by forming clusters, but the parameter range for this effect is limited. In snowdrift-type games, spatial structure can actually reduce cooperation. The effect of spatial structure is highly sensitive to the update rule: the death-birth process is most favorable to cooperation, while the replicator rule can lead to reduced cooperation in some parameter regions. The main outcome is the equilibrium fraction of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it is informative about how spatial structure, group size, cost-benefit ratios, and update rules affect the equilibrium level of cooperation in group social dilemmas. If the prediction task involves spatial structure or local interactions, the findings suggest that such structure can have complex, sometimes counterintuitive effects on cooperation, which may indirectly affect efficiency. The absence of punishment as a mechanism means the paper cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The main value is in understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatially structured games.

- source: Billard, E. A. (1996). Evolutionary strategies of stochastic learning automata in the prisoner's dilemma. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in resource dilemmas (including public goods and commons dilemmas) is shaped by individual value orientation (pro-social vs egoistic), situational constraints (such as group identification, communication, and social norms), and the degree and type of uncertainty (social and environmental). It proposes that pro-social individuals are more likely to cooperate regardless of uncertainty, while egoistic individuals are more likely to defect, especially under uncertainty. Situational factors (including the possibility of sanctions or rewards) can constrain egoism and promote cooperation. The paper reviews prior findings that rewards increase cooperation and sanctions decrease defection, but notes that the costs of such interventions may outweigh collective gains and that effects may not persist when interventions are removed. The analysis is theoretical and hypothesis-generating, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on the psychological mechanisms that may moderate the effect of punishment, reward, and uncertainty on cooperation in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but may not always improve efficiency due to their costs, and that their effects may not persist. The paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of situational constraints (including punishment/reward), group identification, communication, and uncertainty, and in warning that interventions may have costs that offset their benefits. However, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Salomonsson, M. (2010). Group selection: The quest for social preferences. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that group selection can, under certain conditions, support the evolution of cooperation and altruism in public-goods-like and Prisoner's Dilemma games. Three main approaches are discussed: fixation (requiring isolated pure groups), assortative group formation (e.g., via conformity or signalling), and reproductive externalities (where group-level payoffs affect evolutionary success). The paper notes that group selection is more likely to support cooperation when migration is low, groups are small, or positive externalities can be internalized. It also reviews how cultural mechanisms (like conformity) and group competition can foster social preferences. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, and only discusses punishment as a possible mechanism in the context of conformity and social preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how group structure, migration, and mechanisms like conformity or signalling can affect the evolution of cooperation in public-goods-like games. It is useful for understanding the broader conditions under which cooperation and social preferences may arise, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that group structure and the ability to internalize externalities are important moderators, but it does not inform the specific effect of punishment interventions in experimental public goods games.

- source: Santos, F. C., Vasconcelos, V. V., Santos, M. D., Neves, P. N. B., & Pacheco, J. M. (2012). EVOLUTIONARY DYNAMICS OF CLIMATE CHANGE UNDER COLLECTIVE-RISK DILEMMAS. *Mathematical Models & Methods in Applied Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in collective-risk dilemmas (a variant of public goods games with a threshold and risk of collective loss), cooperation and group efficiency are maximized in small groups with high risk and stringent requirements for success. The evolutionary dynamics show that risk is a key driver for cooperation: without risk, defection dominates, but with increasing risk, stable coexistence or dominance of cooperation can emerge. The model provides analytical and simulation results for both infinite and finite populations, showing how group size, risk, and threshold interact to determine the likelihood of cooperation and the average payoff. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting efficiency in threshold public goods games with collective risk, but it does not address the effect of punishment. It provides strong theoretical evidence that group size, risk level, and threshold requirements are key moderators of efficiency and cooperation. However, since punishment is not modeled, the paper does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. Its results are most relevant for understanding baseline efficiency in collective-risk settings and for interpreting the effects of group size, risk, and threshold, but not for the incremental effect of punishment.

- source: Song, Q. Q., Li, Z. P., Fu, C. H., & Wang, L. S. (2011). Optional contributions have positive effects for volunteering public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing an option for low contributions in volunteering public goods games (VPG) increases the average payoff of the population (i.e., efficiency), compared to the standard VPG with only a single contribution level. This effect is observed in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations, and is especially pronounced when the multiplication factor (r) is above a threshold that depends weakly on group size. The mechanism is that the low-contribution option induces more players to cooperate, which in turn increases the number of high contributors as well, leading to higher overall payoffs. The results are based on theoretical analysis and extensive simulations of evolutionary dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in VPG-like public goods games, allowing optional (especially low) contributions can increase group efficiency compared to a single, higher contribution requirement. While the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, it is highly informative for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the contribution structure (e.g., all-or-nothing vs. continuous, or optional vs. required). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, introducing more flexible contribution options (even without punishment) can increase efficiency, especially when the MPCR is above a moderate threshold. However, the paper does not address the effects of punishment, so it is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Bach, L. A., Helvik, T., & Christiansen, F. B. (2006). The evolution of n-player cooperation - threshold games and ESS bifurcations. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in n-player threshold public goods games, the existence and stability of cooperative equilibria depend critically on the cost-benefit ratio and the threshold required for the collective benefit. For certain parameter regions, both cooperative and defecting equilibria can coexist, and small changes in parameters can lead to sudden transitions (catastrophes) in group efficiency and cooperation. The model predicts hysteresis: once a population falls into full defection, simply restoring favorable parameters may not be sufficient to recover cooperation. The analysis provides explicit mathematical conditions for these transitions and demonstrates that such complex dynamics are only possible in games with at least three players. No punishment or reward mechanisms are included.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how group size, threshold structure, and cost-benefit parameters affect the efficiency and stability of cooperation in public-goods-like games without punishment. It is directly relevant for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in threshold public goods games, especially when the benefit is all-or-nothing and depends on a minimum number of cooperators. However, it does not inform the effect of punishment or sanctions, as these mechanisms are not modeled. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for understanding how efficiency varies with game design in the absence of punishment, but provides no evidence on how enabling punishment would change efficiency.

- source: Brown, S. P., & Taddei, F. (2007). The Durability of Public Goods Changes the Dynamics and Nature of Social Dilemmas. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that when the public good is durable (i.e., changes slowly relative to the frequency of cooperators), the dynamics of cooperation and group payoff can be substantially altered compared to classic models. For example, durable public goods can lead to multi-generational oscillations in cooperation and payoff, shift the thresholds for invasion and stability of cooperation, and even allow for transient increases in cooperation in settings where defection would otherwise dominate. The explicit modeling of public good dynamics reveals that group payoff and efficiency are not solely determined by current cooperation rates, but also by the history and durability of the public good. However, the paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how the dynamics and durability of the public good itself can affect efficiency and group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It highlights that efficiency is not always a simple function of current cooperation rates, especially when the public good is durable or slow to change. However, because the paper does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most relevant for understanding baseline dynamics and for considering how lagged or durable public goods might interact with other interventions in future models.

- source: Hadzibeganovic, T., Lima, F. W. S., & Stauffer, D. (2012). Evolution of tag-mediated altruistic behavior in one-shot encounters on large-scale complex networks. *Computer Physics Communications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that tag-mediated cooperation can emerge and temporarily dominate in one-shot, multiplayer PD-like games on complex networks, especially when agents can recognize and preferentially help those with similar tags. The dominance of specific cooperative strategies (altruist vs. ethnocentric) depends on network topology (rewiring probability), but over long timescales, all strategies tend to equilibrate. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperation and strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how tag-based recognition and network structure can support or suppress cooperation in the absence of punishment, but does not address payoff-based outcomes or the impact of sanctions. It may provide weak contextual information about baseline cooperation in one-shot, multiplayer PD-like environments without punishment, but is not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Janus, T., & Lim, J. J. (2009). Sticks and carrots: Two incentive mechanisms supporting intra-group cooperation. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper proves that in a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma, the introduction of either collective punishment (Nash reversion by all after any defection) or reward mechanisms (transfers contingent on cooperation) can sustain full cooperation as a subgame perfect equilibrium, even when bilateral incentive compatibility constraints are violated. This means that group efficiency (welfare) can be maintained at the cooperative level under broader conditions than in the absence of such mechanisms. The results are general and apply to settings where players interact repeatedly and can observe each other's actions, and where the game is either infinite or has an unknown end point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (or reward) mechanisms in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency by sustaining cooperation, even when cooperation would not be sustainable without such mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially collective punishment with Nash reversion) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, provided players are sufficiently patient (high discount factor) and can observe actions. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma), so the results are most directly applicable to small-group, repeated, fully observable settings. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes or parameterized predictions, but it does establish that the direction of effect is positive for efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: ELLISON, G. (1994). COOPERATION IN THE PRISONERS-DILEMMA WITH ANONYMOUS RANDOM MATCHING. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that contagious punishment strategies can sustain cooperation and near-efficient payoffs in large, anonymous, randomly-matched repeated prisoner's dilemma games. The equilibrium is robust to small amounts of noise (players making mistakes) and does not require excessive patience except in very large populations or when the gain from defection is very small. The efficiency loss due to noise can be made arbitrarily small as the noise probability decreases. The results hold even without public randomization devices, though the required patience may increase in some parameter regimes. The analysis provides explicit conditions (in terms of discount factor, population size, and gain from defection) under which efficient cooperation is possible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that punishment mechanisms (here, contagious punishment) can enable high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even under severe informational constraints (anonymity, random matching, no identification, no communication). The results are most directly applicable to repeated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that, for games with similar structure and sufficient patience, enabling punishment can substantially increase efficiency relative to the baseline (no punishment) case. The paper also provides explicit formulas and numerical examples for the minimum patience (discount factor) required for cooperation at different population sizes and temptation levels. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that punishment can enable near-full efficiency in repeated, anonymous, randomly-matched games, provided the population is not too large, the gain from defection is not too high, and players are sufficiently patient. The results are robust to small noise and do not require public randomization, though the patience requirement may increase in those cases.

- source: Haag, M., & Lagunoff, R. (2006). Social norms, local interaction, and neighborhood planning. *International Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of repeated cooperation enforced by punishment depends critically on the network structure and the distribution of individual patience (discount factors). In maximally connected cliques, punishment is most effective due to perfect monitoring, and full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) is sustainable if all individuals are sufficiently patient. However, when some individuals are impatient (low discount factor), cliques can lead to cascades of defection and lower welfare. In such cases, less connected networks (e.g., grids) can isolate the effects of uncooperative types, allowing more tolerant individuals to absorb free riding and maintain higher overall welfare. The optimal network design thus balances the benefits of effective punishment (which requires connectivity and observability) against the costs of social conflict from connecting intolerant and uncooperative types. The paper provides explicit formulas for expected welfare under different network designs and type distributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in repeated local public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma environments as a function of network structure, monitoring, and heterogeneity in patience. It shows that punishment is most effective (and efficiency is highest) in small, maximally connected groups with perfect monitoring and homogeneous patience. As heterogeneity increases, or as monitoring becomes more local/imperfect, the efficiency gains from punishment can be reduced or even reversed, with less connected networks sometimes yielding higher welfare. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by network connectivity, monitoring structure, and the distribution of patience among players. However, the results are theoretical and assume rational, forward-looking agents using trigger strategies; they do not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Lippert, S., & Spagnolo, G. (2011). Networks of relations and Word-of-Mouth Communication. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in networks of repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games, the ability to transmit information (word-of-mouth) and the use of forgiving, targeted punishment strategies (Multilateral Repentance) allow for cooperation to be sustained under a wider range of conditions (i.e., lower discount factors) than under traditional contagion (Grim Trigger) strategies. This leads to higher efficiency and welfare, especially during punishment phases, as only defectors are punished and cooperation among others continues. The structure of the network (closure, density) and the speed of information transmission further moderate these effects. The results are formalized in terms of equilibrium payoff conditions and sustainability criteria.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling targeted, forgiving punishment (as opposed to all-or-nothing breakdowns) and facilitating information transmission (communication) in networked repeated social dilemmas increases the efficiency of cooperation. The results suggest that, all else equal, punishment regimes that are more targeted and allow for communication will yield higher efficiency than those that rely on contagious, permanent breakdowns. The paper also highlights the importance of network structure (closure, density) and information speed, which may inform how design dimensions like communication, punishment technology, and information feedback affect efficiency. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes.

- source: Langlois, C. C., & Langlois, J. P. P. (2004). Provisions for noncompliance and treaty value: A game theoretic perspective. *International Studies Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with possible unintentional noncompliance (noise), the design of punishment schemes has a large effect on long-run efficiency (treaty value). Probabilistic trigger schemes, which are analogous to mutual punishment phases after a deviation, are self-punishing and reduce efficiency because both the perpetrator and the victim suffer during punishment. Contrite tit-for-tat (CTFT) schemes, which allow for redress (compensation to the victim) and delay retaliation (generosity), achieve much higher efficiency by minimizing the time spent in mutual defection and ensuring that only the guilty party is punished. The paper provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for how efficiency declines with noise under each scheme, and shows that CTFT dominates trigger schemes in most of the relevant parameter space.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of the punishment scheme (especially whether punishment is self-punishing or allows for redress) is a critical moderator of the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated social dilemma games. If the punishment mechanism is costly to the punisher and involves mutual punishment phases (as in standard trigger schemes), efficiency is substantially reduced, especially as noise increases. If the punishment mechanism allows for redress (compensation to the victim) and avoids mutual punishment (as in CTFT), efficiency is much higher. The paper also shows that the speed of retaliation (trigger probability) and the expected duration of punishment phases are important: swifter, shorter punishments are more efficient, but only if they still deter defection. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment based on the punishment technology, cost structure, and whether redress is possible.

- source: Hilbe, C., & Sigmund, K. (2010). Incentives and opportunism: from the carrot to the stick. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that negative incentives (punishment) can stabilize cooperation and lead to higher average payoffs (efficiency) in the population, provided that players have enough information about others' reputations. Rewarding (positive incentives) is not stable in the long run but is important for catalyzing the initial emergence of cooperation, especially when information is limited. When both punishment and reward are available, the population transitions from defection to cooperation more quickly, and the stable outcome is a cooperative, high-payoff equilibrium enforced by punishment. The results are robust to population size and mutation structure, and the authors discuss how the findings generalize to n-person public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency (average group payoff), especially when players can observe or infer others' incentive-providing behavior. The model suggests that the effect of punishment is more pronounced when information about others is available, and that the presence of reward mechanisms can accelerate the transition to cooperation but does not affect the long-run efficiency as much as punishment does. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, two-stage games but are argued to generalize to n-person PGGs. For prediction tasks, the paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when information/reputation is high, and suggests that the marginal benefit of adding reward is mainly in catalyzing cooperation rather than sustaining higher efficiency in the long run.

- source: Nakao, K. (2009). CREATION OF SOCIAL ORDER IN ETHNIC CONFLICT. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of punishment mechanisms (peer monitoring, collective peer punishment, or ethnic conflict) can sustain higher group efficiency (expected payoffs) compared to no punishment, but only if in-group monitoring is sufficiently efficient and not too costly. When monitoring is inefficient or costly, or when noise (misinterpretation of actions) is high, punishment can reduce efficiency due to frequent erroneous punishment or high monitoring costs. The model provides comparative statics showing that efficiency is maximized with efficient peer monitoring and high tolerance, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the quality of in-group policing, the length of conflict, and the density of inter-group interactions. The model predicts that groups with high-quality in-group policing achieve higher efficiency and more stable cooperation, while groups with low-quality policing suffer more frequent and longer conflicts, reducing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games depends critically on the efficiency and cost of monitoring and punishment. If the game design allows for efficient, low-cost peer monitoring (low punishment cost, high punishment tech), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control. If monitoring is costly or ineffective, punishment can reduce efficiency due to high costs or frequent erroneous punishment. The model also shows that the effect of punishment is moderated by noise (misinterpretation), group size, and the structure of interactions. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency only when monitoring is efficient and not too costly, and that the marginal effect of punishment is lower or negative when monitoring is costly or noise is high. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so findings should be transferred with caution.

- source: Matsushima, H. (2012). FINITELY REPEATED PRISONERS' DILEMMA WITH SMALL FINES: THE PENANCE CONTRACT. *Japanese Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that with a suitable contract that imposes a fine for deviation in the final period, full cooperation (i.e., maximal efficiency) can be uniquely sustained in finitely repeated prisoners' dilemma games. The required fine can be small relative to total payoffs if the game is long enough. The penance contract is robust to renegotiation, unlike trigger contracts. The result generalizes to other two-player games with similar payoff structure. The findings are theoretical and show that the design of the punishment mechanism (timing, size, and contingency) is crucial for achieving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in two-player finitely repeated social dilemma games, the introduction of a well-designed punishment mechanism (specifically, a contract that imposes a fine for final-period deviation) can move the group from inefficient (all-defect) equilibria to fully efficient (all-cooperate) equilibria. The effect is robust to renegotiation if the punishment is contingent on final-period behavior and the fine is large enough to outweigh the gain from deviation. The result is directly relevant for predicting that enabling such a punishment mechanism will increase efficiency to the maximum possible, provided the contract is enforceable and the fine is sufficiently large. However, the result is limited to two-player, finitely repeated, contractible games and does not address larger groups or public goods games directly.

- source: Skatova, A., & Ferguson, E. (2011). What makes people cooperate? Individual differences in BAS/BIS predict strategic reciprocation in a public goods game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with super cooperators (who both punish defectors and reward cooperators), the ability of speculative defectors to identify and cooperate with super cooperators (i.e., higher information level) generally increases the prevalence of super cooperators and raises average social payoff (efficiency), provided the cost of punishment/reward is not too high. When the cost is high, increasing information can instead destroy super cooperators. The alliance between speculative defectors and super cooperators can insulate super cooperators from exploitation by unconditional cooperators, further supporting cooperation and efficiency. The model demonstrates that the introduction of punishment/reward mechanisms and information about others' strategies can substantially improve group efficiency compared to the baseline with only unconditional cooperators and defectors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatially structured prisoner's dilemma environments, enabling punishment (and reward) mechanisms—especially when paired with higher information about others' strategies—can increase group efficiency, as measured by average social payoff. The effect is strongest when the cost of punishment/reward is moderate or low. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in games with similar structure, though the model is not a standard public goods game but a spatial PD with three strategies. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient information) should increase efficiency relative to the control. However, if the cost of punishment/reward is high, the effect can reverse. The model's focus on spatial structure, information, and cost of punishment/reward provides indirect but informative guidance for analogous PGG-like environments.

- source: Stahl, D. O. (2011). Cooperation in the sporadically repeated prisoners' dilemma via reputation mechanisms. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in the SRPD, only a small subset of reputation mechanisms can sustain cooperation and achieve efficient outcomes. Specifically, mechanisms that distinguish between justified punishment and defection, and allow for penitence, can support equilibria where the long-run group payoff matches the fully cooperative outcome. The analysis shows that, with these mechanisms and high enough continuation probability, the population evolves toward full cooperation and efficiency. In contrast, most reputation mechanisms result in universal defection and zero efficiency. The paper also extends the analysis to clan-based (group) settings, showing that the efficiency result generalizes when within-group matching is sufficiently likely.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the structure of the punishment/reputation mechanism is a critical moderator of the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. If the punishment mechanism is designed to avoid penalizing justified punishers and allows for penitence, and if the continuation probability is high, then enabling punishment can move efficiency from zero (universal defection) to the full-cooperation benchmark. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the details of the punishment/reputation update rule and the effective 'delta' (continuation probability) are crucial for forecasting the efficiency impact of punishment. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, anonymous-matching games with public reputations, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like settings with similar reputation mechanisms.

- source: Corriveau, L. (2012). Game theory and the kula. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a repeated, circular n-player game (the 'game of kula'), the unique Nash equilibrium of the one-shot game is inefficient (all defect). However, when the game is repeated indefinitely and players are sufficiently patient, efficient (fully cooperative) equilibria can be sustained by social norms that attach punishment to deviations. The model formalizes three types of punishment-based norms: direct reciprocity (punished by the harmed party), indirect reciprocity (punished by a third party), and mixed reciprocity (punished by both). The mixed norm is shown to be the most robust, existing under the widest range of parameters. The analysis is entirely theoretical, but it provides formal conditions under which punishment enables efficient cooperation, and shows that the presence of punishment strategies can transform an inefficient game into an efficient one.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in repeated, multi-player social dilemma games (with circular or networked structure) can increase efficiency, provided players are sufficiently patient and punishment is credible. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the results generalize the repeated prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is possible and observable, and where players have high discount factors, the introduction of punishment mechanisms can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (defection) to efficient (cooperation), thus increasing group payoff. The paper also clarifies that the structure of punishment (direct, indirect, mixed) affects the robustness of cooperation, with mixed norms being most robust. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially as the number of players increases and as the possibility of both direct and indirect punishment exists.

- source: Tao, J., Zhong, X., & Lu, Y. F. (2011). Restrictive mechanism of flow control among non-cooperative Internet users. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated flow control games, the use of punishment strategies (including trigger, tit-for-tat, and finite punishment) can deter selfish deviations and sustain cooperation among users, leading to optimal or near-optimal system utility. In the absence of such mechanisms, the equilibrium is inefficient. The FCAR algorithm, which operationalizes finite punishment, is shown via simulation to effectively regularize user behavior and maintain high system utility, with only temporary utility losses during punishment. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the number of users and the length of punishment. In finitely repeated games, cooperation is harder to sustain, especially in the final stage, but rational punishment strategies can still maintain cooperation for most of the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in repeated resource allocation games (adjacent to public goods games) increases efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to the number of users, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong and the game is sufficiently long or indefinite. The findings support the prediction that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will increase average efficiency, especially when users are patient (high discount factor) and punishment is coordinated. The results are most directly applicable to repeated, continuous-action, non-anonymous settings with full information about others' actions and payoffs.

- source: Bendor, J., & Swistak, P. (2001). The evolution of norms. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated games of enforceable cooperation, only social norms (strategies that prescribe third-party punishment) can be evolutionarily stable. The presence of effective punishment is necessary for stability. Both efficient and inefficient social norms can be stable, but efficient (cooperative) norms are more robust to invasion and require a lower frequency to stabilize. The model predicts that, over time, groups are more likely to drift toward efficient, cooperative equilibria regulated by social norms, but inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria are also possible and robust. The findings are general for two-person repeated games and extend to sparse networks, provided third-party information is sufficiently available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the necessity of punishment (sanctions) for stable cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. It shows that the presence of punishment mechanisms is a necessary condition for the evolutionary stability of cooperative, efficient outcomes, but does not guarantee efficiency: inefficient, punishment-backed equilibria can also be stable. However, more efficient equilibria are more robust to invasion and require a lower threshold to stabilize. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in a repeated game with enforceable cooperation will generally increase the likelihood of stable cooperation and higher efficiency, but the actual efficiency achieved may depend on the specific norm that evolves. The robustness of efficient equilibria to invasion is higher, so in the long run, efficiency is more likely with punishment enabled, but inefficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to two-person repeated games, but the logic extends to larger groups and sparse networks if third-party information is available.

- source: dos Santos, M., Rankin, D. J., & Wedekind, C. (2011). The evolution of punishment through reputation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that punishment, when reputationally visible, can evolve and be stable because it increases the likelihood of receiving help in the future, offsetting its immediate cost. Analytical and simulation results show that punishing discriminators (who punish defectors and are recognized for it) achieve higher payoffs and can dominate, leading to high cooperation and group efficiency. The effect is robust to a range of costs and benefits, but is sensitive to the accuracy of reputation information and the mutation rate. The findings suggest that reputation is a key moderator of punishment's efficiency-enhancing effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the efficiency gains from punishment depend critically on whether punishment is reputationally visible and can influence future interactions. In environments where reputation for punishing is tracked and used by others to condition their cooperation, punishment can increase group efficiency and stabilize cooperation. The results are robust across a range of costs and benefits, but efficiency gains are reduced if reputation information is noisy or mutation rates are low. For prediction, the presence of a reputation system that makes punishment visible is a key moderator for positive efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Saak, A. E. (2012). Collective Reputation, Social Norms, and Participation. *American Journal of Agricultural Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that collective reputation mechanisms with local peer punishment can sustain higher efficiency (average payoffs, welfare) than individual reputation, as long as group size is not too small or too large and peer monitoring is present. Peer punishment deters shirking and allows for softer public punishment, increasing the duration of efficient cooperation. The optimal group size increases with the discount factor, the informativeness of public signals, and the quality premium, but decreases with the relative gain from shirking. Without peer monitoring, collective reputation is less efficient than individual reputation. Perfect global peer monitoring allows for arbitrarily large efficient groups. Mandatory traceability (which enables individual punishment) can reduce welfare by undermining group-based peer punishment. The model provides clear comparative statics and welfare implications for the design of collective brands and group-based sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling peer punishment (local or global) in repeated group settings with imperfect public monitoring increases efficiency and group payoffs, compared to settings without such punishment. The model is not a literal public goods game, but is adjacent: it is a repeated moral hazard game with group-level payoffs, endogenous peer punishment, and collective reputation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment mechanisms (especially peer punishment) on efficiency, conditional on group size, monitoring structure, and information. The paper also provides comparative statics on how efficiency gains from punishment depend on group size, monitoring, and information, which can inform predictions about moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Thomadsen, R., & Bhardwaj, P. (2011). Cooperation in Games with Forgetfulness. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of forgetfulness on efficiency in repeated games with punishment is fundamentally ambiguous: it can either reduce or increase the set of discount factors and game parameters under which full cooperation (and thus maximum efficiency) is sustainable. When the stage game is such that the cooperative action always yields nonnegative payoffs, forgetfulness weakens punishment and makes cooperation harder to sustain, reducing efficiency. However, when the stage game allows for the possibility that a forgetful player can be punished below the minimax, forgetfulness can make severe punishments credible and thus expand the range of parameters supporting cooperation, increasing efficiency. The paper provides explicit formulas and graphical illustrations for these effects in the repeated prisoner's dilemma and a franchising effort game. The results are theoretical and depend on the structure of the stage game and the probability of forgetfulness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games is not monotonic and can be moderated by cognitive or institutional features like forgetfulness. In environments where punishment is only as severe as the stage minimax, introducing forgetfulness will generally reduce the efficiency gains from punishment. However, in environments where forgetfulness allows for below-minimax punishments, punishment can become more effective at sustaining cooperation, potentially increasing efficiency beyond what is possible with perfect recall. For prediction tasks, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined solely from standard game design dimensions; one must also consider whether the environment allows for forgetfulness and whether the stage game payoffs permit below-minimax punishments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates but gives clear theoretical boundaries and mechanisms.

- source: Evans, R., & Thomas, J. P. (2001). Cooperation and punishment. *Econometrica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated common interest games (with no discounting), the mere presence of cooperative (nondraconian) strategies in the set of possible strategies does not guarantee efficient (Pareto-optimal) outcomes in equilibrium. However, if the set of possible strategies includes at least one 'draconian' strategy (i.e., a strategy that is willing to punish noncooperation to the maximum extent, such as min-maxing the opponent forever after a deviation), then all Nash equilibria are efficient. The paper also shows that this result is robust to certain computability restrictions and discusses the limits of these results under discounting and other technical assumptions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in repeated games with a structure similar to public goods or common interest games, the availability of sufficiently severe punishment (i.e., the ability to impose maximal punishment at low or no cost) is both necessary and sufficient for punishment to induce efficient outcomes in equilibrium. If the game design allows for draconian punishment strategies, then enabling punishment should lead to high efficiency, regardless of the control efficiency. If only mild or 'cooperative' punishment is possible, efficiency gains are not guaranteed. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, infinite-horizon, undiscounted games, but the logic is relevant for understanding the role of punishment severity and credibility in other settings.

- source: Okamoto, K., & Matsumura, S. (2000). The evolution of punishment and apology: an iterated prisoner's dilemma model. *Evolutionary Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that strategies incorporating both punishment and apology signals (especially the Pavlov-like pPAS strategy) can maintain high levels of cooperation and average payoffs in the iterated prisoner's dilemma, even in the presence of errors and deceptive strategies. The average payoff in a pPAS-dominated population is nearly as high as in a fully cooperative population, and much higher than in populations dominated by tit-for-tat (TFT) or defectors. The success of pPAS depends on the costs of punishment and apology: pPAS dominates when the cost of giving punishment is low, the cost of receiving punishment is high, or the cost of giving apology is high. The model also shows that pPAS can remain stable even when there is a dominance asymmetry in costs between players. The findings suggest that punishment and apology mechanisms can evolve to support efficient cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated social dilemmas with error, the availability of punishment (and apology) mechanisms can substantially increase group efficiency, provided the costs of punishment and apology are within certain bounds. The results are most directly applicable to iterated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent but not identical to public goods games. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should expect a positive effect if the punishment cost is not too high, the punishment is effective, and the apology cost is not too low (to prevent deception). The paper also highlights the importance of considering the cost structure of punishment and apology when making such predictions. However, the results are based on theoretical models and evolutionary simulations, not empirical experiments.

- source: Amor, D. R., & Fort, J. (2011). Effects of punishment in a mobile population playing the prisoner's dilemma game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
