  findings: The paper finds that introducing social punishment (where cooperators punish defectors with a small probability) in a mobile PD population can transform a system that would otherwise end in universal defection into one of universal cooperation, thereby maximizing group payoff. Even a 10% probability of social punishment is sufficient in many parameter regimes. The effect is robust to some degree of nonsocial punishment, but as nonsocial punishment increases, cooperation and mean payoff decline. The paper provides explicit analytical predictions and simulation results for mean payoff in the final state, showing that social punishment increases efficiency (mean payoff), while nonsocial punishment reduces it. The effect of punishment is moderated by mobility and temptation to defect: higher values of these parameters require higher rates of social punishment to sustain cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in pairwise PD games with mobile agents, enabling social punishment (even at low rates) can dramatically increase group efficiency (mean payoff), often shifting the system from all-defection (zero payoff) to all-cooperation (maximum payoff). The effect is robust to moderate levels of nonsocial punishment, but high nonsocial punishment or harsh environments (high mobility, high temptation to defect) can negate the benefits. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs (pairwise PD, mobile or spatially structured populations, punishment with cost:impact ratio of 1:3), enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. However, the results are for pairwise PD, not n-person public goods games, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs should be cautious, though qualitative patterns are likely similar.

- source: Leimar, O. (1997). Repeated games: A state space approach. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games like the IPD and dominance games, the ability to punish can support the evolutionary stability of cooperative strategies, leading to higher efficiency (i.e., higher expected payoffs, closer to full cooperation) when the probability of continuation is high and punishment is sufficiently severe and/or effective. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is parameter-dependent: if the probability of continuation is too low or punishment is too costly/ineffective, cooperation cannot be sustained and efficiency is low. The analysis also shows that there are many possible stable strategies, some of which are more efficient than others, and that the structure of punishment (e.g., how many rounds of mutual defection follow a defection) affects the range of stability and efficiency. In dominance games, punishment by a dominant can enforce resource control and higher efficiency for the dominant, but alternative equilibria exist where the subdominant prevails. In alternating PD, reciprocal strategies can be stable and efficient if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high enough. The findings are theoretical and based on formal analysis of payoffs and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games depends on game design dimensions such as the probability of continuation (analogous to number of rounds), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the structure of the game (simultaneous vs. alternating moves, role asymmetry). It shows that punishment can increase efficiency by supporting cooperation, but only when parameters are favorable (e.g., high continuation probability, effective punishment). The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, one should expect punishment to improve efficiency in repeated games when the game is long enough and punishment is not too costly or weak, but that the effect can be neutral or negative if these conditions are not met. The paper also highlights that many different punishment strategies can be stable, so the specific implementation of punishment matters. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in repeated, two-player, payoff-based games with punishment.

- source: Aramendia, M. (2006). Asymmetric finite punishments in repeated games. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in repeated games, including social dilemmas and Cournot oligopoly, AFP strategies—where punishment is finite and asymmetric—can sustain any feasible and individually rational payoff profile when players are sufficiently patient. The punishment phase is designed so that nondeviators are always better off than the deviator, and after a finite number of periods, cooperation resumes. This approach is less restrictive than some standard equilibrium refinements and can support efficient outcomes (e.g., cartel profits) even in cases where other concepts (like WRP) cannot. The results are illustrated with examples including the prisoner's dilemma and Cournot oligopoly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that finite, asymmetric punishment strategies can sustain efficient, cooperative outcomes in repeated games, even when standard trigger or minimax punishments are not credible or too harsh. For the prediction task, it suggests that the design of the punishment phase (finite duration, asymmetry favoring nondeviators) can be critical for supporting high efficiency, especially as the number of players increases or when renegotiation-proofness is problematic. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide direct quantitative predictions for specific experimental public goods games, but they do indicate that efficiency can be maintained with appropriately designed punishment even in large groups or complex environments.

- source: Isakov, A., & Rand, D. G. (2012). The Evolution of Coercive Institutional Punishment. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment can evolve and be sustained when leaders (kings) can punish subjects for low tribute, especially when subjects are intelligent (accurate learners) and update their strategies quickly relative to kings. Strong punishment leads to high tribute payments but reduces subject payoffs and overall efficiency, except in the idealized case where punishment is never actually used. The model predicts that confusion or slow learning among subjects can collectively benefit them by undermining the effectiveness of coercive punishment. Increased global awareness (subjects learning from other kingdoms) destabilizes coercion and can collapse institutional punishment. The model is robust to changes in population size and punishment strength bounds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional punishment, when used coercively in asymmetric settings, can increase compliance (tribute payment) but generally reduces group efficiency and aggregate payoffs compared to non-punishment or noncoercive equilibria. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that enabling punishment in similar asymmetric games will likely decrease efficiency unless compliance is perfect and punishment is never actually used. The effects are moderated by learning dynamics (intelligence, update speed) and the degree of information subjects have about other groups. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is imposed by a central authority and the 'public good' is tribute to that authority, rather than a symmetric PGG.

- source: Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A., & Sadedin, S. (2012). Evolving Righteousness in a Corrupt World. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a small benefit to punishers (such as status or a payment from cooperators) destabilizes corrupt equilibria and enables the evolution and stability of 'righteousness'—a state where cooperative punishers dominate and both corruption and defection are suppressed. Righteousness is associated with higher collective payoffs (efficiency/wellbeing) than either corruption or defection. The model demonstrates that egalitarian and harsh punishment (i.e., similar and sufficiently high penalties for defectors and corrupt punishers) are key to achieving righteousness. Power inequalities that favor corrupt punishers stabilize corruption, but removing or reducing these inequalities (and/or providing small benefits to punishers) can shift the population to righteousness. Once established, righteousness is highly stable and resistant to invasion by defectors or corrupt punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling punishment (especially when punishers receive even small additional benefits) can increase group efficiency/wellbeing compared to control conditions without punishment or with only corrupt punishment. The model suggests that the design dimensions most relevant for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment are: (1) ensuring punishers are not disadvantaged relative to cooperators (e.g., via small payments or status), (2) making punishment of defectors and corrupt punishers sufficiently harsh, and (3) minimizing power inequalities that allow corrupt punishers to avoid punishment. While the model is not an exact PGG, its structure and outcomes are highly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games, especially regarding the role of corruption, punishment severity, and benefits to punishers.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., Iwasa, Y., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Indirect reciprocity provides only a narrow margin of efficiency for costly punishment. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper shows that in models of indirect reciprocity, strategies that use costly punishment (punishing defectors at a cost to oneself) are almost always less efficient than strategies that simply withhold cooperation from defectors. Costly punishment can only be the most efficient equilibrium in a very small region of parameter space, requiring fine-tuned values of benefit, cost, punishment cost, punishment impact, and reputation accuracy. In most cases, the highest group payoff (efficiency) is achieved by not using costly punishment. The results are robust to errors in reputation assignment and action execution, as long as these errors are not too frequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity settings (which are adjacent to public goods games), enabling costly punishment will almost always reduce group efficiency compared to not enabling punishment, except in a very narrow set of parameter values. For prediction tasks, this suggests that unless the game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment impact, benefit/cost ratio, reputation accuracy) are in the narrow region identified, enabling punishment should be expected to lower efficiency. The findings are most directly applicable to indirect reciprocity games with binary reputation and second-order norms, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar structure.

- source: Annen, K. (2011). Lies and slander: truth-telling in repeated matching games with private monitoring. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement mechanisms, where players can report on others' behavior and the group can punish defectors, can support efficient cooperation in repeated matching games with private monitoring. When experience-sharing (reporting) is exogenous and truthful, cooperation can be supported for any group size, and the minimal discount factor required does not depend on the number of players. When experience-sharing is endogenous (players choose what to report), personal punishments are needed to incentivize truth-telling, which reduces the severity of community punishment and increases the minimal discount factor required for cooperation. The ability to sustain efficient cooperation thus depends on the number of players and the matching rule in the endogenous case, but for large enough groups, community enforcement remains more effective than personal enforcement alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling community punishment (via experience-sharing/reporting) increases the efficiency of repeated matching games with private monitoring, especially in large groups. However, the efficiency gain is moderated by the need to incentivize truthful reporting, which introduces personal punishments that reduce the overall effectiveness of community enforcement. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is more positive in larger groups and when experience-sharing is exogenous or can be made truthful at low cost. When experience-sharing is endogenous, the efficiency gain from punishment is still present but smaller, and the minimal discount factor required for cooperation is higher. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus context-dependent, especially on group size and the structure of information sharing.

- source: Cai, G. S., & Kock, N. (2009). An evolutionary game theoretic perspective on e-collaboration: The collaboration effort and media relativeness. *European Journal of Operational Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in two-player e-collaboration games, introducing or increasing social punishment for defection increases the likelihood of full cooperation, which is Pareto efficient and maximizes group payoff. When punishment is low, defection or asymmetric cooperation is stable; when punishment exceeds a threshold (typically half the cost of cooperation), full cooperation becomes the unique stable Nash equilibrium. This result holds in both discrete and continuous effort models. The paper also shows that the required punishment to induce cooperation decreases as collaboration efficiency increases. The analysis is entirely theoretical, with explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium characterizations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling or increasing punishment in a two-player, public-goods-like game will increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently strong relative to the cost of cooperation. The analytic results specify the threshold at which punishment changes the equilibrium from defection to cooperation. While the model is for two players and not a standard PGG, the structure is closely related, and the findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in small-group cooperation games. The results are most applicable when the punishment mechanism is salient, effective, and the cost/benefit structure is similar to the model.

- source: Jaffe, K. (2004). Altruism, altruistic punishment and social investment. *Acta Biotheoretica*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to examine the effects of altruistic punishment on aggregate wealth (efficiency) in artificial societies. It finds that while punishment increases generosity and norm compliance, it reduces aggregate wealth compared to societies without punishment, unless the punished behaviors trigger synergistic benefits for society. The effect is observed in both structured (with division of labor) and unstructured societies, though structured societies are more susceptible to social control via punishment. The study concludes that punishment is a strong mechanism for norm enforcement but is not, by itself, efficiency-enhancing.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides evidence that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, an agent-based resource-sharing simulation) increases norm compliance and generosity but reduces group efficiency (aggregate wealth), unless there are additional synergistic benefits from the enforced behaviors. The negative effect on efficiency is robust to variations in social structure and punishment intensity. Thus, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper suggests that, in the absence of explicit synergistic returns, punishment is likely to reduce efficiency relative to the control. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where punishment is costly and does not directly generate group-level surplus.

- source: KANDORI, M. (1992). SOCIAL NORMS AND COMMUNITY ENFORCEMENT. *Review of Economic Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that informal sanctions (punishment by community members) can support efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, provided there is sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized labels/reputation). Under public observability, any efficient and individually rational outcome can be sustained as an equilibrium, regardless of population size or matching rule. With only private experience and no information processing, cooperation (and thus efficiency) can sometimes be sustained via a 'contagious equilibrium,' but this is fragile and breaks down in large populations. Introducing local information processing (labels/reputation) allows for robust, straightforward equilibria that sustain efficiency for any population size and matching rule. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and conditions for when these results hold.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the presence of a punishment mechanism, when combined with sufficient information transmission (public observability or decentralized reputation/labels), enables the group to achieve efficient outcomes in repeated matching games, even with random matching and large populations. For the prediction task, this suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency to the maximum feasible level, provided the game design allows for sufficient information about past actions (e.g., public histories, visible labels, or reputation). If information is very limited (players only know their own history), punishment is less effective, and efficiency gains are limited, especially as group size increases. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes but gives clear qualitative predictions about the moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Ubeda, F., & Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A. (2011). POWER AND CORRUPTION. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a Prisoner's Dilemma with costly punishment, allowing for corrupt punishers and power asymmetries enables the evolution of stable states with both cooperation and corruption. When the social cost of punishing a punisher (q+d) is less than the cost of punishing a civilian (p), and within a certain range, a mixed equilibrium emerges with both cooperating civilians and corrupt policers. In this equilibrium, the population mean payoff (efficiency) is higher than in the all-defect equilibrium, and can approach the fully cooperative payoff as power asymmetries increase. The model demonstrates that costly punishment can increase both cooperation and efficiency, especially when punishers are protected by power asymmetries. The likelihood of reaching the efficient equilibrium depends on the relative costs and effectiveness of punishment. The results challenge the view that costly punishment is always inefficient, showing that under the right conditions, it can benefit the group even when punishers are themselves corrupt.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the structure of punishment costs and power asymmetries. If punishment is costly but power asymmetries exist (punishers are less vulnerable to punishment or punishment is more effective against non-punishers), then enabling punishment can increase efficiency above the control (no-punishment) baseline. The model suggests that the efficiency gain is larger when the punishment of defectors is strong and the punishment of punishers is weak. The findings are most directly applicable to Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but the logic is relevant for public goods games with similar punishment structures. The paper does not address all game design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, communication), but provides direct, mechanistic insight into how punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and power asymmetries moderate the efficiency impact of punishment.

- source: Watve, M. G., Damle, A., Ganguly, B., Kale, A., & Dahanukar, N. (2011). Blackmailing: the keystone in the human mating system. *Bmc Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a hidden-strategy game modeling human mating, the combination of punishment (social sanctions) and policing (gossip/observation), when stabilized by the possibility of blackmailing, leads to the stable coexistence of monogamy and other strategies, with monogamy maintained at high frequency. This results in higher average fitness (efficiency) for the population. If punishment is present but blackmailing is not possible, policing collapses due to second-order free-riding, and monogamy cannot be maintained. Thus, the introduction of punishment (with blackmailing) increases efficiency relative to the baseline with no punishment or with punishment alone. The model is robust to variations in the opportunity for blackmailing and to gender differences in payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (with sufficient severity and the possibility of side-payments/blackmailing) in a public-goods-like environment increases group efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. The model is not an exact PGG but is structurally analogous, with cooperation (monogamy) as the group-beneficial act and defection (EPM) as the selfish act. The results suggest that punishment alone may not be sufficient unless stabilized by additional incentives (e.g., blackmailing), but when these are present, efficiency is higher. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is costly and subject to second-order free-riding, and where additional mechanisms (like blackmailing) can offset these costs.

- source: Friedman, D., & Singh, N. (2004). Negative reciprocity - The coevolution of memes and genes. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that negative reciprocity (punishment) can transform a standard social dilemma (Prisoner's Dilemma) from an inefficient all-defect equilibrium to a coordination game with a Pareto efficient all-cooperate equilibrium, provided the punishment motive is strong enough. However, individual willingness to punish is undermined by evolutionary free-rider problems, as the costs are borne individually but the benefits are shared. The key finding is that group-level enforcement of punishment norms (memes), using low-cost sanctions such as shunning or status reduction, can sustain the optimal level of negative reciprocity and thus maximize group fitness. The model predicts that the efficiency of cooperation increases with the strength and enforcement of punishment norms, and that these norms will coevolve with individual capacities and environmental hostility. The comparative statics suggest that efficiency is higher when punishment is less costly, more effective, and more strongly enforced by group norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (especially when group-enforced) increases efficiency in social dilemmas, compared to control conditions without punishment. The model is based on a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, but the authors argue that the logic extends to n-person games like public goods games. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the rigor of group enforcement are key moderators of the efficiency gain from punishment. The model also predicts that efficiency gains from punishment are larger when punishment is less costly and more effective, and when group norms for enforcement are strong. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct evidence from public goods games.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2010). Honor among thieves: Cooperation as a strategic response to functional unpleasantness. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and high group efficiency can emerge and persist in populations where all individuals are fundamentally self-interested and prefer to cheat, as long as some are willing to punish cheaters and some are strategic enough to cooperate when the risk of being punished is high. The presence of 'Strategic Unpleasant' types—who punish cheaters and cooperate only to avoid punishment—can lead to equilibria where most or all players cooperate, resulting in high average payoffs (efficiency). The model provides explicit payoff matrices and equilibrium conditions showing that, under reasonable parameter values (punishment not too costly, punishment more aversive than being cheated, etc.), punishment enables efficient outcomes. The analysis also shows that if punishment is too costly or ineffective, cooperation and efficiency break down.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency, provided that punishment is not excessively costly and is sufficiently effective to deter cheating. The model clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost/impact structure of punishment and the strategic response of players. If the control (no-punishment) game is dominated by cheaters (low efficiency), introducing punishment—even by self-interested types—can shift the equilibrium to high efficiency. The paper's explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions can be used to inform quantitative predictions, especially regarding the importance of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the strategic incentives for cooperation.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2008). Functional unpleasantness: the evolutionary logic of righteous resentment. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation can be sustained in populations where some individuals are willing to punish cheaters, even if those punishers are themselves not cooperative. The evolutionary stability of cooperation depends on the relative costs of punishing, being punished, and being cheated. When the cost of punishing is less than the cost of being cheated, punishment can support a stable mix of cooperators and punishers, increasing overall efficiency compared to a world with only cheaters. If punishment is too costly, cheaters dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the existence of punishment mechanisms—regardless of the punishers' motives—can improve group welfare and efficiency in collective action settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in collective action games (including public goods game variants) can increase efficiency, as measured by average group payoff, provided the cost of punishing is not too high. The findings suggest that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms are key moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment. While the model is not an exact public goods game, its structure and payoff logic are closely related, and its results are directly relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency. The paper does not address empirical game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, or communication, but it does provide strong evidence that the cost and effectiveness of punishment are critical for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Vincent, J. R. (2007). Spatial dynamics, social norms, and the opportunity of the commons. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that economic incentives—specifically, the use of sanctions (punishments for non-cooperation and rewards for cooperation)—are critical for achieving efficient and sustainable management of common pool resources. Theoretical models (e.g., repeated games, evolutionary game theory) and empirical case studies (e.g., Ostrom 1990) show that monitoring and enforcement mechanisms enable groups to avoid the tragedy of the commons, leading to higher group profits and resource stocks. The author is skeptical that social norms alone, without sanctions, can achieve similar efficiency, especially when rapid or large-scale behavioral change is needed. The paper also notes that public goods (as opposed to common pool resources) face similar free-rider problems and require external enforcement (e.g., taxes, quotas) to achieve efficient provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment or sanctions in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency, group payoff, and resource sustainability, especially when compared to environments without such mechanisms. The presence of monitoring and enforcement is highlighted as a key moderator: systems with sanctions are much more likely to achieve efficient outcomes than those relying on norms alone. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates but offers strong theoretical and qualitative support for the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment/sanctions should be coded as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially in settings analogous to common pool resources.

- source: Rand, D. G., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Direct reciprocity with costly punishment: Generous tit-for-tat prevails. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, the addition of a punishment option does not increase cooperation or group payoffs in evolutionary simulations. Even when Nash equilibrium analysis allows for cooperative equilibria using punishment, evolutionary selection in finite populations favors generous tit-for-tat strategies that do not use costly punishment. Costly punishment is rarely used, and when it is, it is associated with lower payoffs. The presence of costly punishment does not allow cooperation to evolve in parameter regions where it would not already be supported by direct reciprocity alone. These findings are robust to variations in game parameters and are supported by experimental data from Dreber et al. (2008).
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling costly punishment in a repeated two-player PD-like environment does not increase efficiency or group payoff, regardless of the punishment cost or effectiveness. Evolutionary dynamics select against strategies that use costly punishment, and the most successful strategies are those that reciprocate cooperation and defect against defectors (generous tit-for-tat), not those that punish. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar repeated games, one should not expect an increase in efficiency or group payoff, and may even expect a negative association between punishment use and payoff. However, the results are specific to repeated two-player PDs and may not generalize to larger group public goods games.

- source: CLUTTONBROCK, T. H., & PARKER, G. A. (1995). PUNISHMENT IN ANIMAL SOCIETIES. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical models show that punishment can be stable and effective in animal societies, especially when dominants can punish subordinates at low cost and with high impact. Punishment can enforce cooperation, deter cheating, and maintain social order. The models identify conditions under which punishment is likely to evolve, such as strong asymmetries in power or fighting ability. Empirical examples illustrate that punishment is widespread in animal societies and can modify behavior, but the paper does not report quantitative effects on group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that punishment can increase the fitness of punishers by reducing the frequency of damaging actions by others, but do not directly address group-level efficiency as in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment can enforce cooperation and deter selfishness, which is relevant to predicting the effects of punishment in PGG-like games. However, the models are dyadic, focus on fitness rather than explicit group payoff, and do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes. The paper suggests that punishment is more likely to be effective when the cost to the punisher is low and the impact on the punished is high, and when there are clear asymmetries in power. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation and (by analogy) efficiency, but does not provide direct empirical or quantitative evidence for use in predictive modeling.

- source: Sánchez, A., & Cuesta, J. A. (2005). Altruism may arise from individual selection. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (altruistic punishment) can arise and persist in agent-based evolutionary models of the Ultimatum Game under individual selection, without the need for group or cultural selection, repeated interactions, or reputation. The key mechanism is the role of fluctuations and finite selection intervals, which allow altruistic punishers to survive and proliferate. The model reproduces the empirical finding that offers and acceptance thresholds are much higher than the self-interested Nash equilibrium. However, the paper does not analyze or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the distribution and evolution of behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the possibility that punishment (in the form of rejection of unfair offers) can evolve and persist under individual selection in one-shot, anonymous games. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding the evolutionary plausibility of punishment behaviors, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited, though it may inform mechanistic expectations about the robustness of punishment behaviors.

- source: Shinada, M., Yamagishi, T., & Ohmura, Y. (2004). False friends are worse than bitter enemies: Altruistic punishment of in-group members. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The bidirectional supervision mechanism, where players can check and avoid corrupt umpires, is effective at suppressing both corruption and defection in a spatial donation game with third-party punishment. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the punishment amount (must be within a certain range), the bribe amount (must not be too high), and the ratio of tax to checking cost (higher tax or lower checking cost is better). The benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation has little effect within reasonable ranges. The main outcomes are the fractions of different strategies (cooperation, defection, corruption, fairness) in the population. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but argues that the mechanism is most beneficial to collective interests when parameters are tuned appropriately.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the design of punishment, bribe, tax, and checking cost parameters can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation and corruption in a donation game with third-party punishment. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as qualitative or mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction of efficiency. The results suggest that punishment is only effective at suppressing defection and corruption if its severity is within a certain range and if corruption incentives (bribes) are not too high. The presence of a mechanism for players to avoid corrupt punishers is critical. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs with third-party punishment and corruption, but less directly transferable to standard public goods games or to quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Shutters, S. T. (2009). Strong reciprocity, social structure, and the evolution of fair allocations in a simulated ultimatum game. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a simulated ultimatum game, third-party punishment (altruistic punishment) does not generally lead to fair allocations, except in linearly structured populations where fair offers can evolve even without punishment. As the punishment multiplier (punishment impact per unit cost) increases, populations with spatial structure exhibit a sharp transition from low to very high offers, but these are not necessarily fair. The number of neighbors (social structure) strongly moderates both baseline offers and the threshold at which punishment induces high offers. The main outcome is the mean offer rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost-effectiveness and social structure (number of neighbors) affect behavioral outcomes (mean offer rates) in an ultimatum game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment in PGG-like environments. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in changing behavior is highly sensitive to the punishment cost/impact ratio and the social network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of considering punishment cost-effectiveness and group structure, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Xu, C., Ji, M., Yap, Y. J., Zheng, D. F., & Hui, P. M. (2011). Costly punishment and cooperation in the evolutionary snowdrift game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing costly punishment into the evolutionary snowdrift game can lead to two types of steady states: (1) a mixture of cooperators and punishers (with defectors eliminated), or (2) a mixture of cooperators and defectors (with punishers eliminated), depending on the cost-to-benefit ratio, punishment parameters, and initial conditions. The presence of punishers can enhance cooperation relative to the standard snowdrift game, but this is measured in terms of the frequency of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model provides analytic and simulation results for the conditions under which defectors can or cannot invade a population with punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment on cooperation in a two-player snowdrift game, which is adjacent to public goods games. The main outcomes are strategy frequencies, not efficiency or group payoff, so the results are not directly usable for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. However, the finding that costly punishment can eliminate defectors and increase the frequency of cooperators under certain conditions suggests that punishment may increase efficiency in analogous settings, especially when the cost of punishment is low relative to its impact. The model's focus on initial conditions and parameter sensitivity may inform qualitative expectations about when punishment is more or less effective, but quantitative prediction of efficiency is not supported.

- source: Xu, C., Hui, P. M., Yu, Y. Y., & Gu, G. Q. (2009). Self-organized cooperative behavior and critical penalty in an evolving population. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of agents playing a two-action game (cooperate or defect), increasing the penalty for defection increases the mean probability to cooperate. There is a critical penalty threshold, dependent on initial conditions, above which the population becomes fully cooperative. For populations with higher initial cooperation, less penalty is needed to achieve full cooperation, and in some cases, no penalty is required. The model also shows that after a population is trained to cooperate with penalty, the penalty can be relaxed without immediate loss of cooperation. The structure of the population (well-mixed vs. 2D lattice) affects the level of cooperation at intermediate penalties, with lattices supporting higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the findings are not directly about payoff-based efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the severity of punishment can increase cooperation rates in repeated social dilemma games, with a critical threshold for full cooperation. However, because the main outcome is the mean probability to cooperate and not group payoff or efficiency, the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and may inform qualitative expectations about the effect of punishment on cooperation, but not on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective in increasing cooperation, especially when initial cooperation is low, and that population structure can moderate this effect. For the prediction task, this paper is best used as indirect, qualitative support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation, but not as quantitative evidence for efficiency effects.

- source: Sekiguchi, T., & Nakamaru, M. (2009). Effect of the presence of empty sites on the evolution of cooperation by costly punishment in spatial games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of empty sites in spatially structured populations (lattices) promotes the evolution of cooperation and does not reduce the effect of costly punishment. In contrast, in completely mixing populations, empty sites reduce the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation. The evolutionary success of strategies depends on the benefit of cooperation, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the density of empty sites. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather which strategies dominate under different parameter regimes. The findings suggest that spatial structure and population density (via empty sites) are important moderators of the evolutionary dynamics of punishment and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that spatial structure (analogous to networked or local interaction in PGGs) and the presence of empty sites (analogous to non-participation or dropout) can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. In spatial settings, punishment remains effective even with empty sites, while in well-mixed settings, empty sites undermine punishment's effectiveness. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to promote cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) depending on spatial structure and participation rates, but do not provide quantitative efficiency effects.

- source: Jones, M. A. (1999). The effect of punishment duration of trigger strategies and quasifinite continuation probabilities for Prisoners' Dilemmas. *International Journal of Game Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to sustain cooperation in indefinitely repeated Prisoners' Dilemma games depends critically on the duration of punishment (in trigger strategies) and the properties of the continuation probability sequence. Longer punishment durations (including the grim trigger) make it easier to support cooperation in equilibrium. If the continuation probability is 'quasifinite' (i.e., its geometric average tends to zero), then no amount of punishment can sustain cooperation, and only the noncooperative equilibrium exists. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for when cooperation is possible, and shows how to compute the minimum punishment duration needed for cooperation given the discount factor and continuation probability. These results are framed in terms of equilibrium existence and structure, not direct efficiency or payoff outcomes, but imply that punishment can enable higher group payoffs when the conditions are met.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how punishment duration and the probability of future interaction (continuation probability) affect the possibility of sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, the results suggest that increasing punishment duration (severity) generally increases the likelihood that punishment will improve efficiency, provided the continuation probability is not too low. If the effective game horizon is short (quasifinite), punishment will not improve efficiency. While the analysis is for Prisoners' Dilemma rather than public goods games, the logic and mathematical structure are closely related and can inform predictions about when punishment will be effective in increasing efficiency. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency changes, only conditions for the existence of cooperative equilibria.

- source: Li, X., & Cao, L. (2009). Largest Laplacian eigenvalue predicts the emergence of costly punishment in the evolutionary ultimatum game on networks. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the evolutionary ultimatum game on networks, there is a critical value of the population's selfishness degree (the share of the resource claimed by the proposer) above which a fraction of players become pure costly punishers (always reject offers). This critical value is analytically predicted by the inverse of the largest Laplacian eigenvalue of the network. The emergence of costly punishment is thus determined by network topology. The study also shows that pure punishers can have a higher average payoff ratio than others, especially as selfishness increases, but group-level efficiency or welfare is not directly analyzed. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations on various network types (scale-free, small-world, real-world collaboration networks).
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it studies the ultimatum game (an adjacent but distinct paradigm) and focuses on the emergence of costly punishment rather than on group efficiency or total payoff. The main outcome is the fraction of pure punishers as a function of network structure and selfishness degree. While the paper provides insight into how network topology affects the threshold for costly punishment, it does not provide data or analysis on how punishment affects group efficiency or welfare in a public-goods-like environment. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to theoretical context and mechanism discussion.

- source: Pothos, E. M., Perry, G., Corr, P. J., Matthew, M. R., & Busemeyer, J. R. (2011). Understanding cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that higher punishment severity, higher disguise cost, lower cost-to-benefit ratio, and larger group size all promote the extinction of defectors and the prevalence of cooperation (cooperators and punishers). When these parameters are less favorable, the system remains in a mixed state with both cooperators and defectors. The model shows that the system typically evolves to a state with only two strategies (either cooperators and punishers, or cooperators and defectors), and the boundaries between these regimes are mapped as functions of the key parameters. The results are robust to both deterministic replicator dynamics and stochastic mutation-selection dynamics in finite populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence about how punishment parameters (cost, effectiveness), group size, and cost-to-benefit ratio affect the prevalence of cooperation in an N-person social dilemma with punishment. While the model is for the snowdrift game (not a standard PGG), and the main outcomes are behavioral frequencies rather than efficiency, the results suggest that increasing punishment severity, increasing group size, and increasing the cost for defectors to avoid punishment all make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff ratios, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible. The findings are most useful for understanding qualitative moderators of punishment effectiveness in related social dilemmas.

- source: Woodcock, S., & Heath, J. (2002). The robustness of altruism as an evolutionary strategy. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment mechanisms, such as enforcement and ostracism, dramatically increase the robustness of altruism in evolutionary simulations of the prisoner's dilemma. While group selection and imitation can help sustain altruism under certain conditions (e.g., small group size, high imitation rates), punishment allows altruism to persist in much larger groups and under a wider range of conditions. The key mechanism is that punishment increases correlation among altruists, allowing them to interact more with each other and less with defectors. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes; it focuses on the evolutionary persistence of altruism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment mechanisms can greatly increase the persistence of altruism (cooperation) in evolutionary environments similar to the prisoner's dilemma. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of altruists rather than group efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency in public goods games. The results suggest that, all else equal, adding punishment mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation and thus could plausibly increase efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this effect in payoff terms. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence and effectiveness of punishment, group size, and the structure of interaction (e.g., group partitioning, imitation).

- source: Traxler, C., & Winter, J. (2012). Survey evidence on conditional norm enforcement. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reputation can sustain norm compliance, but their roles differ depending on the distribution of social motivation in the population. When reputation effects are weak, higher levels of punishment are needed to enforce the norm. When reputation effects are strong, norms can be sustained with little or no punishment. The model shows two stable equilibria: one with high compliance (if initial compliance is above a critical threshold) and one with low compliance (if initial compliance is below the threshold). The probability of punishment increases as the benefit-cost ratio for punishers decreases (i.e., as the psychological cost of punishing decreases). The interaction of punishment and reputation provides a robust mechanism for norm stability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can increase norm compliance, especially when reputation effects are weak. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify the effects of game design dimensions such as MPCR, player count, or number of rounds. The model is adjacent to public goods games but is not directly parameterized in terms of efficiency. For prediction tasks, the main insight is that punishment is more effective at sustaining compliance when reputation incentives are weak, and that the two mechanisms can substitute for each other to some extent. The paper does not inform how punishment affects efficiency or payoff in public goods games with specific design features.

- source: Bshary, R., & Bronstein, J. L. (2011). A General Scheme to Predict Partner Control Mechanisms in Pairwise Cooperative Interactions Between Unrelated Individuals. *Ethology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper provides a decision-tree framework linking specific game structures (defined by investment, payoff structure, symmetry, repetition, and partner choice) to the partner control mechanisms that can stabilize cooperation. Punishment is predicted to be a viable control mechanism in repeated interactions without partner choice, especially when both players can invest and defect. The framework also highlights the roles of reward, sanctions, partner switching, and indirect reciprocity in other game structures. The review does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment, but it does clarify the conditions under which punishment is theoretically expected to promote cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for identifying which game structures are theoretically compatible with punishment as a mechanism for stabilizing cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on the efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods or similar games. For the downstream prediction task, the framework can help map which design dimensions (e.g., repeated interactions, partner choice, symmetry) are necessary for punishment to be a relevant mechanism, but it cannot inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes. The paper is most useful for contextualizing when punishment is likely to be effective, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bingham, P. M. (1999). Human uniqueness: A general theory. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The central finding is that coalitional enforcement—cooperative, low-cost punishment of defectors—was necessary and sufficient for the evolution of large-scale, stable cooperation among unrelated individuals in humans. The theory predicts that as the efficiency and scale of coalitional enforcement increased (via technological and social monitoring advances), so did the extent and complexity of human cooperation. The paper discusses how this mechanism underlies the emergence of language, culture, large political units, and rapid technological progress. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings about efficiency or payoffs in public goods game-like environments; rather, it offers a theoretical framework for understanding the evolutionary roots of cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment (specifically, coalitional or group-based punishment) can enable and stabilize high levels of cooperation among non-kin, by reducing the individual cost of enforcement and making defection unprofitable. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence and efficiency of punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of group cooperation and, by extension, group efficiency. However, because the paper does not report empirical or experimental data on efficiency or payoffs in public goods games, it cannot be used to calibrate quantitative predictions. Its main value is in supporting the general principle that more efficient, lower-cost, and more widely distributed punishment mechanisms should be associated with higher group cooperation and efficiency, all else equal.

- source: Jaffe, K. (2008). Evolution of Shame as an Adaptation to Social Punishment and its Contribution to Social Cohesiveness. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that shame can evolve as an adaptation to social punishment, increasing pro-social behavior (generosity) after punishment. When punishment is present (especially if costless to the punisher) or when shame is present, populations maintain higher levels of generosity and a balance between punishers and non-punishers. However, the model does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of behavioral traits. The results suggest that shame and punishment can stabilize cooperation for long periods, but with fluctuations between cooperative and selfish phases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (and shame) can increase pro-social behavior in resource-sharing environments, which may translate to higher efficiency in public goods games. However, because the model is not a direct PGG and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The main relevance is in showing that punishment and shame can stabilize cooperation, especially when punishment is not costly to the punisher. The findings are more about evolutionary stability and trait frequencies than about efficiency per se.

- source: Huang, F. (2007). Building social trust: A human-capital approach. *Journal of Institutional and Theoretical Economics-zeitschrift Fur Die Gesamte Staatswissenschaft*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment institutions are more effective at detecting and punishing defection, more individuals choose to invest in cooperative tendencies, leading to higher social trust (i.e., a higher proportion of cooperative players). However, the average level of intrinsic cooperative tendency among cooperators is lower, since extrinsic incentives substitute for intrinsic motivation. The model predicts multiple equilibria depending on the distribution of investment costs, and highlights the potential for both high- and low-trust equilibria. The relationship between institutional punishment and cooperation is complex: stronger institutions can crowd in more cooperators but may reduce intrinsic motivation. The paper does not provide direct predictions or analysis of group efficiency or total payoff, but the results are relevant for understanding how punishment institutions affect the prevalence of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that increasing the effectiveness of punishment institutions (e.g., higher probability of detection or stronger sanctions) increases the proportion of cooperative players in a prisoners' dilemma-like environment. While it does not directly model public goods games or report efficiency outcomes, it suggests that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates, which may translate into higher efficiency in related environments. However, the paper also warns that reliance on extrinsic punishment may reduce intrinsic motivation over time. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that enabling or strengthening punishment is likely to increase cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency), but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified. The model is most relevant for settings where the main outcome is the prevalence of cooperation rather than group payoff.

- source: Boehm, C. (2008). Purposive Social Selection and the Evolution of Human Altruism. *Cross-cultural Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in small-scale, mobile hunter-gatherer societies, both punitive and positive social sanctioning are pervasive and target behaviors relevant to group cooperation, such as sharing, generosity, and suppression of dominance or cheating. Punishment (including ostracism and even capital punishment) is used to control antisocial behaviors, while positive sanctioning (reputation, social approval, preferential partnerships) rewards prosocial behaviors. The author theorizes that these mechanisms have had significant evolutionary effects, shaping both the prevalence of altruism and the development of conscience in humans. However, the findings are based on ethnographic coding and theoretical reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative analysis of efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and ethnographic support for the idea that punishment and reward mechanisms are central to the maintenance of cooperation in small-scale societies, and that they have long-term evolutionary consequences. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but does not provide data or effect sizes relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

- source: Kritikos, A., & Bolle, F. (2004). Punishment as a public good. When should monopolists care about a consumer boycott?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of consumers to punish a monopolist (e.g., via boycott) and thereby reduce prices depends on the number of consumers and the degree of solidarity among them. With few consumers, individual punishment can be effective and lead to lower prices. As the number of consumers increases, the incentive to individually punish diminishes (public good problem), and the monopolist can more easily charge higher (Cournot) prices unless solidarity is high. The model predicts that successful collective punishment (boycott) is more likely in small groups or when solidarity is strong, and less likely in large, uncoordinated groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how group size and solidarity affect the effectiveness of punishment in public-good-like environments, specifically in the context of consumer boycotts against monopolists. While it does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, it suggests that increasing group size without mechanisms for solidarity or coordination will reduce the effectiveness of punishment in improving group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is less likely to improve efficiency in large, uncoordinated groups, but may be effective in small groups or when solidarity is high. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its quantitative predictive value.

- source: López-Pérez, R. (2008). Aversion to norm-breaking:: A model. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which agents internalize social norms (specifically, an 'E-norm' combining efficiency and equality) and experience emotional costs (shame, anger) when violating or observing violations of these norms. The model predicts that people will cooperate and punish norm violators even at a cost to themselves, and that punishment is targeted at those responsible for norm violations. The model is shown to qualitatively account for a range of experimental findings in games such as dictator, ultimatum, trust, centipede, Cournot, and public goods games. In the context of public goods games, the model predicts that the introduction of punishment opportunities increases cooperation by deterring selfish behavior, as selfish agents comply to avoid being punished by principled agents. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical rationale for why punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance in public goods and related games, suggesting that the presence of punishment opportunities should increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment, especially when a sufficient proportion of players are 'principled' and willing to punish norm violations. However, the model is qualitative and does not specify the magnitude of efficiency gains or how they depend on specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR). The model does not provide direct evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game parameters, but supports the general expectation that punishment opportunities will have a more positive effect on efficiency in environments where norm internalization and willingness to punish are present.

- source: Ziegler, R. (1997). The normative structure of solidarity and inequality. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both dyadic and collective solidarity games (including public-goods-like settings), strategies that combine defection with active sanctioning of others (Hypocritical Cooperation) can robustly enforce high levels of solidarity (cooperation), provided the costs of sanctioning are not too high. However, this can also lead to 'overcompliance' or excessive norm enforcement unless checked by counterstrategies (Full Opposition). The evolutionary outcome depends on the mix of strategies, the costs and effectiveness of sanctions, and the distribution of 'poor' and 'rich' in the population. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that informal punishment can sustain cooperation under certain conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that informal punishment mechanisms can sustain cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of sanctioning is not prohibitive and when the population includes actors motivated to enforce norms even while defecting themselves. However, the findings are based on simulation and focus on the prevalence of strategies and 'level of solidarity' rather than explicit group efficiency or payoff. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency will depend on the cost and effectiveness of sanctions, the strategic mix in the population, and the degree of inequality. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, so its value for direct prediction is limited to qualitative insights about when punishment is likely to be effective.

- source: Sripada, C. S. (2005). Punishment and the strategic structure of moral systems. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
