  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment is a more general and empirically plausible mechanism for sustaining compliance with moral norms than reciprocity. Punishment can support cooperation in large groups and can enforce arbitrary rules, not just those with a cooperative payoff structure. The author reviews theoretical models (e.g., Boyd and Richerson 1992) and ethnographic/experimental evidence to support the claim that punishment, including direct, exclusion-based, and reputation-based forms, is universal in human groups. The paper also discusses the problem of stabilizing costly punishment and the implications for understanding moral diversity. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the importance of punishment mechanisms in sustaining cooperation and compliance in public-goods-like environments, especially in large groups. It suggests that, in theory, enabling punishment should increase compliance and cooperation, and thus could improve efficiency, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes. The arguments are useful for understanding why punishment may be effective, but the paper does not supply empirical data or parameterized results that can be directly used for predicting efficiency changes in experimental public goods games. Its value is mainly in supporting the general plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation and norm compliance, especially in large groups.

- source: Dubreuil, B. (2008). Strong reciprocity and the emergence of large-scale societies. *Philosophy of the Social Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper claims that strong reciprocity and altruistic punishment are key to human cooperation, but that their effectiveness is limited by cognitive constraints, especially as group size increases. As groups grow, the costs and logistical difficulties of monitoring and punishing free riders rise, reducing the effectiveness of informal punishment and thus limiting group size. The emergence of large-scale societies required the development of institutions that formalize and distribute the responsibility for sanctioning (the 'social division of sanction'), which in turn enabled larger, more complex societies. The paper references experimental evidence (e.g., Isaac et al. 1994; Carpenter 2007; Keser and van Winden 2000) to support claims about group size, punishment, and cooperation, but does not itself report new data or analyze efficiency directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and cooperation interact with group size and institutional structure, but does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It suggests that as group size increases, the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation may decrease due to cognitive and monitoring constraints, unless institutional mechanisms are introduced. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that group size and the presence or absence of formal sanctioning institutions are important moderators of punishment's effect, but does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence for efficiency outcomes.

Drawing on public goods game experiments (Fehr & Gächter, 2002), the author claims that the availability of punishment increases cooperation, and extends this logic to explain extreme forms of group cohesion and violence in historical and contemporary political movements. However, the paper does not report any new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.

However, it does not provide empirical data, efficiency outcomes, or direct analysis of game design dimensions. Its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited to its summary of prior experimental findings (e.g., Fehr & Gächter, 2002), not to any new or directly relevant evidence.

  findings: The paper reviews and synthesizes theoretical and simulation results showing that lattice-structured populations (as opposed to well-mixed populations) can promote the evolution of cooperation and punishment (Altruist-Punisher), as well as spiteful behavior, depending on the model's updating rules. In particular, the Altruist-Punisher strategy can invade selfish populations more easily in lattice models, and punishment is more effective in promoting cooperation in these settings. However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on the evolutionary success of strategies and the spread of norms.

For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper's findings are only adjacent: they suggest that punishment may be more effective in structured populations, but do not quantify the effect on efficiency or provide data on payoff outcomes. The paper is more useful for understanding mechanisms and contextual moderators than for direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Nakao, H., & Machery, E. (2012). The evolution of punishment. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment is widespread in nature and often evolves for reasons other than inducing behavioral change in the punishee. In many cases (especially among plants and insects), punishment is a loss-cutting or cost-imposing strategy, not a behavior-modification strategy. Even among vertebrates, where behavioral modification is more common, the evolutionary benefits of punishment often do not depend on the punishee changing its behavior. The paper challenges the view that punishment primarily evolved to promote cooperation or efficiency, and instead highlights alternative evolutionary pathways.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative data relevant to predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments. It does, however, offer theoretical context suggesting that the evolutionary function of punishment may not always be to increase cooperation or group efficiency, but could instead be to reduce harm to the punisher or its kin. For the prediction task, this paper suggests caution in assuming that punishment mechanisms will always improve efficiency or cooperation, especially in settings where behavioral modification is unlikely or where punishment serves other evolutionary functions. However, it does not provide empirical or experimental data to calibrate such predictions.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that, although theoretical models (including those based on the Prisoner's Dilemma) predict that punishment can stabilize cooperation, actual evidence for punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in nonhuman animals is rare. Most observed 'punishment' is better characterized as harassment, which yields immediate rather than delayed benefits. The rarity of punishment and reciprocity in animals is attributed to cognitive constraints, particularly the need for individual recognition, low temporal discounting, and sufficient memory to track interactions. In contrast, humans possess cognitive adaptations that enable widespread use of punishment and reciprocity to sustain cooperation. The paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for why punishment may or may not be effective in sustaining cooperation, especially in nonhuman animals. It highlights the importance of cognitive prerequisites (recognition, memory, discounting) for punishment to function as a stabilizer of cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence or data that can be used to predict the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar experimental designs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on whether the game environment allows for individual recognition, repeated interactions, and low discounting, but it does not quantify these effects or relate them to efficiency outcomes.

- source: Henrich, J. (2004). Cultural group selection, coevolutionary processes and large-scale cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cultural group selection, enabled by human-specific social learning mechanisms, can explain the evolution and stability of large-scale cooperation and punishment in humans. It theorizes that punishment, when stabilized by conformist transmission, can support high levels of cooperation even in large, anonymous groups, and that these group-beneficial equilibria can be selected for at the group level. The paper does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games, but it does argue that punishment and cooperation can be maintained at high frequency under plausible cultural evolutionary dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation and group-beneficial outcomes in large groups, especially when cultural transmission mechanisms are present. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of group structure, cultural transmission, and norm enforcement (punishment) as moderators of cooperation and efficiency, but it does not specify how game design dimensions quantitatively affect efficiency outcomes.

- source: Abramson, N. R., & Senyshyn, Y. (2010). Effective Punishment Through Forgiveness: Rediscovering Kierkegaard's Knight of Faith in the Abraham Story. *Organization Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that punishment, when combined with forgiveness and a commitment to reconciliation (as in the Knight of Faith model), is more likely to restore trust and effective relationships than traditional retributive approaches. It draws on philosophical analysis, biblical narrative (the Abraham story), and real-world examples (South African Truth and Reconciliation Commission vs. Sarbanes-Oxley Act) to argue that restorative justice processes can lead to repentance and reconciliation, whereas retributive punishment alone often leads to retaliation, distrust, and ongoing conflict. However, these findings are conceptual and interpretive, not empirical or quantitative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative data relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games or similar environments. Its value for the prediction task is limited to conceptual insights: it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and restoring group functioning may depend on the presence of forgiveness and opportunities for reconciliation, rather than punishment alone. However, it does not address or manipulate the game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & McAuliffe, K. (2012). Does Inequity Aversion Motivate Punishment? Cleaner Fish as a Model System. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in cleaner fish, punishment of cheating partners is not motivated by inequity aversion, as these fish do not show behavioral sensitivity to unequal outcomes in controlled experiments. Instead, punishment appears to be triggered by loss aversion—when the punisher experiences a reduction in expected payoff (e.g., when a client leaves after a partner cheats). In humans, both inequity aversion and loss aversion may motivate punishment, but the evidence is not conclusive. The paper reviews but does not present new data on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is useful for understanding the psychological or proximate mechanisms underlying punishment (loss aversion vs. inequity aversion), which may inform the design of future experiments or the interpretation of why punishment sometimes increases cooperation. However, it does not report or synthesize efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. Its relevance is primarily theoretical and mechanistic.

- source: Chassang, S., & Takahashi, S. (2011). Robustness to incomplete information in repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoners' dilemma games, the set of robustly sustainable equilibrium payoffs (i.e., efficiency) is sharply affected by concerns about incomplete information. Under complete information, grim-trigger strategies can sustain cooperation if the discounted value of future cooperation exceeds the temptation to defect. However, when robustness to incomplete information is required, the cost of being the 'sucker' (cooperating while the other defects) becomes critical, and the set of sustainable payoffs can shrink dramatically. Selective-punishment strategies, which punish only the deviator, are more robust than grim-trigger strategies, and can sustain cooperation under a wider range of parameters. The paper provides explicit characterizations of when efficient cooperation is robustly sustainable and when only inefficient outcomes (permanent defection) are robust. The main outcomes are theoretical characterizations of efficiency/welfare as a function of game parameters and strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated prisoners' dilemma–like games under incomplete information. It shows that the design of the punishment mechanism (grim-trigger vs. selective punishment), the cost of being punished, and the enforceability of asymmetric outcomes are critical moderators. If only symmetric cooperation (CC) is enforceable, robust efficiency collapses to permanent defection. If asymmetric outcomes (DC or CD) are enforceable, robust efficiency can be as high as in the complete information case. The results are directly about efficiency/welfare, not just contribution rates. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the details of the punishment scheme and the game's payoff structure, especially under incomplete information. However, the results are for repeated prisoners' dilemma, not public goods games per se, so transfer to PGGs should be done with caution.

- source: Röhl, T., Röhl, C., Schuster, H. G., & Traulsen, A. (2007). Impact of fraud on the mean-field dynamics of cooperative social systems. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a reputation-based cooperation system (image scoring), the introduction of fraud (scammers who fake cooperation) and punishment (penalties for detected fraud) leads to complex dynamics. If the probability of detecting fraud and the penalty for fraud are high enough, cooperation (discriminators) can dominate. For intermediate detection probabilities, a limited presence of scammers can actually increase the basin of attraction for cooperation, making it easier for cooperation to evolve. The number of rounds of interaction is a key moderator: with more rounds, the payoff advantage of cooperation increases. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of cost, benefit, penalty, detection probability, and rounds) for when each strategy is favored. The overall effect of punishment on efficiency is mixed: it can increase efficiency by suppressing fraud, but the presence of some fraud can also facilitate cooperation under certain conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in reputation-based cooperation games depends on the detection probability, penalty size, cost-benefit ratio, and number of rounds. In games with more rounds and higher detection/penalty, punishment is more likely to increase efficiency by supporting cooperation. However, a limited amount of undetected fraud can sometimes increase the basin of attraction for cooperation, so the relationship is not strictly monotonic. The model is not a standard public goods game, but its structure and payoff logic are closely related. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the efficiency impact of punishment will be more positive when detection is high, penalties are large, and the game is repeated over more rounds. The presence of fraud and its detectability are important moderators.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2004). How should we define goodness? Reputation dynamics in indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that only a small subset of reputation assignment rules—those that treat defection against bad individuals as good (i.e., as a form of justified punishment or sanction)—can sustain high efficiency (group payoff close to the maximum possible) in indirect reciprocity games. These 'leading eight' rules enable stable cooperation by allowing individuals to refuse help to bad actors without losing their own good reputation. The model shows that, under these rules, the average payoff in the population approaches the maximum possible (b-c), minus a small term due to errors. The results are robust to error rates and to the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as b > c. The paper provides a formal evolutionary justification for the importance of sanctioning/punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in reputation-based social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms—specifically, the ability to sanction or refuse help to bad actors without reputational cost—are critical for sustaining high efficiency in indirect reciprocity environments. While the model is not a standard public goods game, it is closely related: it is a repeated, pairwise helping game with a social dilemma structure. The findings suggest that, in environments where individuals can sanction defectors (and where such sanctioning is not itself punished), group efficiency can approach the maximum possible. For prediction tasks, this implies that enabling punishment or justified sanctioning in a game design is likely to increase efficiency, provided the reputation system is designed so that justified punishment does not harm the punisher's reputation. The results are robust to errors and to variations in the benefit-to-cost ratio, as long as cooperation is individually costly but socially beneficial.

- source: Choi, J. K. (2007). Trembles may support cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of trembles (errors) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games can support the evolutionary stability of conditional cooperation (punishment-based strategies), but only when the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation is not too high. Specifically, if the benefit of cooperation is less than or equal to twice the cost (b ≤ 2c), then the ALLT (all trigger) equilibrium is asymptotically stable for sufficiently high continuation probability and low error rate. If the benefit is too high (b > 2c), then the equilibrium becomes unstable, and unconditional cooperators can invade, undermining the effectiveness of punishment. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using explicit payoff calculations and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in supporting cooperation (and thus efficiency) in repeated prisoner's dilemma-like environments depends critically on the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation and the presence of errors. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment will only improve efficiency when the benefit-to-cost ratio is not too high and when errors are present but not too frequent. The results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated games with trigger strategies, but the logic may extend to public goods games with similar strategic structure. The findings highlight that increasing the benefit of cooperation does not always make punishment more effective; in fact, it can undermine stability if too large. The paper does not provide direct evidence for multi-player public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed are relevant for adjacent settings.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2005). Indirect reciprocity, image scoring, and moral hazard. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in models of indirect reciprocity with binary reputation, cooperation can be stably maintained if individuals' knowledge of others' reputations increases over time (e.g., with age or experience). Costly punishment—refusing to help those with a Bad score, even at the risk of one's own reputation—can support a stable equilibrium of cooperation, provided the information environment is sufficiently rich. If information is too scarce, defectors dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the efficiency (total payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) is higher when these conditions for stable cooperation are met, and that the presence of costly punishment is essential in the minimal model for resisting defectors. The findings are directly relevant to efficiency and payoff outcomes in social dilemmas with reputation and punishment-like mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment (in the form of costly refusal to help those with a Bad reputation) can increase efficiency in social dilemma environments, but only when the information environment allows for effective reputation tracking. The model is not a literal public goods game, but the mechanisms and payoff logic are closely related. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control only if players can reliably observe or infer others' reputations, and if the cost-to-benefit ratio of helping is not too high. The findings also highlight the importance of the number of rounds (or opportunities for information to accumulate) and the structure of information flow. However, the model does not address group size, communication, or some other public goods game design features directly.

- source: Nuño, J. C., Herrero, M. A., & Primicerio, M. (2010). Fighting cheaters: How and how much to invest. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in the absence of anti-cheating expenditure, cheaters dominate and total wealth collapses. Introducing and increasing expenditure to fight cheating (via social or punitive means) reduces the cheater population and increases total wealth, but only up to an optimal point. Beyond this, further expenditure reduces wealth due to the cost of enforcement. The optimal strategy involves a balance between social promotion and deterrence, with the best compromise (for the parameter values used) being to spend about 5% of wealth on anti-cheating measures, allocating about 90% of that to social promotion. The model also shows that the effectiveness of punishment (police efficiency) is a critical moderator: if punishment is highly effective, more resources should be allocated to it; if not, social measures are preferable. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by sensitivity analyses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment or anti-cheating measures in a public-goods-like environment increases efficiency (total wealth) up to an optimal point, after which further increases in punishment expenditure can reduce efficiency. The model highlights the importance of both the amount spent and the effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (or anti-cheating expenditure) will increase efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline, but the magnitude depends on the cost, effectiveness, and allocation of resources. The model also warns that excessive punishment spending can be counterproductive. While the model is not an experimental PGG, its structure and outcomes are closely analogous and informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Levine, D. K., & Pesendorfer, W. (2007). The evolution of cooperation through imitation. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary models where players can imitate successful strategies and occasionally innovate, the long-run outcome depends on the ability to identify others' strategies. With perfect identification, efficient cooperation enforced by punishment is stable and the group achieves high efficiency. With imperfect or noisy identification, punishment becomes less effective, and the long-run outcome is less efficient, sometimes even less efficient than the static Nash equilibrium. The model shows that punishment strategies that condition on signals about others' types can sustain cooperation, but only if those signals are sufficiently informative. The presence of strategies that can 'masquerade' as cooperators (i.e., avoid detection) can undermine cooperation and efficiency unless masquerading is costly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency in public-goods-like environments depends on the information structure—specifically, the ability to identify defectors. If the game design allows for perfect or highly informative identification of defectors, punishment can sustain efficient cooperation. If identification is noisy or easily circumvented, punishment is less effective and may not improve efficiency. The results suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, one should pay close attention to the information available to players about others' actions or types, and to the possibility of undetectable defection or masquerading. The model is not an empirical test, but it provides a mechanistic basis for expecting positive, null, or even negative effects of punishment on efficiency depending on these design features.

- source: Castro, L., Serrano, J. M., & Toro, M. A. (1998). Conceptual capacity to categorize and the evolution of altruism. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the Assessor strategy, which incorporates a punishment-like mechanism (reprobation) based on observed or reported selfish behavior, achieves higher fitness (i.e., greater group efficiency or payoff) than standard reciprocal strategies like Tit-for-Tat or Observer Tit-for-Tat when competing against always-defect strategies. This advantage is robust across both unstructured and family-structured populations, provided certain thresholds of cooperation frequency are met. The Assessor strategy is especially effective when individuals can observe or be informed about others' reprobation of selfish behavior, allowing them to withhold cooperation from defectors more efficiently. The model predicts that such punishment-like mechanisms can support higher levels of cooperation and group payoff than reciprocity alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctioning mechanisms (here, reprobation) in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency/payoff relative to control conditions without such mechanisms. The findings are most directly relevant to iterated prisoner's dilemma settings, which are adjacent to public goods games, and suggest that the ability to observe and act on others' punishment signals is a key moderator. For prediction tasks, the results imply that, all else equal, introducing a punishment mechanism should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when players can observe or learn about others' punishment actions. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide quantitative effect sizes or empirical validation.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Iwasa, Y. (2006). The leading eight: Social norms that can maintain cooperation by indirect reciprocity. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations, only eight specific social norms (the 'leading eight') can maintain both evolutionary stability and a high level of cooperation/efficiency, even when errors in action and assessment occur. These norms require players to cooperate with good-reputation individuals, punish defectors, justify punishment, and allow for apology and forgiveness. Under these norms, the average payoff per interaction is close to the theoretical maximum (b-c), and the fraction of cooperative (good) players remains high, with only a small reduction due to errors. The paper provides analytical conditions for when these norms are stable and efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in indirect reciprocity games with binary reputations and one-shot interactions, the structure of the social norm (reputation dynamics) is critical for achieving high efficiency. Specifically, norms that combine justified punishment (refusing to help bad actors and not being penalized for it) and forgiveness (allowing recovery from bad reputation) can maintain efficiency close to the maximum. While the game is not a standard public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms in repeated or reputation-based social dilemmas. The results suggest that, when punishment is structured as justified refusal to cooperate (and not as costly peer punishment), and when forgiveness is possible, efficiency losses due to punishment can be minimized or avoided. The paper does not address group size, communication, or other PGG-specific dimensions directly, but it does show that the design of sanctioning and reputation mechanisms is a key moderator of efficiency.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2008). Mutualism, market effects and partner control. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the threat of terminating an interaction (partner control) can suppress cheating and maintain cooperation, but its effectiveness depends on the relative abundance of exploiters and victims (market effects). When victims are rare, they can enforce cooperation and achieve higher payoffs; when they are common, exploiters can cheat more, reducing the efficiency of the interaction. If exploiters gain more control over the duration of interaction, they can extract more benefit, potentially driving the system into parasitism (negative payoffs for the victim). The model provides explicit conditions for when mutualism (positive payoffs) or parasitism (negative payoffs) will occur, and shows that efficiency is maximized when partner control is strong and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like partner control on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is highly sensitive to the structure of partner choice and market conditions. If the 'punishment' (exit threat) is credible and the cost of finding new partners is high for exploiters, efficiency is increased. If exploiters can easily find new victims, or if they gain control over the interaction, efficiency can decrease or even become negative for the exploited party. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency will depend on parameters analogous to partner abundance, search costs, and control over interaction duration. The model does not map directly onto standard PGGs, but its logic is relevant for settings where partner choice or exit is possible.

- source: Gintis, H. (2003). The hitchhiker's guide to altruism: Gene-culture coevolution, and the internalization of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that internalized prosocial norms, including altruistic punishment, can become evolutionarily stable through gene-culture coevolution, especially when the fitness benefits of prosocial norms are high, extraparental socialization is strong, and assortative mating is present. Simulations show that groups with internalized altruistic punishment achieve high mean fitness and eliminate antisocial norms. Altruistic punishment is particularly effective at sustaining cooperation and group efficiency because its cost is lower and more concentrated than the cost of direct cooperation. The models predict that, in the presence of punishment, group efficiency and mean fitness are higher than in its absence, especially in large groups or when repeated interaction is infrequent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments increases group efficiency and mean payoff, especially when direct cooperation is not stable due to high costs. The models suggest that punishment is a robust mechanism for sustaining cooperation and high group payoff, even in large groups or with high migration, as long as the cost of punishment is low relative to the benefit of cooperation. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where internalized norms and cultural transmission play a role, and less so to short-run laboratory games, but the core logic supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like dilemmas.

- source: Lehmann, L., & Keller, L. (2006). The evolution of cooperation and altruism - a general framework and a classification of models. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and altruism can only evolve when at least one of four general conditions is met: (1) direct benefits to the actor, (2) repeated interactions with information about partner behavior, (3) preferential interactions with kin, or (4) genetic linkage between recognition and helping traits (greenbeard effect). Punishment, coercion, and policing are highlighted as mechanisms that can shift the cost-benefit ratio in favor of cooperation, making it more likely to evolve and persist. The paper reviews and classifies a large number of theoretical models, showing that all can be mapped onto these four categories. It emphasizes that punishment is effective in promoting cooperation when it increases the cost of defection relative to cooperation, but notes that the stability of punishment depends on its own cost and on whether it is linked to cooperation genetically or behaviorally.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase the efficiency (fitness, group payoff, or productivity) of cooperative systems by making defection less attractive. It does not provide quantitative predictions for specific game designs, but it clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its ability to alter the cost-benefit ratio and on the presence of at least one of the four general conditions for cooperation. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments if it is not prohibitively costly and if it is implemented in a way that aligns with one of the four evolutionary pathways. However, the paper does not address specific design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, chat) in the context of laboratory or field experiments, so its guidance is primarily mechanistic and qualitative.

- source: Jin, Q., Wang, Z., Wang, Z., & Wang, Y. L. (2012). Strategy changing penalty promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a strategy changing penalty (a form of endogenous punishment for switching strategies) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game increases the fraction of cooperators. Both higher penalty rates and longer memory (the period after which the penalty is lifted if the player does not change strategy again) monotonically promote cooperation, with the effect saturating at high memory. The mechanism works by inducing heterogeneity in fitness coefficients, which helps cooperator clusters survive and expand. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that endogenous penalty mechanisms can increase cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the punishment is not peer-to-peer but is an automatic penalty for changing strategy. Thus, while the findings suggest that certain forms of punishment can promote cooperation, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The main value is as adjacent, mechanistic support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, especially when combined with memory or forgiveness.

- source: Vukov, J., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2012). Cognitive strategies take advantage of the cooperative potential of heterogeneous networks. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to use reactive strategies (punishing defectors and rewarding cooperators based on memory of past actions) leads to high and robust levels of cooperation in scale-free networks, even when participation costs are present. The effect is much stronger than for unconditional strategies. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative acts, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is based on the prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, but the mechanisms of punishment/retaliation and reward are closely related. The findings suggest that cognitive strategies can overcome the negative effects of participation costs and defector invasion on cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment/retaliation mechanisms (as part of cognitive/reactive strategies) can sustain high cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, even with participation costs and frequent defector invasion. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The main signal is that punishment/retaliation can be highly effective in maintaining cooperation under adverse conditions, especially when players can condition their behavior on others' past actions. The findings are most relevant for settings with network structure, memory, and the possibility of both punishment and reward.

- source: Helbing, D., & Johansson, A. (2010). Cooperation, Norms, and Revolutions: A Unified Game-Theoretical Approach. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the dynamics and outcomes of cooperation and norm establishment in multi-population games depend critically on the payoff structure (game type), the relative power of populations, and initial conditions. In multi-population prisoner's dilemma games, defection dominates regardless of power. In stag hunt games, shared norms can emerge, often set by the more powerful or initially larger group, but sometimes by the weaker group depending on initial conditions. In snowdrift games, polarization and abrupt 'revolutionary' transitions can occur. Costly punishment can transform a prisoner's dilemma into a stag hunt, supporting norm establishment. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral equilibria and norm dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment, payoff structure, and group power shape the emergence of cooperation and norms in environments related to, but not identical with, public goods games. It suggests that punishment can promote norm establishment by changing the effective game structure, but does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment can shift equilibria toward more cooperative outcomes in some settings, but does not quantify efficiency gains or losses. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of game structure and group asymmetries as moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & Bshary, R. (2011). Resolving the iterated prisoner's dilemma: theory and reality. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that while the strict assumptions of the IPD are rarely met in nature, the pay-off structure (temptation to cheat, mutual investment) is relevant to many real-world cooperative interactions. Punishment (negative reciprocity) is identified as a mechanism that can sustain cooperation in IPD-like games, as illustrated by the cleaner fish example, where dominant individuals punish defectors to promote future cooperation. However, the paper does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is sustained.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation in iterated social dilemmas, especially when cooperation is not all-or-nothing and when partner choice or punishment is possible. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically vary game design dimensions. As such, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, but it does support the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation in IPD-like settings.

- source: Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2005). Evolution of indirect reciprocity. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity, supported by reputation mechanisms and sometimes by costly punishment (altruistic punishment), can stabilize cooperation in social dilemmas. Theoretical models show that discriminating strategies (which punish defectors by withholding help) can be evolutionarily stable under certain conditions, especially when information about others' behavior is available. The paper reviews experimental evidence that reputation and information about others' past actions increase cooperation, and that costly punishment is observed in public goods and related games. However, the paper does not report direct effects of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games; instead, it focuses on the conditions for the evolution and maintenance of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical support for the idea that punishment and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when information about others' behavior is available. It suggests that punishment (including costly punishment) can help stabilize cooperation, which may lead to higher efficiency, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency outcomes. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the importance of information, group size, and the cost/benefit structure, but does not allow direct mapping from control efficiency to treatment efficiency with punishment enabled. Its value is mainly in mechanism and moderator identification rather than direct effect estimation.

- source: Boehm, C. (1997). Impact of the human egalitarian syndrome on Darwinian selection mechanics. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that in prehistoric human forager societies, egalitarian social norms—enforced by moralistic punishment and social sanctions—dampened within-group competition and amplified between-group variation. This, in turn, empowered group selection and enabled the evolution and maintenance of altruistic and cooperative behaviors, despite the presence of free riders. The author argues that active punishment and social isolation of noncooperators reduced the free-rider problem, making group-beneficial traits more likely to persist. However, these arguments are theoretical and based on ethnographic and evolutionary reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and social sanctions can promote cooperation and suppress free riding in group contexts, which is relevant to public goods game environments. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify how game design dimensions (such as punishment cost, player count, or MPCR) would moderate these effects. Its value for prediction is limited to supporting the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, not for estimating efficiency outcomes in specific game designs.

- source: Chen, Y. S., Lin, H., & Wu, C. X. (2007). Evolution of prisoner's dilemma strategies on scale-free networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) lower average degree and higher clustering coefficient in scale-free networks promote higher cooperation frequency in the iterated prisoner's dilemma; (2) introducing a punishment mechanism, where unsatisfied cooperators can sever and rewire links to defectors with some probability, further increases the frequency and persistence of cooperation. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all results are based on simulation of strategy frequencies and network structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation frequency in networked iterated prisoner's dilemma environments, especially when combined with certain network structures. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote cooperation, but should be used with caution for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Ostrom, E. (2009). Building Trust to Solve Commons Dilemmas: Taking Small Steps to Test an Evolving Theory of Collective Action. *Games, Groups, and the Global Good*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in commons dilemmas is highly context-dependent and that institutional features such as communication, information about others' actions, and the ability to sanction (punish or reward) are critical for building trust and reciprocity, which in turn support higher cooperation. It reviews evidence that allowing punishment or graduated sanctions in lab and field settings increases cooperation and joint outcomes, but does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff. The chapter also notes that the effect of design dimensions (e.g., group size, repetition, communication, sanctioning) on cooperation is variable and context-specific.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that enabling punishment or sanctions in public goods or CPR-like games increases cooperation and can improve group outcomes, especially when combined with communication and information about others' actions. However, it does not provide direct, quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff effects, nor does it systematically analyze the effect of specific game design dimensions on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the general expectation that punishment mechanisms (especially graduated sanctions) are associated with higher cooperation and likely higher efficiency, but the evidence is indirect and not parameterized for specific game designs.

- source: Rosas, A. (2008). Multilevel selection and human altruism. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of human altruism depends on the degree to which individuals (or their social/psychological mechanisms) can control assortative grouping, such as by punishing defectors and maintaining group cohesion. It claims that in human societies, mechanisms like punishment, reputation, and social norms can, in principle, prevent subversion from within and make altruism individually adaptive. The discussion references public goods games and the importance of punishment for sustaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and social mechanisms might enable stable cooperation in public goods settings by engineering assortative grouping and deterring free-riders. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or variants. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of mechanisms (like punishment and reputation) that allow groups to control subversion from within, suggesting that such mechanisms could, in principle, improve group outcomes. For direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in experimental public goods games, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Giardini, F. (2012). Deterrence and transmission as mechanisms ensuring reliability of gossip. *Cognitive Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that deterrence (punishment for unreliable gossip) and transmission (the ability to hide the source of information) jointly ensure the reliability and evolutionary stability of gossip. It suggests that the threat of punishment alone would make gossip too risky, but the ability to transmit information as 'reputation' (with an unknown source) allows individuals to avoid direct responsibility and punishment. The paper references agent-based simulations showing that when agents can only use direct experience, average payoffs are lower than when communication (gossip/reputation) is allowed. It also cites experimental economics studies where reputation mechanisms increase cooperation and prevent overuse of public resources. However, these findings are not original to this paper and are used to support the theoretical argument.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and reputation mechanisms may affect cooperation and, indirectly, group efficiency in social dilemmas. It does not provide direct empirical evidence or parameterized results that can be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it supports the general idea that punishment (deterrence) can sustain cooperation, but only when paired with mechanisms (like transmission/reputation) that allow information to spread without excessive risk to informers. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment and reputation mechanisms can increase cooperation and possibly efficiency, but the effect depends on the balance between deterrence and the ability to avoid punishment via transmission. The lack of direct empirical or payoff data limits its utility for precise prediction.

- source: Ishida, J. (2009). Incentives in academics: Collaboration under weak complementarities. *Labour Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated game with voluntary team formation, optimal contracts may use high-powered team incentives and low-powered individual incentives to exploit peer monitoring and reputation effects. This can make collaboration sustainable and efficient even with weak complementarities, overcoming the free-rider problem present in static settings. However, when the opportunity to collaborate is frequent and agents are patient, the optimal contract may under-incentivize individual effort, leading to inefficiency when collaboration is not possible. Thus, repeated interaction and peer sanctions can both improve and harm efficiency, depending on parameter values (discount factor, frequency of collaboration opportunities, degree of complementarities).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling peer sanctions (punishment via reputation loss and exclusion from collaboration) in repeated public-goods-like environments depends on the structure of incentives and the frequency of collaboration opportunities. When peer sanctions are possible, efficiency can increase by overcoming free-rider problems, but if individual incentives are weakened too much to make peer sanctions more effective, efficiency can decrease when collaboration is not possible. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not unconditionally positive: it depends on whether the contract design balances team and individual incentives, the patience of agents, and the frequency of opportunities for collaboration. The model is adjacent to public goods games, with peer sanctions implemented via exclusion and reputation rather than explicit costly punishment.

- source: Chalub, F. A. C. C., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2006). The evolution of norms. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that, in a two-level selection model with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, simple and unambiguous social norms emerge that maximize group efficiency (average payoff). The dominant norm (10011001) enforces cooperation by rewarding help to good individuals and punishing refusal to help good individuals or helping bad individuals. This norm allows for forgiveness and rapid recovery of reputation, but also implacable punishment for bad actions. Under this norm, cooperative strategies (CO, OR) dominate, and islands reach maximal possible fitness. The model demonstrates that the evolution of such norms is robust across a wide range of benefit parameters (b), and that a small amount of migration between islands is essential for the evolution of efficient norms and strategies. The findings support the idea that reputation-based punishment and norm enforcement can sustain high efficiency in group-structured populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that reputation-based punishment (loss of reputation for non-cooperation) in repeated social dilemma games can lead to the evolution of norms that maximize group efficiency. The model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a give-and-receive game with binary actions and reputation updating. However, the core mechanism—punishment via reputation loss, indirect reciprocity, and norm enforcement—closely parallels the logic of punishment in public goods games. The findings suggest that, when such mechanisms are present, enabling punishment (via reputation or direct sanctions) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The results are robust across a range of benefit-to-cost ratios and do not depend on fine-tuning of parameters. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that introducing effective punishment or norm enforcement in repeated social dilemma environments will increase efficiency, especially when group structure and reputation are salient.

- source: Takahashi, S. (2010). Community enforcement when players observe partners' past play. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that community enforcement—whereby third parties punish cheaters based on first-order information about partners' past play—can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in repeated prisoner's dilemma games with random matching. Under the independent and indifferent equilibrium concept, for sufficiently high discount factors, any symmetric group payoff between 0 (full defection) and 1 (full cooperation) can be achieved. The paper also shows that these results are robust to noise in actions or records, and that the equilibrium construction extends to finite populations. The threshold for sustaining cooperation does not depend on population size under this mechanism, in contrast to contagious equilibria. The paper provides explicit equilibrium constructions and analyzes the stability of cooperation under shocks and noise.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with random matching and first-order information, enabling community enforcement (punishment by third parties based on partners' past play) can increase group efficiency up to the full cooperation level, provided players are sufficiently patient. The results are robust to population size and some forms of noise. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in analogous public-goods-game-like environments, enabling punishment mechanisms (even with limited information) can substantially increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, especially when the discount factor is high. The paper also clarifies that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the structure of the stage game (supermodular vs. submodular) and the information available to players.

- source: Chassang, S. (2010). Building Routines: Learning, Cooperation, and the Dynamics of Incomplete Relational Contracts. *American Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, in repeated relationships with incomplete information about how to cooperate, initial stages are characterized by inefficient punishment (exit) due to imperfect monitoring and the need to incentivize information revelation. As players learn and routines are established, the need for punishment disappears and efficiency increases. However, the cost of inducing further information revelation can outweigh the benefits, leading to path dependence and persistent inefficiency: partnerships may settle into suboptimal routines even when more efficient cooperation is possible. The extent of inefficiency depends on the magnitude of incentive conflicts and the cost of aligning incentives. The model provides qualitative predictions about the efficiency costs of punishment and the conditions under which punishment is required or can be avoided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment (or inefficient exit) in repeated games with incomplete information can reduce efficiency, especially in the early stages of a relationship. The efficiency cost of punishment is highest when players are still learning about how to cooperate, and disappears once routines are established and monitoring becomes perfect. The model suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment depends on the information structure and the stage of the relationship: punishment is necessary to induce information revelation but is always inefficient compared to the complete information benchmark. The findings imply that, in environments similar to public goods games with incomplete information and learning, enabling punishment may initially reduce efficiency, but its necessity diminishes as learning occurs. The model also highlights the importance of path dependence and the possibility of persistent inefficiency due to early random events. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is context-dependent and may be negative or mixed, especially when learning is costly and incomplete.

- source: Frean, M. R., & Abraham, E. R. (2004). Adaptation and enslavement in endosymbiont-host associations - art. no. 051913. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The main finding is that in a two-species repeated cooperation game with differential adaptation rates, the slower-adapting species (host) evolves to provide just enough cooperation to keep the faster-adapting species (symbiont) maximally cooperative. This 'miser-slave' dynamic leads to highly asymmetric payoffs: hosts receive nearly all the benefit, while symbionts receive very little, even though they are highly cooperative. The total group payoff (efficiency) is much less than the maximum possible (full mutual cooperation), because the host withholds cooperation. The model provides a theoretical explanation for observed asymmetries in natural symbioses, where hosts benefit much more than symbionts. The findings are robust across a range of cost-benefit ratios and are supported by both analytic and simulation results.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical mechanism showing that, in repeated cooperation games with contingent strategies and differential adaptation rates, the introduction of a contingent response (analogous to punishment or reward) by one party can lead to highly asymmetric efficiency outcomes. Specifically, if the 'punishing' party adapts more slowly, it can extract maximal cooperation from the other party while contributing minimally itself, resulting in low overall efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the structure of contingent responses and adaptation rates can strongly moderate the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. However, the model is not a standard public goods game, and the 'punishment' is implemented as a contingent reward/withholding rather than explicit costly punishment. Thus, while the findings are informative about the potential for punishment/contingency to reduce efficiency via asymmetry, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs with explicit punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Fishman, M. A. (2006). Involuntary defection and the evolutionary origins of empathy. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated dyadic cooperation games with involuntary defection, unconditional punishment (retaliation for any defection) undermines cooperation and reduces efficiency, as it leads to cycles of mutual defection. In contrast, an empathic retaliator strategy—where punishment is withheld if the opponent's defection follows one's own involuntary defection—can be evolutionarily stable and support high efficiency, especially when the expected number of repeated interactions is large. The stability and efficiency of cooperation depend on the cost of helping, the probability of being able to help, and the expected number of interaction cycles. The paper provides explicit conditions and payoff matrices for these outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms critically affects efficiency in repeated cooperation games. Specifically, unconditional punishment (retaliation) in the presence of involuntary defection leads to lower efficiency, while empathic (conditional) punishment can sustain high efficiency if repeated interactions are frequent. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on whether punishment is unconditional or conditional/empathic, the frequency of repeated interactions, and the possibility of involuntary defection. However, the model is not a public goods game, and punishment is not costly in the sense of standard PGG experiments; rather, it is refusal to help. Thus, direct quantitative predictions for PGGs should be made with caution, but the qualitative insight is that punishment can either harm or help efficiency depending on its design and the game context.

- source: Robert, J. M., Otrok, H., Quttoum, A. N., & Boukhris, R. (2012). A distributed resource management model for Virtual Private Networks: Tit-for-Tat strategies. *Computer Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: Simulation results show that classical Tit-for-Tat fails in this asynchronous, stochastic setting, leading to deadlocks and low total gain. Generous Tit-for-Tat avoids deadlocks and achieves high group gain, but is vulnerable to exploitation by selfish participants. Altruistic and reputation-based strategies can effectively punish selfish participants, forcing them to cooperate to receive benefits. The best strategies (reputation-based and altruistic-reputation) allow cooperative participants to achieve near-optimal total gain, while selfish participants are excluded unless they match cooperative behavior. The findings are robust across variations in the number of cooperative/selfish participants and the probability of offering resources.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (retaliation, exclusion, reputation-based refusal) can substantially increase group efficiency in repeated resource-sharing games with asynchronous moves and stochastic cooperation. The results are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment or exclusion in public-goods-like environments, especially when cooperation is probabilistic and not always feasible. The specific game structure differs from standard PGGs (asynchronous, resource lending/borrowing, not simultaneous contribution), but the payoff logic and the effect of punishment on group efficiency are closely analogous. The paper supports the prediction that enabling effective punishment or exclusion will increase efficiency, especially when selfish behavior is otherwise possible.

- source: Gutnisky, D. A., & Zanutto, B. S. (2004). Cooperation in the iterated prisoner's dilemma is learned by operant conditioning mechanisms. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The operant conditioning model (ZL) can learn to cooperate in the IPD and achieves high average payoffs against a variety of strategies. In evolutionary simulations, ZL can dominate or survive in populations depending on the mix of strategies and the presence of noise (random moves). However, ZL is always slightly less efficient than non-learning strategies because it requires time to learn optimal responses. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on reinforcement learning from payoffs. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning mechanisms can support cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, but do not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide insight into how learning mechanisms (operant conditioning) can support cooperation and high payoffs in repeated dyadic dilemmas (IPD). The absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means that the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that learning-based strategies can achieve high efficiency, but may be less efficient than fixed strategies in the short run due to learning costs. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, contextual support.

- source: Rob, R., & Yang, H. X. (2010). Long-term relationships as safeguards. *Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous relationship formation and dissolution can sustain cooperation and high efficiency in a repeated prisoner's dilemma, but only if the fraction of always-defect (bad) types is neither too low nor too high. When the fraction of bad types is in an intermediate range, a 'good equilibrium' exists in which strategic players cooperate, and social welfare is maximized at the lower end of this range. If the fraction of bad types is too low, there is no credible punishment for defection, and cooperation unravels. If too high, the probability of forming a cooperative relationship is too low, and defection dominates. The model also characterizes mixed strategy equilibria and shows that welfare can drop discontinuously as parameters cross critical thresholds. The main outcome is social welfare (efficiency), which is explicitly calculated as a function of the type distribution and model parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that endogenous social structure (relationship formation and dissolution) can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency, but only under certain conditions on the population's type distribution. The efficiency of the 'treatment' (i.e., the equilibrium with endogenous punishment via exclusion) is maximized when the fraction of bad types is just high enough to make the threat of exclusion credible, but not so high as to make cooperation infeasible. The model does not map directly onto standard public goods games with explicit punishment options, but it is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated games with endogenous partner choice or ostracism mechanisms. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency is non-monotonic and highly sensitive to the underlying population structure. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the presence and credibility of punishment (even if not costly or explicit) can have strong, but non-linear, effects on efficiency.

- source: Ho, T. H. (1996). Finite automata play repeated prisoner's dilemma with information processing costs. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
