  findings: The main findings are: (1) In a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with boundedly rational agents (finite automata), cooperation and high efficiency can evolve even from hostile initial conditions, as long as there are no costs for strategy complexity. (2) Imposing a cost on the size of the automaton (i.e., the 'storage' or monitoring cost) destroys cooperation and reduces efficiency, with populations converging to simple, defecting strategies. (3) Imposing a cost on the frequency of switching states (i.e., 'retrieval' or attention cost) does not harm, and may even promote, cooperation and efficiency, especially if the cost is quadratic. (4) The results are robust to a range of simulation parameters. (5) The findings suggest that the form of cognitive or implementation cost is a critical moderator of whether punishment-like strategies (contingent defection) can sustain cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player games structurally similar to public goods games (but not group PGGs), the ability to implement contingent punishment (e.g., Tit-for-Tat or more sophisticated automata) is crucial for sustaining high efficiency. However, if the game design imposes costs on monitoring or strategy complexity (analogous to high punishment cost or high cognitive burden), efficiency collapses and simple, non-cooperative strategies dominate. In contrast, costs on the frequency of switching (analogous to costs for changing behavior or attention) do not undermine efficiency. For prediction, this suggests that the presence and form of punishment, and especially the cost structure for implementing contingent strategies, are key moderators of the efficiency effect. The results are most directly relevant to two-player, long-horizon, repeated games with deterministic feedback and no communication.

- source: Lipman, B. L., & Wang, R. Q. (2000). Switching costs in frequently repeated games. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that adding small switching costs to frequently repeated games can overturn standard results from finitely repeated game theory. In the Prisoners' Dilemma, switching costs can enable cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) if the incentive to punish is greater than the incentive to cheat, but can also make cooperation impossible if the reverse is true. In coordination games, switching costs can make commitment to efficient outcomes credible, sometimes leading to unique, efficient equilibria. In some games, switching costs allow for more complex credible threats, which can result in equilibrium payoffs above Stackelberg levels for some players. The overall effect of switching costs on efficiency is thus mixed and highly context-dependent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, switching costs that make threats and commitments credible) on efficiency in repeated games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure and the relative size of incentives to cooperate, cheat, or punish. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of enabling punishment (or switching costs) on efficiency cannot be determined solely from the presence of punishment, but must also account for the specific incentive structure of the game. In some cases, enabling punishment (via switching costs) increases efficiency by sustaining cooperation; in others, it has no effect or even reduces efficiency by making punishment non-credible. The results are not directly in public goods games, but the mechanisms are adjacent and informative for settings where punishment credibility and switching costs are relevant.

- source: Bicchieri, C., Duffy, J., & Tolle, G. (2004). Trust among strangers. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in one-shot trust games, strategies that do not trust or reciprocate dominate, resulting in low average payoffs. When the game is repeated for more rounds (increasing the shadow of the future), conditional strategies that punish defection (e.g., grim trigger, hopeful) become viable and eventually dominate the population, leading to the emergence of a norm of trust and reciprocation and higher average payoffs. The transition from non-cooperation to cooperation occurs as the number of rounds increases beyond a threshold (typically r > 2 or 3). The stable outcome is a polymorphic population of conditional strategies supporting trust/reciprocation. The results are robust to initial conditions and to the inclusion of stochasticity in the evolutionary process.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments, the length of the repeated interaction (number of rounds) is a critical moderator of the emergence of cooperation and higher efficiency. When the game is one-shot or very short, efficiency remains low due to dominance of non-cooperative strategies. As the number of rounds increases, conditional strategies that implement punishment by withholding trust/reciprocation after defection can support the emergence of trust and higher efficiency. The findings suggest that, in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, repeated interaction alone can enable the emergence of efficient, cooperative outcomes if conditional strategies are available. The model does not directly manipulate or analyze explicit punishment cost or technology, but the logic of conditional strategies is analogous to endogenous punishment via future play. The results are most relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated trust games as a function of the number of rounds and the available strategy set.

- source: Kraines, D. P., & Kraines, V. Y. (2000). Natural selection of memory-one strategies for the iterated prisoner's dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with memory-one strategies and evolutionary dynamics, the population tends to evolve toward a small set of stable strategies. The Pavlov (Win-Stay Lose-Switch) strategy is particularly robust, achieving high average payoffs and being resistant to invasion by other strategies, especially in the presence of noise. The analysis partitions the space of strategies into basins of attraction: cooperative (high payoff), defecting (low payoff), alternating (zero payoff), and a small equilibrium plane. The size of these basins depends on the initial conditions and the payoff matrix. The paper also shows that strategies that combine trust with readiness to retaliate (but not excessive forgiveness) are most successful. While explicit punishment mechanisms are not modeled, the evolutionary success of strategies that retaliate after being exploited is highlighted.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or explicit punishment institutions, it analyzes the efficiency (average payoff) of strategies in a closely related repeated social dilemma (the IPD) under evolutionary dynamics. The findings suggest that strategies incorporating contingent retaliation (analogous to punishment) can sustain high efficiency, but only if they avoid excessive forgiveness or spite. The mapping of strategy space to efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the likely efficiency of repeated games with different behavioral rules, especially regarding the role of contingent sanctions. However, the lack of explicit group structure, multi-player interactions, and institutional punishment limits direct transfer to public goods games.

- source: Amegashie, J. A., & Runkel, M. (2012). The Paradox of Revenge in Conflicts. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to conventional wisdom, the desire for revenge does not always escalate conflict. In a two-period contest model, the anticipation of revenge (self-deterrence effect) can lead factions to reduce their initial effort, resulting in lower total conflict costs (higher efficiency) than in a setting without revenge motives. The paradox is most likely when the benefit of revenge is highly elastic to past destruction or when there are asymmetries in the value of revenge or resource valuation between factions. The model provides explicit comparative statics and closed-form solutions for aggregate effort with and without revenge, showing conditions under which efficiency is improved by the possibility of revenge.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the introduction of a punishment/revenge mechanism can, under certain conditions, increase efficiency (reduce total cost) in conflict-like games by inducing self-deterrence. While the setting is a two-player, two-period conflict game (not a standard public goods game), the mechanism is closely related to punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, when the threat of punishment is credible and especially when the benefit of punishment is highly sensitive to prior harm, the net effect on efficiency can be positive. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the possibility that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially in asymmetric or highly elastic settings, but the result is context-dependent and may not generalize to all PGG-like environments.

- source: Masuda, N. (2012). Ingroup favoritism and intergroup cooperation under indirect reciprocity based on group reputation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of cooperation in group-structured indirect reciprocity games depends critically on the social norm used for reputation assignment and the frequency of intragroup versus intergroup interactions. Full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency, with payoff b-c per round) is stable only when all observers use a 'serious' social norm (standing or judging) for all types of interactions. If observers use double standards (different norms for ingroup and outgroup interactions), then ingroup favoritism emerges, leading to lower efficiency (payoff reduced by the fraction of intergroup interactions). The key condition for any cooperation to be stable is b*r_in > c, where r_in is the probability of intragroup interaction. The model shows that changing the social norm (e.g., forcing consistency across interaction types) can shift the equilibrium from inefficient ingroup favoritism to efficient full cooperation. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward, but reputation loss/gain serves a similar function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with group structure. It shows that the efficiency outcome (full cooperation, partial, or ingroup favoritism) is determined by the social norm for reputation assignment and the structure of interactions (r_in). In the absence of explicit punishment or reward, but with reputation-based indirect reciprocity, efficiency is maximized only if the social norm is consistent and 'serious' (standing/judging) across all interaction types. If the game design allows or encourages double standards (different norms for ingroup/outgroup), expect lower efficiency due to ingroup favoritism. The model's efficiency results are directly parameterized by the benefit-to-cost ratio and the frequency of intragroup interaction, which can be mapped to MPCR and group structure in PGG-like games. However, the absence of explicit punishment/reward means the results are most applicable to games where reputation is the main enforcement mechanism.

- source: KRAINES, D., & KRAINES, V. (1993). LEARNING TO COOPERATE WITH PAVLOV - AN ADAPTIVE STRATEGY FOR THE ITERATED PRISONERS-DILEMMA WITH NOISE. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that Pavlovian strategies, which adapt their probability of cooperation based on positive and negative payoffs (interpreted as rewards and punishments), can achieve high average payoffs and are robust to noise in the iterated Prisoner's Dilemma. Pavlov can exploit overly cooperative opponents (like All-C), is not easily exploited except by always-defect strategies, and can recover mutual cooperation after errors more quickly than Tit-for-Tat (TFT). The optimal learning rate (parameter n) balances exploitability and speed of learning, with n=3 or 4 performing best in simulations. The paper provides detailed payoff tables and Markov chain analyses for various strategy matchups, but does not study group interactions, explicit peer punishment, or public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in public goods games, as it does not study group games, peer punishment, or any of the relevant game design dimensions. However, it is informative about how strategies that adapt to positive and negative payoffs (interpreted as endogenous rewards and punishments) can lead to high efficiency and mutual cooperation in repeated dyadic dilemmas. The findings suggest that adaptive learning can be robust to noise and can outperform simple reciprocity (TFT) in some environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address the effect of enabling punishment mechanisms or any of the design dimensions in public goods games.

- source: Zimmermann, M. G., & Eguíluz, V. M. (2005). Cooperation, social networks, and the emergence of leadership in a prisoner's dilemma with adaptive local interactions - art. no. 056118. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that network adaptation (agents breaking unsatisfactory links and seeking new partners) enables the emergence of highly cooperative, high-efficiency steady states in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma. In these states, most agents cooperate, and the group achieves high average payoffs, even though defectors individually do better than cooperators. The system is robust to a range of parameters, but can be destabilized by noise or by the loss of key cooperative leaders, leading to cascades of defection and efficiency collapse. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or reward mechanisms, but the ability to break links with defectors functions as a form of social sanction/ostracism. The results show that such adaptive partner selection can substitute for explicit punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that adaptive partner selection (ostracism, link breaking) in networked social dilemmas can dramatically increase group efficiency, even in the absence of explicit costly punishment or reward. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that enabling mechanisms for social exclusion or network adaptation (even if not labeled as 'punishment') can have similar or greater positive effects on efficiency as explicit punishment in public goods games. However, the model is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and the 'punishment' is indirect (ostracism, not costly reduction of others' payoffs). The results are most relevant for predicting the effects of partner choice, ostracism, or network adaptation, and less directly for classic costly punishment. The paper is highly informative for adjacent mechanisms but should be used with caution for direct predictions about explicit punishment in PGGs.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). Persistent parochialism: trust and exclusion in ethnic networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that parochial networks can achieve higher efficiency (average payoff) than anonymous markets in social dilemma environments, due to better information and informal enforcement (via monitoring and exclusion). However, the relationship between parochialism and efficiency is non-linear: too little parochialism leads to poor information and low trust, while too much parochialism reduces trading opportunities and group size, also lowering efficiency. The model includes a monitoring/inspection strategy (analogous to punishment), which, when information is sufficiently accurate, supports a stable trust equilibrium with higher payoffs. The optimal efficiency is achieved at an intermediate level of parochialism, balancing enforcement and trading opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (monitoring, exclusion) on efficiency in social dilemma games depends on the information structure and group composition. Efficiency is maximized at an intermediate level of parochialism, where information is good enough to support trust and informal enforcement, but group size and trading opportunities are not too restricted. The model suggests that enabling punishment/monitoring can increase efficiency relative to a control (anonymous market) when information is sufficiently accurate and group size is within a viable range. However, excessive exclusion (high parochialism) can reduce efficiency by limiting opportunities. The findings are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment is implemented via monitoring and exclusion, rather than costly direct punishment as in standard PGGs.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2007). Indirect reciprocity in asymmetric interactions: when apparent altruism facilitates profitable exploitation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric games where only one party (the actor) can help or exploit, and the other (the recipient) can only choose whether to interact, a form of indirect reciprocity can evolve. Actors help when the gains from exploitation are low to maintain a positive image, which encourages recipients to approach them in the future, allowing profitable exploitation when the gains are high. This strategy is stable and increases the long-term average payoff for both actors and recipients, provided certain conditions on observability, state-switching, and payoff parameters are met. The model shows that efficiency (average group payoff) is higher under these conditions than if actors always exploit or if recipients always avoid interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in asymmetric games with image scoring and conditional approach, mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, avoidance of exploiters) can increase efficiency by supporting conditional cooperation. The findings suggest that when harmful behavior is observable and actors' states vary, efficiency is maximized for intermediate values of key parameters (cost of harm, switching probability, observability). While not a PGG or direct punishment setting, the model is adjacent and supports the idea that social mechanisms that deter exploitation (even without direct punishment) can improve group efficiency. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling mechanisms for reputation-based avoidance or indirect sanctions can increase efficiency, especially when exploitation is not always profitable and can be detected.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2002). From parasitism to mutualism: partner control in asymmetric interactions. *Ecology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The model demonstrates that in asymmetric interactions, the ability of the exploited party to terminate the interaction acts as a deterrent to exploitation, increasing the efficiency (joint payoff) of the system. As the exploiter's power to prolong the interaction increases, exploitation rises, the victim's payoff falls (eventually becoming negative), and the system shifts from mutualism to parasitism. The temptation to cheat (benefit/cost ratio) and the rate at which mutualistic benefits diminish also modulate these outcomes. The model is supported qualitatively by empirical data from cleaner-fish systems, where larger clients (with more to gain from cleaning) experience less exploitation and longer interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing the exploited party to control the duration of interaction (analogous to exit, partner choice, or punishment in PGG-like games) can increase efficiency by deterring exploitation. While not a PGG or direct punishment experiment, the model is relevant for predicting that increasing the ability of group members to sanction or exit (or otherwise control exploiters) will generally increase group efficiency, especially when the temptation to cheat is not overwhelming. The model also suggests that efficiency gains from punishment or partner control are greatest when the benefits of cooperation are high and the costs of cheating are high relative to the exploiter's gain. However, the model does not address repeated group interactions, explicit punishment costs, or the full range of PGG design dimensions, so its predictive value is indirect.

- source: Bravo, G. (2011). Agents' beliefs and the evolution of institutions for common-pool resource management. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The key finding is that enabling agents to build management institutions in a CPR environment leads to much higher efficiency and group payoffs compared to scenarios where agents act only on individual beliefs. In the base (no-institution) model, payoffs are 12–35% of the optimum; with institution building, payoffs rise to 72–91% of the optimum. The effect is robust to changes in cost, resource abundance, and agent tolerance. The models suggest that the feedback loop between beliefs and institutions is critical: institutions prevent the selection of purely self-interested, over-exploiting agents, allowing cooperation and sustainable resource use to persist. The results are consistent with empirical findings from CPR experiments and field studies, though the models do not include explicit punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong evidence that the introduction of effective, collectively agreed-upon management institutions in CPR/public-goods-like environments can transform group efficiency from low (tragedy of the commons) to high (near-optimal). While the models do not include explicit punishment, the institution acts as a binding rule, functionally similar to enforced cooperation. The results suggest that, in designs where institution-building is possible and effective, one should predict a large positive jump in efficiency relative to the control. However, the absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means the findings are most directly relevant to institution-building or rule-setting treatments, not to punishment per se.

- source: MacLeod, W. B. (2007). Reputations, relationships, and contract enforcement. *Journal of Economic Literature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that informal enforcement mechanisms—such as reputational punishment for breach—can support efficient outcomes in repeated interactions, especially when formal legal enforcement is costly or unavailable. The efficiency of punishment depends on contract form: warranty and bonus contracts can achieve first-best efficiency under certain conditions, while simple fixed-price contracts relying on reputation alone are often inefficient due to risk of reputation loss even when performance is adequate. The paper predicts that warranty contracts are more efficient for 'normal' goods (where bad outcomes are rare), while bonus contracts are more efficient for 'innovative' goods (where good outcomes are rare). The efficiency of punishment/reward mechanisms is moderated by the observability of breach, the frequency of trade, the quality of law, and the allocation of reputational capital. The paper also discusses how formal and informal enforcement can be substitutes or complements, and how the choice of contract form and enforcement mechanism affects overall surplus and welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment and reward mechanisms in repeated games and contract-like environments. It suggests that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the contract form (e.g., whether punishment is triggered by observable breach, whether rewards are available), the type of good or task (frequency and likelihood of good/bad outcomes), and the quality of formal enforcement. For public-goods-game-like environments, the results imply that informal punishment (reputation loss) can improve efficiency, but only under certain conditions—especially when breach is easily observed and when contract terms (such as warranties or bonuses) are used to align incentives. The paper also highlights that the efficiency gains from punishment may be limited or negative if punishment is triggered by events outside the agent's control, or if the risk of reputation loss is high even for compliant agents. Thus, for the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the idea that the design of punishment/reward mechanisms and the information structure are key moderators of efficiency effects.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2006). Unifying the theories of inclusive fitness and reciprocal altruism. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that the evolution of cooperation (altruism) in both kin and non-kin contexts can be predicted by a generalized Hamilton's rule that incorporates the covariance between genotypes and the cooperative behaviors of others, as well as nonadditive (synergistic) effects. In iterated prisoner's dilemma models, conditional strategies like tit-for-tat can increase in frequency when the benefit received from others' cooperation exceeds the cost, as predicted by Queller's rule. The same logic applies to mutualistic interactions between species. The paper shows that synergy (nonadditivity) and conditional behavior can both facilitate the evolution of cooperation, even in the absence of kinship or repeated interactions. Punishment is discussed as a possible mechanism for positive assortment but is not modeled. The findings are theoretical and do not provide empirical estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background for understanding how mechanisms like punishment, synergy, and conditional behavior can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency, but it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that any mechanism (including punishment) that increases positive assortment or synergy can increase the likelihood that cooperation evolves, but does not specify the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes in experimental settings. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is useful for mechanistic context but not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Golubski, A. J., & Klausmeier, C. A. (2010). Control in mutualisms Combined implications of partner choice and bargaining roles. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the ability to appropriate surplus (analogous to control over punishment/reward) and the ability to choose partners (partner choice) interact to determine the evolution of mutualism and the distribution of payoffs in mutualistic associations. When the guild with partner choice also receives the surplus, mutualistic strategies are favored and average payoffs are high. However, the presence of empty slots or high frequencies of exploiters can lead to cyclical dynamics where exploitative strategies temporarily increase, reducing average payoffs. The model predicts that partner choice is most effective at selecting against moderately exploitative partners, but highly exploitative partners can persist under some conditions. The overall effect on efficiency is mixed: partner choice and surplus control can increase efficiency, but not always, especially if exploiters are present or if association opportunities are limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that mechanisms analogous to punishment (partner choice, surplus appropriation) can increase efficiency (group payoff) in mutualism-like bargaining games, especially when combined. However, the effect is not universally positive: the presence of exploiters and the structure of association opportunities (e.g., empty slots) can lead to reduced efficiency or cyclical dynamics. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling partner choice and surplus control generally increases efficiency, but the effect may be moderated or reversed if exploiters can persist or if association opportunities are limited. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its predictions should be transferred with caution, but it provides informative indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment-like mechanisms improve or fail to improve efficiency.

- source: Wang, R. W., & Shi, L. (2010). The evolution of cooperation in asymmetric systems. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric cooperative systems, the net benefit (efficiency) to the recipient depends critically on the intrinsic growth rates of benefit and cost, the strength of density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors, and the availability of common resources. Stronger density-dependent interference competition among cooperative actors and higher initial relatedness (intrinsic contribution ratio) increase the probability of maintaining cooperation (positive efficiency). However, changes in these parameters or resource saturation can lead to a transition from cooperation to conflict, with net benefit becoming negative. The model also shows that recipient-enforced discrimination (punishing cheaters, rewarding cooperators) can increase initial relatedness and favor cooperation, but does not guarantee global stability. The system can exhibit oscillatory or chaotic dynamics between cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how efficiency in public-goods-like asymmetric systems is affected by the number of actors, resource saturation, and density-dependent competition. It suggests that efficiency gains from cooperation are not guaranteed by punishment or sanctions alone, but depend on ecological and structural parameters such as the strength of interference competition and resource availability. For prediction tasks, the model implies that enabling punishment (in the form of recipient-enforced discrimination) can increase efficiency only when it effectively increases initial relatedness and when density-dependent competition among actors is strong enough to prevent resource overexploitation. However, efficiency can decrease or oscillate if these conditions are not met. The findings are most relevant for systems with asymmetric roles and may be less directly transferable to standard symmetric PGG lab games.

- source: Nowak, M. A. (2006). Five rules for the evolution of cooperation. *Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: mpcr
  findings: The paper derives and summarizes simple mathematical rules for when cooperation can evolve and be maintained in populations, based on five mechanisms: kin selection, direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, network reciprocity, and group selection. For each, a threshold condition is given (e.g., benefit-to-cost ratio exceeding a critical value) that determines whether cooperation can increase average fitness or group payoff. The paper notes that, without such mechanisms, natural selection favors defection and reduces average fitness. Punishment is discussed as a factor that can enhance cooperation within these frameworks, but is not itself a primary mechanism for the evolution of cooperation. The paper does not provide direct predictions or data on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background on the conditions under which cooperation can increase group efficiency or average payoff, but does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment in public goods games or the impact of specific game design dimensions. It is useful for understanding the broader theoretical context and for mapping how punishment may interact with other mechanisms (e.g., indirect reciprocity, group selection), but does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or specific design features. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used as high-level theoretical context rather than as a source of empirical effect sizes or moderator effects.

- source: Lotem, A., Fishman, M. A., & Stone, L. (2003). From reciprocity to unconditional altruism through signalling benefits. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated cooperation games with individual variation in quality, the addition of signalling benefits to helping can lead to stable unconditional altruism among high-quality individuals. This occurs when the signalling benefit (S) is greater than the cost of helping (C) for high-quality individuals but not for low-quality individuals. Under these conditions, high-quality individuals cooperate unconditionally, while low-quality individuals defect or play tit-for-tat. The result is a higher average payoff (efficiency) for high-quality cooperators, and the overall level of cooperation and efficiency in the population is increased compared to models without signalling benefits. The model also shows that if signalling benefits become too cheap (affordable to all), the correlation between quality and cooperation disappears, undermining the signalling function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding signalling benefits to cooperation games can increase efficiency, especially among high-quality individuals. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in environments where helping can serve as a costly signal (and where the signalling benefit exceeds the cost for some but not all), enabling such signalling will increase the efficiency of cooperation relative to control. The model is adjacent to public goods games and punishment, but the payoff logic and ESS analysis are directly relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes when game design allows for signalling or reputation effects. However, the model does not directly manipulate punishment or public goods game parameters, so its predictive value is strongest for settings where signalling is a plausible mechanism.

- source: Pacheco, J. M., Santos, F. C., & Chalub, F. A. C. C. (2006). Stern-judging: A simple, successful norm which promotes cooperation under indirect reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that, in a multilevel evolutionary model of indirect reciprocity, the stern-judging norm—where helping good individuals or refusing help to bad individuals is rewarded, and the opposite is punished—emerges as the dominant and most robust social norm. This norm leads to maximized average payoffs for groups, indicating high efficiency in sustaining cooperation. The result is robust to different forms of group selection and inter-tribe conflict. The model suggests that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms are sufficient to promote and maintain high levels of cooperation and group welfare, even in the presence of errors and migration.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that simple, reputation-based punishment mechanisms (like stern-judging) can robustly increase group efficiency and cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is adjacent: it involves costly helping, reputation, and punishment via social norms. The findings suggest that, in environments where indirect reciprocity and reputation are salient, enabling punishment (in the form of social norm enforcement) is likely to increase group efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines. However, the model is abstract and does not manipulate most game design dimensions directly, so its predictive value is strongest for settings with similar indirect reciprocity structure and less for standard PGGs.

- source: Brandt, H., & Sigmund, K. (2004). The logic of reprobation: assessment and action rules for indirect reciprocation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost
  findings: The study shows that indirect reciprocity models with assessment rules that distinguish justified from unjustified defections (STANDING) are more effective at sustaining cooperation and suppressing defectors than simple scoring rules, especially in the presence of errors. However, even simple scoring can support cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and players have enough interactions. The model demonstrates that the structure of the population, the number of observed interactions, and the error rates in action and perception all influence the stability of cooperation. Analytical results show that, under certain assumptions about the distribution of the number of rounds, stable mixtures of discriminating and non-discriminating altruists can exist, leading to high average payoffs. The findings suggest that costly punishment (refusing to help) can sustain cooperation, but is less robust than non-costly forms (STANDING).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation (and thus efficiency) depends on whether punishment is costly, the benefit-to-cost ratio, error rates, and the number of interactions. While the model is not a standard PGG, the parallels are strong: refusing to help is analogous to punishing defectors, and the group payoff is analogous to efficiency. The results imply that costly punishment can increase efficiency, but is less robust than non-costly punishment, and that the details of the assessment and action rules, as well as the game structure (e.g., number of rounds, observation probability), are important moderators. For prediction, the paper supports the view that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is high and errors are not too frequent, but the effect is sensitive to the costliness and implementation of punishment.

- source: Ma, Y. P., Gonçalves, S., Mignot, S., Nadal, J. P., & Gordon, M. B. (2009). Cycles of cooperation and free-riding in social systems. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model predicts that, depending on parameters, communities can settle into fixed points (Nash equilibria) with stable mixtures of cooperators and free-riders, or exhibit persistent cycles in the fractions of cooperators and free-riders. The presence and width of the distribution of moral costs (idiosyncratic disutility for free-riding) are crucial for the emergence of cycles. Stronger social interactions (bandwagon effects) can stabilize high-cooperation equilibria and suppress cycles. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning actions, but models the deterrent to free-riding as an internalized moral cost. The efficiency or total surplus of the group is not directly compared across different institutional designs (e.g., with/without punishment), so the findings are not directly predictive for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not model explicit punishment or sanctions, nor does it report efficiency outcomes in the standard sense. However, it provides indirect evidence that internalized costs for free-riding (moral disapproval) can sustain cooperation and lead to complex group dynamics, including cycles. The model's structure and results may be informative for understanding how heterogeneity in social preferences and costs can affect cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative predictions for the effect of punishment interventions or for the mapping from control to treatment efficiency in PGGs.

- source: Aktipis, C. A. (2004). Know when to walk away: contingent movement and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that a simple contingent movement rule ('Walk Away') enables cooperation to evolve and persist in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, outperforming more cognitively complex strategies like TFT and PAVLOV under many conditions. Walk Away is robust to invasion, can invade populations of defectors at lower initial frequencies than other strategies, and is most successful when movement costs, error rates, and agent density are low. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and outcomes are measured by the evolutionary success of strategies (frequency in the population), not by direct group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that contingent movement (exit) can substitute for more complex memory-based or punishment-based strategies in promoting cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It does not study public goods games or explicit punishment, but shows that contingent movement (exit after defection) can promote cooperation and evolutionary success in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments. The findings suggest that mechanisms allowing agents to avoid defectors (analogous to exit or partner choice) can be highly effective, even without explicit punishment or memory. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, and does not manipulate punishment cost or technology, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited. The results are most relevant for understanding the role of partner choice, mobility, and spatial structure as alternatives or complements to punishment.

- source: Cordes, C., Richerson, P. J., & Schwesinger, G. (2010). How corporate cultures coevolve with the business environment: The case of firm growth crises and industry evolution. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation within firms is sustainable up to a critical group size, after which opportunistic behavior spreads and performance declines. In innovative, uncertain environments, cooperative cultures provide a competitive advantage, but as firms grow, the influence of informal leadership wanes and cooperation collapses. In mature, stable environments, large firms can succeed by implementing monitoring regimes and realizing economies of scale, even with more opportunistic employees. The model links these dynamics to firm growth crises, industry shakeouts, and the evolution of market structure. While the model is not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, it provides theoretical insight into how group size, monitoring, and social learning affect cooperation and firm performance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that group size and the presence or absence of monitoring (analogous to punishment) are key moderators of cooperation and efficiency in group settings. It suggests that in small groups, informal cooperation can sustain high performance, but as group size increases, cooperation collapses unless formal monitoring (akin to punishment) is introduced. The model implies that enabling monitoring or sanctions in large groups can restore or maintain efficiency, especially in stable environments. However, the findings are not directly transferable to PGG lab experiments, as the context is organizational and the outcomes are modeled rather than measured. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and monitoring on efficiency, rather than for quantitative prediction.

- source: Manapat, M. L., Rand, D. G., Pawlowitsch, C., & Nowak, M. A. (2012). Stochastic evolutionary dynamics resolve the Traveler's Dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in the Traveler's Dilemma, evolutionary dynamics favor higher (more cooperative) claims when the reward/penalty R is small relative to the maximum claim M, and lower (less cooperative) claims when R is large. The model reproduces the empirical finding that average claims decrease as R increases. The analysis is focused on the distribution of claims, not on group efficiency or total payoff, and the Traveler's Dilemma is structurally different from public goods games: it is a two-player, competitive undercutting game with a specific bonus/penalty mechanism, not a multi-player contribution game. The findings are relevant for understanding how incentive structures (like R) shape cooperative behavior, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or punishment effects in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It shows that in a two-player social dilemma with a reward/penalty for undercutting (Traveler's Dilemma), increasing the size of the penalty/reward (R) leads to less cooperative behavior (lower claims). However, the game structure is not a public goods game, and the outcomes are not group efficiency or total payoff, but rather the average claim. There is no direct manipulation or analysis of punishment in a PGG or direct efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform general intuitions about how incentive structures affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Wardil, L., & da Silva, J. K. L. (2009). Adoption of simultaneous different strategies against different opponents enhances cooperation. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to adopt different strategies against different opponents, combined with a 'replace the worst confrontation' update rule (model A), leads to the dominance or persistence of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on both square lattices and rings. This mechanism acts as a form of punishment by neutralizing exploiters, but does not impose a cost on the punisher. The fraction of cooperation remains high for a wide range of defection temptations and is robust to some misjudgment. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its relevance is limited to the insight that certain update rules that allow targeted responses to defectors can sustain high cooperation rates, even without explicit costly punishment. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG), and the outcome is cooperation rate (not efficiency or payoff), its transfer value to the prediction task is limited and indirect. The paper does not report or analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so it cannot be used to calibrate efficiency predictions for PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Rankin, D. J., & Eggimann, F. (2009). The evolution of judgement bias in indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that judgement bias—weighting bad acts more heavily than good acts in reputation updating—can evolve in indirect reciprocity models, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is low. Under 'standing' reputation rules, negative judgement bias is favored over a wider range of parameters than under 'scoring.' Allowing judgement bias to evolve increases the level of cooperation in the population. The model does not include explicit costly punishment, but negative judgement bias acts as a form of social sanctioning by reducing the likelihood of future help to defectors. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that mechanisms that increase the reputational cost of defection (such as negative judgement bias) can promote cooperation, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation-based indirect reciprocity is the main mechanism, rather than explicit costly punishment. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Fu, F., Chen, X. J., Liu, L. G., & Wang, L. (2007). Promotion of cooperation induced by the interplay between structure and game dynamics. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to adapt their social ties by breaking links with the most frequent defectors (after a multi-round PDG) and rewiring to others can promote the emergence and dominance of cooperation in the population. The interplay between network structure adaptation and strategy evolution is key: there is a critical rate of network adaptation above which cooperators can dominate. The model explores how parameters such as temptation to defect, number of rounds, average connectivity, and selection intensity affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and network properties, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its model is adjacent: it studies a networked PDG with social tie breaking as a form of sanction, but does not include explicit costly punishment or measure efficiency/welfare. The findings suggest that adaptive social structure (link breaking as a response to defection) can promote cooperation, but the results are not directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit punishment and efficiency outcomes. Use this paper as weak, indirect support for the idea that social sanctions (even non-costly ones) can promote cooperation, but do not use it for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., & Tsujimoto, M. (2010). Evolution of cooperation in rotating indivisible goods game. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a rotating indivisible goods game (a model of ROSCAs), cooperation collapses unless both a peer selection rule (based on reputation) and a forfeiture rule (excluding non-contributors from receiving goods) are present. Peer selection alone or forfeiture alone is insufficient. The model tracks the evolution of cooperation probabilities before and after receiving goods, showing that only the combination of both rules leads to sustained high cooperation. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it shows that in a public-goods-adjacent environment with indivisible goods, the presence of both peer selection (reputation-based group formation) and a forfeiture rule (exclusion of non-contributors) is necessary to sustain high cooperation rates. For prediction, this suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (here, exclusion and peer selection) may only be effective in combination, and that the structure of the game (indivisible goods, rotation, exclusion) is critical. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: BIEL, A., & GARLING, T. (1995). THE ROLE OF UNCERTAINTY IN RESOURCE DILEMMAS. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The main finding is that a reputation mechanism that rewards/punishes based on the number of consecutive rounds a player has cooperated or defected can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. Higher reputation factors and higher upper bounds on consistent strategy times both further promote cooperation. The mechanism is more effective and leads to faster stabilization of cooperation than traditional reputation mechanisms. However, the paper does not analyze or report on efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based punishment/reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG environments, and that the strength and structure of the reputation mechanism matter. However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. Its relevance is further limited by the use of a spatial PDG rather than a PGG or direct payoff-based analysis. It may be useful for mechanism design intuition, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Calderón, J. P., & Zarama, R. (2006). How learning affects the evolution of strong reciprocity. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of the ultimatum game, agents with greater phenotypic plasticity (ability to adapt their offer and acceptance strategies during their lifetime) evolve to make higher offers and demand higher minimum acceptable offers, compared to rigid agents. This is interpreted as the emergence of strong reciprocity and altruistic punishment. The diversity of behaviors also increases with plasticity. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all outcomes are at the level of individual behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the evolution of punishment and cooperation in a bargaining game (ultimatum game), not a public goods game. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it suggests that mechanisms increasing behavioral diversity and adaptability can promote more cooperative and punishing behaviors. The findings are only adjacent to the prediction task, as the game structure, outcome measures, and focus differ from standard PGGs.

- source: Wardil, L., & da Silva, J. K. L. (2010). Distinguishing the opponents promotes cooperation in well-mixed populations. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to distinguish between opponents and update their strategies in the interaction that gave the worst payoff leads to the evolutionary stability of cooperation in well-mixed populations. Under synchronous update, cooperation eventually dominates, while under asynchronous update, the fraction of cooperation remains at the lower bound set by the initial mutual cooperation. The mechanism of 'implicit punishment'—where exploitative (C,D) interactions are replaced by mutual defection (D,D)—prevents defectors from exploiting cooperators indefinitely, but does not involve explicit costly punishment. The model does not include explicit payoff-based punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of explicit punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it studies a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with a novel update rule and no explicit costly punishment or reward. The 'implicit punishment' mechanism is a structural feature of the strategy update process, not a player choice to pay a cost to reduce another's payoff. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be relevant for understanding how mechanisms that neutralize exploitation without explicit costs can stabilize cooperation, but do not provide quantitative evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in standard PGG-like environments.

- source: Szilagyi, M. N., & Somogyi, I. (2010). Agent-Based Simulation of an N-Person Game with Parabolic Payoff Functions. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the final proportion of cooperators depends strongly on the shape and relative position of the parabolic payoff functions, the agent update rule, and the definition of neighborhood (local vs. global). For some parameterizations, stable intermediate levels of cooperation emerge; in others, the system converges to all-cooperation or all-defection. The results are often unpredictable except in special cases (e.g., greedy or conformist agents with global neighborhoods). The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms as in standard public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The game studied is adjacent to PGGs in that it is an N-person game with binary choices and payoff externalities, but it lacks explicit punishment/reward actions and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be useful for understanding how nonlinear payoff structures and local interactions affect cooperation rates, but they do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment interventions.

- source: Page, K. M., Nowak, M. A., & Sigmund, K. (2000). The spatial ultimatum game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that spatial structure in the ultimatum game leads to the evolution of fairer offers and higher acceptance thresholds compared to non-spatial (random encounter) settings. In well-mixed populations, average offers and acceptance levels are low, approaching zero as mutation error decreases. In spatially structured populations (1D and 2D lattices), average offers and acceptance levels are much higher, approaching a fair split in some cases. The paper interprets rejection of low offers as a punishment mechanism that, when combined with spatial structure, supports the evolution of fairness. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that spatial structure and local competition can support the evolution of fairer, more cooperative behavior in bargaining games where punishment is endogenous (via rejection). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of spatial structure and local interactions for the evolution of fairness and the effectiveness of endogenous punishment mechanisms.

- source: Hock, K., & Fefferman, N. H. (2011). Violating Social Norms when Choosing Friends: How Rule-Breakers Affect Social Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when individuals in a network break social conventions for choosing partners, the effects depend on the convention in use. In 'intermediary-based' (betweenness) networks, rule-breakers primarily affect their own social position, and only at higher frequencies do they impact group organization. In 'popularity-based' (in-degree) networks, rule-breakers do not affect their own or others' individual positions, but as their frequency increases, they significantly alter the overall network structure (decentralization). The paper discusses the potential for rule-breaking to transform social systems, but does not measure or report efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, efficiency, or payoff, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. Its findings on how rule-breaking alters network structure may be contextually relevant for understanding the broader consequences of norm violation, but do not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The paper may be useful for mechanism discussion or for understanding the importance of social structure, but not for direct prediction in the target task.

- source: Sinatra, R., Iranzo, J., Gómez-Gardeñes, J., Floría, L. M., Latora, V., & Moreno, Y. (2009). The Ultimatum Game in complex networks. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The main findings are that network topology and the type of evolutionary update rule strongly influence the distribution of offers and acceptance thresholds in the Ultimatum Game. Under natural selection, strategies converge toward fair offers (around 50%) in both homogeneous and heterogeneous networks, but scale-free networks allow for a broader range of surviving strategies due to the influence of hubs. Under the social penalty rule, which penalizes the lowest-payoff player and its neighbors, scale-free networks promote polarization: hubs can afford to be highly generous, while low-degree nodes may be more selfish. When players can independently choose offers and thresholds, the population tends to self-organize into pragmatic (role-ignoring) strategies, especially under social penalty. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the implications for efficiency are not directly addressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies the Ultimatum Game and focuses on the evolution of behavioral strategies rather than payoff or efficiency outcomes. The 'social penalty' mechanism is a form of network-level selection rather than direct costly punishment. While the findings may be informative about how network structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation and fairness-related behaviors, they do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGGs. Therefore, this paper should be considered only as weak, adjacent evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Wang, R. W., He, J. Z., Wang, Y. Q., Shi, L., & Li, Y. T. (2010). Asymmetric interaction will facilitate the evolution of cooperation. *Science China-life Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of the asymmetric Hawk-Dove game, showing that the frequency of cooperation increases with greater asymmetry between players (i.e., when one player is much stronger or more dominant than the other). The model also shows that a lower cost-to-benefit ratio of cooperation increases cooperation frequency. The discussion links these findings to real-world systems where dominant individuals can punish non-cooperators, suggesting that credible punishment and asymmetry can stabilize cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the probability of cooperation and conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that asymmetry and credible punishment can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas, but it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in public goods games with punishment is limited, as it analyzes a two-player Hawk-Dove game rather than a multi-player public goods game, and focuses on cooperation frequency rather than efficiency. The discussion of punishment is conceptual, not formalized as a game design dimension. Thus, while the paper suggests that punishment and asymmetry can facilitate cooperation, it does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Gardner, A., West, S. A., & Barton, N. H. (2007). The relation between multilocus population genetics and social evolution theory. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that multilocus population genetics provides a general framework for modeling the evolution of social behaviors, including cooperation and punishment. In a simple two-locus model (one for cooperation, one for punishment), it finds that cooperation and punishment cannot invade unless cooperation is already favored by relatedness (i.e., punishment does not expand the parameter space for cooperation). Moreover, increased relatedness directly selects against punishment, as punishment is mutually costly. The only benefit to punishment comes from its association with cooperation, but this is insufficient to overcome the direct costs. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on evolutionary stability and gene frequency change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the evolutionary logic of cooperation and punishment, and suggest that costly punishment may not increase cooperation or group benefit except under restrictive conditions. However, because it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it should not be used as a primary source for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context, especially regarding the evolutionary logic of punishment and cooperation.

- source: Wittman, D. (2005). The internal organization of the family: Economic analysis and psychological advice. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that advice from clinical psychology on setting boundaries and using natural or logical consequences (rather than punishment) in families is consistent with economic reasoning about property rights and efficiency. It argues that punishment systems in families create externalities and deadweight losses, and that structuring choices so that parents are indifferent to children's decisions (the 'indifference principle') minimizes manipulation and strategic behavior. The paper suggests that minimizing emotional involvement and not using punishment leads to more functional, efficient family relationships, but these claims are theoretical and not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its arguments are theoretical and analogical, suggesting that punishment may introduce inefficiencies (externalities, deadweight loss) in small-group settings, and that structuring choices to avoid punishment may be more efficient. However, these claims are not tested in controlled experiments or public goods game variants, so the paper offers only indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment can reduce efficiency by increasing transaction costs and strategic manipulation.

- source: Falk, A., & Fischbacher, U. (2006). A theory of reciprocity. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper develops a psychological game-theoretic model of reciprocity, where individuals' utility depends on both material payoffs and the perceived kindness or unkindness of others' actions, which in turn depends on both outcomes and intentions. The model explains why people sometimes punish defectors or reward cooperators, even at a cost to themselves, and why the same material outcome can elicit different responses depending on the perceived intention behind it. The theory is shown to be consistent with a range of experimental findings, such as higher rejection rates for unfair offers in the ultimatum game, positive wage-effort relationships in gift-exchange, and conditional cooperation in public goods games. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical framework for understanding when and why individuals will punish or reward others in social dilemmas, including public goods games. It highlights the importance of both outcomes and intentions in driving reciprocal behavior. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of how punishment affects efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with punishment enabled), this paper offers conceptual guidance on the psychological mechanisms underlying punishment, but does not supply direct evidence or parameter values for efficiency outcomes. Its value is mainly in informing the types of behavioral responses to expect under different game structures, especially regarding the role of intentions and available alternatives.
