  findings: The paper finds that gossip, understood as the spread of reputation (meta-evaluations with hidden sources), enables more efficient social control by reducing the costs and risks (especially retaliation) associated with punishing or excluding cheaters. Ethnographic evidence shows that gossip is structured to avoid direct responsibility and retaliation, while agent-based simulations demonstrate that reputation-based information exchange can maintain higher product quality and more robust partner selection even with many cheaters. However, these findings are in the context of market-like artificial societies and do not directly address efficiency or payoff in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence that decentralized, reputation-based information exchange (gossip) can improve the effectiveness and reduce the costs of social control mechanisms, such as punishment or exclusion, in environments where cheating is possible. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. The agent-based simulation results suggest that reputation mechanisms can sustain higher system performance (e.g., product quality) under high cheating rates, but these are not directly translatable to PGG efficiency outcomes. Thus, the paper may inform mechanistic reasoning about the role of information and retaliation risk in punishment systems, but does not provide parameter-level evidence for the prediction task.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper provides a unified framework for understanding how different mechanisms (direct reciprocity, indirect reciprocity, kin selection, group selection, network reciprocity) transform the payoff structure of the Prisoner's Dilemma to allow for the evolution of cooperation. For each mechanism, it derives the threshold conditions under which cooperation can be stable or invade. The analysis is mathematical and does not include empirical or simulation results. Punishment is discussed only as an extension, not as a primary focus, and is said to be subsumed under direct or indirect reciprocity. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on these outcomes.

It is a theoretical synthesis of mechanisms for cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma, with only a brief, conceptual discussion of punishment. The paper does not analyze or model the effect of punishment on group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes, and does not provide parameterized results for game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in clarifying that punishment is not a separate mechanism but an extension of reciprocity, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are not directly addressed.

- source: West, S. A., Griffin, A. S., & Gardner, A. (2007). Social semantics: altruism, cooperation, mutualism, strong reciprocity and group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that much confusion in the literature on cooperation, altruism, and punishment arises from inconsistent or ambiguous use of key terms. It clarifies that in many models (including public goods games), behaviors labeled as 'altruistic' or 'strong reciprocity' may actually be mutually beneficial, providing direct fitness benefits to the actor as well as to the group. Punishment, often called 'altruistic punishment,' can also be selfish or spiteful, depending on the context. The paper argues that clear definitions and careful distinction between proximate and ultimate explanations are essential for progress in the field. It also notes that the group selection and kin selection frameworks are mathematically equivalent in most cases relevant to cooperation and public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it offers important theoretical context: it cautions that the effect of punishment on cooperation and efficiency depends on the underlying fitness consequences and the structure of the game. The paper suggests that punishment can increase cooperation and group benefit if it provides a direct or indirect fitness advantage, but the terminology used in the literature may obscure whether the effect is truly altruistic or mutually beneficial. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the need for precise definitions of efficiency and careful mapping of model assumptions to experimental designs. It also highlights that the effect of punishment may depend on whether it is costly, whether it increases group survival or productivity, and how benefits are distributed.

- source: Alonso, J., Fernández, A., & Fort, H. (2006). Prisoner's Dilemma cellular automata revisited:: evolution of cooperation under environmental pressure. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that requiring agents to achieve a minimum payoff threshold (environmental pressure) in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma games can lead to the emergence of cooperation, even when the payoff for mutual defection is not very harsh. The fraction of cooperators increases with the threshold up to a point, and the addition of a Pavlovian update rule (win-stay, lose-shift) further increases cooperation and leads to power-law cluster size distributions in some parameter regions. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms beyond the standard PD payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is of limited direct use for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with environmental thresholds and focuses on the fraction of cooperators as the main outcome. There is no analysis of group efficiency or total payoff, and no manipulation of punishment or sanctioning technology. The findings may be of some indirect relevance for understanding how environmental constraints can promote cooperation, but do not provide evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: MOLM, L. D. (1994). DEPENDENCE AND RISK - TRANSFORMING AND STRUCTURE OF SOCIAL-EXCHANGE. *Social Psychology Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the risk inherent in social exchange relations (mutual dependence) can be reduced by structuring transactions as negotiated (joint decision, binding agreements) or as serially dependent (allowing for contingent action and trust development). Negotiated transactions most directly transform dependence into interdependence, reducing risk and making outcomes more predictable. Serially dependent transactions (such as repeated reciprocal exchanges) also reduce risk, but less so, and rely more on emergent trust and norms. Generalized exchange (indirect reciprocity) is riskier and less amenable to these risk-reducing mechanisms. The paper suggests that these structural features influence the likelihood of stable cooperation, trust, and solidarity, but does not provide empirical evidence or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how structural features of social exchange (e.g., repeated interaction, negotiation, direct vs. generalized exchange) may moderate the risk of defection and the emergence of cooperation. While it does not address public goods games or punishment directly, it suggests that mechanisms which increase interdependence (such as repeated play or enforceable agreements) should improve group outcomes and stability. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game designs with more interdependence, repeated interaction, or enforceable agreements are likely to see higher efficiency or more stable cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence. The relevance to punishment is indirect: punishment is mentioned as a possible influence tactic in negotiation, but not as a central mechanism.

  findings: The paper finds that variation among individuals in traits relevant to cooperation (such as cooperativeness, partner quality, and social sensitivity) fundamentally alters the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. Variation can make it worthwhile for individuals to explore, negotiate, and respond contingently to others, which can stabilize or increase cooperation. Mechanisms such as partner choice, reputation, and negotiation are all promoted by variation. The paper also discusses how sanctions (including punishment, partner switching, and reduced investment) can penalize uncooperative individuals, but the focus is on the role of variation in enabling these mechanisms rather than on the direct effects of punishment on efficiency or payoff.

- source: Palomar, E., Alcaide, A., Ribagorda, A., & Zhang, Y. (2012). The Peer's Dilemma: A general framework to examine cooperation in pure peer-to-peer systems. *Computer Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated RSP game modeling P2P interactions, the introduction of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary withdrawal) allows for stable coexistence of cooperators, non-cooperators, and loners, preventing the collapse of cooperation even in the absence of incentives or punishment. The system exhibits cyclic dominance of strategies, and the stability of cooperation is sensitive to factors such as population size, network overlay dynamism, and initial population composition. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. Simulations show that large, weakly dynamic populations are more likely to sustain stable cycles, while small or highly dynamic overlays are prone to domination by a single strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in showing that, in a repeated game with voluntary participation (the 'loner' option), stable cooperation can emerge without explicit punishment or reward. The model is adjacent to PGGs but differs in key respects: it is a zero-sum RSP game, not a public goods game, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding the role of voluntary participation and cyclic dominance in sustaining cooperation.

  findings: The paper theorizes that the universal presence of supernatural sanction systems (rewarding and punishing afterlives) in archaic civilizations functioned to curb individual selfishness and support group-level cooperation, overcoming the problem of genetic competition among cooperators. It suggests that such beliefs and associated costly rituals (e.g., wasteful royal funerals) reinforced the credibility of these sanction systems, thereby enhancing group cohesion and effectiveness. The argument is that these group-level cultural adaptations, including moral norms and invisible sanction systems, were selected because they increased the survival and success of social groups, even if they imposed costs on individuals. However, the paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff effects.

  findings: The paper argues that human cooperation at large scales is best explained by cultural group selection, where rapid cultural adaptation leads to persistent differences between groups. These differences, when linked to group-beneficial norms (such as those enforced by punishment and reward systems), can spread through group competition, imitation of successful groups, and selective migration. The authors posit that moral systems enforced by sanctions and rewards increase group welfare and the prevalence of prosocial motives. However, the paper is theoretical and does not present empirical data or experimental results on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or similar environments.

However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Its value for prediction is mainly in supporting the plausibility of positive effects of sanctions on group-level outcomes, but it does not inform how specific game design dimensions or control efficiency moderate these effects.

  findings: The paper argues that economic experiments can be classified by their underlying concept of institutions: (1) as exogenous structures (rules), (2) as embedded in agents (norms, beliefs), or (3) as evolving endogenously through agent interaction and feedback. It highlights that experiments with endogenous institutional choice (e.g., voting on punishment or reward mechanisms) are particularly relevant for understanding institutional change in social-ecological systems. The paper references empirical findings (e.g., Walker et al. 2000) that voting on rules can increase efficiency, but does not provide new empirical results. The main contribution is a conceptual framework for interpreting and designing experiments on institutional change, not direct evidence on punishment or efficiency.

However, it is useful for understanding the types of experimental designs and institutional features (such as endogenous vs. exogenous rule change, presence of sanctioning or reward mechanisms, and the role of voting or communication) that may moderate such effects. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that allowing endogenous choice of punishment institutions (e.g., via voting) may increase efficiency, as supported by referenced studies, but does not quantify these effects. The typology can help identify which experimental features are likely to be important moderators in empirical studies.

  findings: The paper argues that dishonesty is not solely determined by external incentives (such as probability and magnitude of punishment), but also by internal psychological reward mechanisms. It reviews evidence that increasing the probability or severity of punishment does not always reduce dishonest behavior, especially when internalized norms or self-awareness are strong. The authors propose that interventions increasing self-awareness or internalization of honesty norms can be more effective than increasing punishment. The paper references experimental studies on cheating in test settings, showing that the opportunity to cheat increases dishonesty, but only up to a point, and that self-awareness interventions (e.g., recalling the Ten Commandments, signing an honor code) can reduce cheating to control levels. The paper does not report on group efficiency or public goods game outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for why punishment may not always increase efficiency or reduce norm violations in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by internal psychological factors, such as internalized norms and self-awareness. However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment effectiveness may depend on context and psychological factors not captured by game design dimensions alone. - source: Hauser, M., McAuliffe, K., & Blake, P. R. (2009). Evolving the ingredients for reciprocity and spite. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.

  findings: The paper finds that reciprocity and spite are rare or absent in non-human animals due to both ultimate (evolutionary) and proximate (mechanistic) constraints. In contrast, humans evolved the necessary cognitive integration and social structures (such as large groups of unrelated individuals) that favor the emergence of reciprocity and punishment. Punishment is argued to be essential for maintaining cooperation and enforcing social norms in humans, and psychological spite is seen as a byproduct of selection for punishment. The paper reviews animal experiments (e.g., with jays, tamarins, chimpanzees) and human studies (including children), showing that only humans reliably exhibit the full suite of behaviors required for sustained reciprocity and norm-enforcing punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for why punishment and reciprocity are effective in humans but not in most non-human animals. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding the evolutionary and cognitive prerequisites for punishment and cooperation, but does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in specific game designs. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of group size, repeated interactions, norm enforcement, and cognitive integration for the emergence of punishment and cooperation.

These mechanisms do not change the fundamental selective pressures on altruism; rather, they introduce constraints or errors that allow maladaptive behaviors to persist. The paper critiques the literature for sometimes conflating mutualistic cooperation (which can be adaptive) with genuine altruism (which is maladaptive by definition), and for overstating the explanatory power of cultural group selection. The authors argue that the prevalence of genuine altruism in human societies is likely limited and always reflects some form of maladaptation, whether cognitive or cultural.

For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the mere presence of punishment mechanisms (especially when costly) does not ensure improved efficiency, and that claims about the benefits of punishment should be scrutinized for underlying assumptions about adaptation and learning. - source: Bravo, G., & Tamburino, L. (2008). The evolution of trust in non-simultaneous exchange situations. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The simulations show that in the Trust Game, average efficiency (measured as average payoff per interaction) is low when agents lack information about others' past behavior or when reputation effects are weak. Introducing strong reputation effects or increasing the spread of information about past behavior leads to a sharp increase in efficiency, with the system moving from a low-efficiency equilibrium (average payoff near 1) to a high-efficiency equilibrium (average payoff above 2, up to ~2.6 out of a maximum of 3). Direct reciprocity (repeated interaction with the same partner) can also increase efficiency, especially when information is incomplete. The transition between low and high efficiency is abrupt, with thin thresholds in the relevant parameters. The model does not include explicit costly punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, 'punishment' is implemented as the refusal to trust low-reputation agents, which is individually rational and costless in this setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in Trust Game-like environments, the availability and reliability of information about others' past behavior (i.e., reputation mechanisms) are critical moderators of the effect of indirect reciprocity on efficiency. When reputation effects are strong and information is widely available, efficiency increases sharply. Direct reciprocity (repeated partners) can also boost efficiency, especially when information is incomplete. However, the model does not include explicit costly punishment or sanctioning mechanisms as in public goods games with punishment. Therefore, while the findings are highly informative about the role of information, reputation, and reciprocity in driving efficiency, they are only weakly informative about the effects of costly punishment per se. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency, this paper is most useful as a source of indirect evidence about the importance of information and reputation, rather than direct evidence about punishment.

The main contribution is a theoretical exploration of how cooperation rates fluctuate and may fail to converge in certain stochastic environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its theoretical results on cooperation dynamics in a two-person positive spillover game may be of limited, adjacent interest for understanding path dependence or stochasticity in cooperation, but are not informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2007). Direct reciprocity on graphs. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation via direct reciprocity is affected by the structure of the population (graph degree k) and the update rule. For birth-death and pairwise comparison updating, structured populations make it harder for cooperation to be evolutionarily stable, but easier for cooperators to be advantageous. For death-birth and imitation updating, structured populations always facilitate cooperation. The critical benefit-to-cost ratio for cooperation to be favored depends on the probability of repeated interaction (w) and the number of neighbors (k). Errors in strategy execution, which increase with the number of neighbors, make cooperation harder to sustain unless the network is sparse (small k). The paper does not consider punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it provides theoretical results on how network structure and repeated interaction affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoffs in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means it cannot inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. Its results may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in repeated dyadic games on networks, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Fort, H. (2003). Exploring the cooperative regimes in an agent-based model: indirect reciprocity vs. selfish incentives. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in a large population of memoryless, tagless agents playing repeated random pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games, the system self-organizes into stationary states with positive average cooperation and per capita income, even in the absence of direct reciprocity, punishment, or reward. The equilibrium level of cooperation and group payoff depend strongly on the specific payoff matrix used. Some payoff matrices that intuitively seem to favor cooperation actually result in low cooperation and low group payoff, due to agents' expectations and update rules. The model demonstrates robustness of cooperation to changes in the payoff matrix, but does not include any mechanism for punishment, sanctioning, or reward.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. It is useful as a baseline for understanding how efficiency and cooperation can emerge in the absence of punishment, but does not inform the prediction of treatment efficiency when punishment is enabled. The findings may be useful for modeling control conditions (no punishment) in related agent-based or repeated PD environments, but cannot be used to estimate the effect of punishment or its moderators.

- source: Hilbe, C. (2011). Local Replicator Dynamics: A Simple Link Between Deterministic and Stochastic Models of Evolutionary Game Theory. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that when individuals interact and update strategies in finite groups (local replicator dynamics), the evolutionary outcome can be captured by a replicator equation with a modified payoff matrix. This approach allows the use of deterministic replicator dynamics to predict outcomes in finite populations, including the emergence of cooperation, risk aversion, and even the dominance of strategies that are not optimal in the classical sense. The model shows that group size has a significant effect: smaller groups can lead to the spread of spiteful or dominated strategies, while larger groups approximate the classical infinite-population results. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or public goods games directly, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant for understanding how group size and local updating affect efficiency and the evolution of cooperation in general evolutionary games, but it does not include punishment mechanisms or analyze public goods games with or without punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects. However, its theoretical insights on group size and local updating may be contextually informative for understanding the broader dynamics of efficiency in group-structured games.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2006). The replicator equation on graphs. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that evolutionary game dynamics on regular graphs can be described by a replicator equation with a transformed payoff matrix, which incorporates local competition effects. For the Prisoner's Dilemma and Snowdrift games, spatial structure (graph topology) can promote or inhibit cooperation depending on the update rule and game parameters, thereby affecting the equilibrium group payoff and efficiency. The framework is general and applies to any n-strategy game, but does not address punishment, reward, or explicit public goods games. The results are most relevant for understanding how population structure alters the efficiency and stability of cooperative outcomes in evolutionary games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical foundation for predicting how spatial structure (regular graphs) and update rules affect efficiency and group payoff in evolutionary games. However, it does not address punishment, reward, or public goods games directly, nor does it provide empirical estimates. For the downstream prediction task, the main value is in understanding that population structure can be mathematically mapped to a transformation of the payoff matrix, which in turn alters equilibrium efficiency. The absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms means the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Wakano, J. Y. (2007). Evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games with common resource dynamics. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in spatially structured, anonymous, and obligate public goods games with resource regeneration and diffusion, cooperative strategies (farmers) can outcompete exploiters under certain parameter regimes, even when cooperation is costly (strong altruism). The mechanism is that cooperators can increase their population density and locally exhaust the benefits of cooperation, preventing exploiters from free-riding. However, the evolution of cooperation does not always improve the environmental resource level; sometimes, higher population density of cooperators leads to a degraded environment. The model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant as an adjacent theoretical model showing that spatial structure and resource dynamics can enable cooperation without punishment, but it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency in the sense required for the prediction task. Its findings may be useful for understanding alternative mechanisms for cooperation, but it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2006). Evolutionary games on cycles. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games on cycles, the update rule strongly affects whether cooperation can evolve. For the Prisoner's Dilemma, birth-death updating never favors cooperation, but death-birth and imitation updating can favor cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold (b/c > 2 for death-birth, b/c > 4 for imitation, for large N). In coordination games, death-birth and imitation updating can favor Pareto efficient outcomes over risk-dominant ones, unlike birth-death updating. The results are analytic and apply to two-strategy games on cycles, not to public goods games or punishment. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how spatial structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation in two-strategy games (such as the Prisoner's Dilemma), and discusses payoff-based concepts like risk dominance and Pareto efficiency in coordination games. However, it does not analyze punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, nor does it report group efficiency or welfare outcomes. Its findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how local interactions and update mechanisms can support cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects in public goods games.

- source: Helbing, D., & Yu, W. J. (2009). The outbreak of cooperation among success-driven individuals under noisy conditions. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The main finding is that success-driven migration, when combined with imitation, enables the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when noise (random strategy mutation and random relocation) is present. This mechanism can lead to a sudden outbreak of predominant cooperation, even from initial conditions dominated by defectors. The results are robust across a range of payoff parameters and noise types. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant as adjacent theory, showing that mechanisms other than punishment (specifically, migration and imitation) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not inform the efficiency impact of punishment, nor does it provide empirical or simulated data on payoff-based outcomes. It may be useful for contextual understanding of alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2012). Accuracy in strategy imitations promotes the evolution of fairness in the spatial ultimatum game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial ultimatum game, the accuracy of strategy imitation (i.e., the number of discrete choices for offers and acceptance thresholds) strongly affects the evolution of fairness. For small N, two rational strategies coexist; for larger N, a single, nearly fair strategy dominates. The closer N is to infinity, the closer the population gets to the fair split. The results are robust to some changes in network structure and mutation. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings about the evolution of fairness in spatial ultimatum games with varying granularity may be contextually informative for understanding how choice structure affects fairness, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment or efficiency.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2004). Strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that, contrary to previous consensus, strong altruism can evolve in randomly formed groups if groups persist for multiple generations. This is due to increased positive assortment (measured by the regression coefficient between actors and recipients) that arises after one or more generations within groups, even without kin selection, homogeneous groups, or population growth. The models show that smaller group size, higher benefit-to-cost ratio, and intermediate numbers of generations within groups favor the evolution of altruism. Stochasticity in group formation and population regulation can further affect the likelihood of altruism evolving. However, the models do not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, and thus do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its relevance is limited to providing theoretical context about the evolution of cooperation/altruism in group-structured populations, especially regarding the effects of group persistence, assortment, and stochasticity. It does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency.
